Hecke L L -series for Sinha modules
Erik Davis, Matthew Papanikolas

Abstract
. We investigate Goss L L -functions associated to Anderson t t -modules defined by Sinha having complex multiplication by Carlitz cyclotomic fields. We show that these t t -modules are defined over the cyclotomic field and that their L L -functions are products of Hecke L L -series for Anderson’s Hecke character defined via Coleman functions. Applying identities of Fang and Taelman, we prove that special values of these L L -functions are expressible in terms of products of values of Thakur’s geometric Γ \Gamma -function.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 11M38; Secondary 11G09, 11G15, 11R60

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Classical Hecke
L
-series

We investigate special values of Hecke
L
-series attached to Carlitz cyclotomic fields. The motivating problem comes from
L
-functions of Jacobi sum Hecke characters
[
4
,
36
,
44
]
, where one defines a Hecke character
J_{K,\mathbf{a}}
on an abelian number field
K/\mathbb{Q}
depending on a parameter
\mathbf{a}
in the free abelian group on
\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}
.

Lichtenbaum
[
36
]
*Thm. 3.1 proved that critical values
L(J_{K,\mathbf{a}},n)
are
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
-multiples of prescribed powers of
\pi
and powers of special
\Gamma
-values
\Gamma(a/N)
, where
a/N\in\mathbb{Q}
depend on
\mathbf{a}
. He conjectured that these multiples were in fact in
\mathbb{Q}
up to a square root of the discriminant of
K
or its maximal real subfield. A few years later Anderson
[
4
]
*Thm. 2 proved Lichtenbaum’s
\Gamma
-hypothesis using his theory of ulterior motives. For detailed accounts of
L
-functions of Jacobi sum Hecke characters, the reader is directed to
[
4
,
36
,
44
]
.

For example, if
\mathbf{a}=2[1/3]-[2/3]
and
K=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-3})
, then the character
J_{K,\mathbf{a}}
is related to the elliptic curve
y^{2}+y=x^{3}
with complex multiplication by
O_{K}
. One finds

L(J_{K,\mathbf{a}},1)=\frac{\Gamma(1/3)^{2}}{9\Gamma(2/3)}.

1.2.
Anderson Hecke characters

Turning to the setting of function fields, we let
A=\mathbb{F}_{q}[\theta]
be the polynomial ring in a variable
\theta
over the finite field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, and let
A_{+}
denote its monic elements. We take
k=\mathbb{F}_{q}(\theta)
for the fraction field of
A
,
k_{\infty}={\mathbb{F}_{q}((1/\theta))}
for the completion of
k
at its infinite place, and
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
for the completion of an algebraic closure of
k_{\infty}
. We let
\overline{k}
denote the algebraic closure of
k
in
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
. For
f\in A_{+}
with
\deg f\geqslant 1
, we let
K=k(\zeta)
denote the
f
-th Carlitz cyclotomic field (see §
3.1
) and
B\subseteq K
the integral closure of
A
in
K
. We let
b\mapsto\rho_{b}
be the isomorphism
(A/fA)^{\times}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Gal(K/k)
such that
\rho_{b}(\zeta)=C_{b}(\zeta)
, where
C
is the Carlitz module.

1.2.1.
Coleman functions.

Inspired by work of Coleman
[
18
]
, Anderson
[
5
]
introduced soliton functions, which are functions on the product
X\times X
, where
X/\mathbb{F}_{q}
is a smooth projective curve for the function field
K
. Later, researchers replaced solitons in applications with more direct specializations called Coleman functions
g_{\mathbf{a}}\in\overline{k}(\mathbf{X})
(
\mathbf{X}=\overline{k}\times_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}X
) in
[
1
,
46
,
47
,
48
]
. These functions possess explicit divisors

\divf(g_{\mathbf{a}})=W_{\mathbf{a}}^{(1)}-W_{\mathbf{a}}+\Xi_{\mathbf{a}}-I_{\mathbf{a}}

(see §
3.3
), which we use to define both Anderson’s Hecke characters and Sinha’s
t
-modules. Coleman functions play a similar role to shtuka functions for Drinfeld-Hayes modules as in
[
54
]
, and they underpin many results (e.g., see
[
12
,
16
,
17
,
55
,
57
]
).

1.2.2.
Hecke characters.

Using Jacobi sum Hecke characters as a model, Anderson
[
5
]
defined a class of Hecke characters, later expanded by Sinha
[
47
]
,

(1.2.3)

\chi_{\mathbf{a}}:\mathcal{I}_{K,\mathfrak{f}}\to K^{\times}

where
\mathbf{a}
is an element of the free abelian group
\mathcal{A}_{f}
on
f^{-1}A/A
,
\mathfrak{f}\subseteq B
is the radical of
fB
, and
\mathcal{I}_{K,\mathfrak{f}}
is the group of fractional ideals of
B
prime to
\mathfrak{f}
. For
\mathfrak{p}
a maximal ideal of
B
relatively prime to
f
, Anderson defines
\chi_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathfrak{p})
by

\chi_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathfrak{p})=\prod_{i=0}^{\ell-1}g_{\mathbf{a}}^{(i)}(\xi)\quad\in K^{\times},

where
\ell=[\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}:\mathbb{F}_{q}]
and
\xi\in\mathbf{X}(K)
is the generic point corresponding to the inclusion
K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
. See §
3.4
. Anderson proved that
\chi_{\mathbf{a}}
is an algebraic Hecke character for
K
of conductor dividing
\mathfrak{f}
and designated infinity type
\Theta_{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathbb{Z}[\Gal(K/k)]
(see Theorem
3.4.7
). It aligns with work of Goss
[
27
]
and Hayes
[
32
]
on Hecke characters in function fields.

1.2.4.
Goss
L
-series.

In Theorem
D
and Corollary
E
below we prove special
L
-value formulas for Goss
L
-series attached to
\chi_{\mathbf{a}}
,

L(\chi_{\mathbf{a}},s)=\prod_{\mathfrak{p}\nmid\mathfrak{f}}\biggl(1-\frac{\chi_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathfrak{p})}{\mathcal{N}(\mathfrak{p})^{s}}\biggr)^{-1},\quad s\in\mathbb{Z},

which is a product over primes of
B
not dividing
\mathfrak{f}
and which takes values in
k_{\infty}(\zeta)\subseteq\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
(see §
2.4
, §
5.1.9
). Previously, Sinha
[
47
]
had proved a function field version of Deligne’s conjecture on special
\Gamma
-values that showed that, when
\Gamma(\mathbf{a})\in\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5muK}
, the Galois action on
\Gamma(\mathbf{a})
transforms via
\chi_{\mathbf{a}}
, namely that
\rho_{\mathfrak{p}}(\Gamma(\mathbf{a}))=\chi_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathfrak{p})\Gamma(\mathbf{a})
for a Frobenius element
\rho_{\mathfrak{p}}\in\Gal(K^{\mathrm{sep}}/K)
. To our knowledge there has been little work on special values of
L(\chi_{\mathbf{a}},s)
to date.

1.2.5.
\Pi
-factorial and special
\Pi
-values.

In
[
53
]
, Thakur defined the geometric factorial function
\Pi:\mathbb{C}_{\infty}\setminus(-A_{+})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
via

(1.2.6)

\Pi(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\prod_{a\in A_{+}}\biggl(1+\frac{x}{a}\biggr)^{-1}.

It is related to the geometric
\Gamma
-function by
\Pi(x)=x\Gamma(x)
, and as such it is an analogue of the classical factorial. Thakur
[
53
]
proved several classes of functional equations for
\Gamma(x)
, which established it as an analogue of the classical
\Gamma
-function.

If
x\in k\setminus(-A_{+})
, then we refer to
\Pi(x)
as a
special
\Pi
-value
. We note that special
\Pi
-values fall in
k_{\infty}
, which will play an important role in calculating Taelman regulators (see Proposition
5.3.9
). Sinha
[
48
]
demonstrated that certain special
\Pi
-values occur as periods of
t
-modules
E_{\mathbf{a}}
(see below, and see Theorem
C
). Algebraic relations over
\overline{k}
on special
\Pi
-values have been studied extensively
[
1
,
12
,
16
,
48
,
57
]
, by applying transcendence techniques in
[
1
,
58
]
, and in the present paper we show they arise naturally in values of
L
-functions (see Theorem
D
and Corollary
E
).

1.3.
Sinha modules

Just as
L
-functions of Jacobi sum Hecke characters can be interpreted as factors in
L
-functions of abelian varieties, or more generally of motives (see
[
4
,
36
,
44
]
), we begin our study of
L(\chi_{\mathbf{a}},s)
by analyzing more closely a family of Anderson
t
-modules with complex multiplication that were originally defined by Sinha
[
46
,
48
]
. For an effective parameter
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
(all coefficients are non-negative) and
\deg\mathbf{a}>0
, Sinha used Anderson’s theory of
t
-motives
[
3
]
to define an Anderson
t
-module

(1.3.1)

E_{\mathbf{a}}:\mathbf{A}\to\Mat_{d}(\overline{k}[\tau]),

where
\mathbf{A}=\mathbb{F}_{q}[t]
and
\tau
is the
q
-th power Frobenius endomorphism. See §
2.2
for more information on
t
-modules.

The
t
-module
E_{\mathbf{a}}
possesses complex multiplication by
\mathbf{B}\cong B
with CM-type
\Xi_{\mathbf{a}}
, as defined by Brownawell, Chang, Wei, and the second author in
[
12
,
11
]
(see Remark
4.1.17
). It is coabelian (
\mathbf{A}
-finite), has rank
r=[K:k]=\#(A/fA)^{\times}
, and has dimension
d=\deg(\mathbf{a})\cdot r/(q-1)
(see Lemma
4.1.2
). Sinha’s constructions yield a
t
-module defined over
\overline{k}
, but he did not produce a field of definition. In order to analyze Goss
L
-functions for
E_{\mathbf{a}}
our first main result is to clarify this situation (stated later as Theorem
4.3.29
).

Theorem A
.

Let
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
be effective with
\deg\mathbf{a}>0
. Then
E_{\mathbf{a}}
has a model defined over
K
.

1.3.2.
The
t
-comotive for
E_{\mathbf{a}}
.

The arguments to prove this theorem focus first on the construction of the
t
-comotive (“dual
t
-motive,” see Remark
2.2.1
)

H_{\mathbf{a}}(\overline{k})=H^{0}\bigl(\mathbf{U},\mathcal{O}_{\mathbf{X}}(-W_{\mathbf{a}}^{(1)})\bigr),\quad\mathbf{U}=\Spec\overline{k}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}\mathbf{B},

from which we define
E_{\mathbf{a}}
. See §
4.1
for precise definitions. We recall that the operation of
\sigma=\tau^{-1}
on
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\overline{k})
is provided through multiplication by the Coleman function
g_{\mathbf{a}}
, which then plays the role of shtuka functions for Drinfeld-Hayes modules as in Thakur
[
54
]
. These
t
-comotives were originally defined by Anderson, Brownawell, and the second author
[
1
]
, and they are parallel to the
t
-motives defined by Sinha
[
48
]
.

As constructed,
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\overline{k})
is a left
\overline{k}[t,\sigma]
-module (see §
2.2
), and it is an ideal of the coordinate ring
\overline{k}[\mathbf{U}]
of the affine part
\mathbf{U}
of
\mathbf{X}
. A first step toward Theorem
A
is showing
g_{\mathbf{a}}
is defined over
B[f^{-1}]
in Proposition
3.3.21
. But then a key difficulty in proving Theorem
A
is determining that
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\overline{k})
has a
\overline{k}[\sigma]
-basis consisting of functions defined over
K
, which we prove in Proposition
4.3.2
. The methods revolve around dissecting the arguments in
[
1
]
*§6.4
[
11
]
*Thm. 4.2.2
[
48
]
*Thm. 3.2.5 to devise an algorithm for producing
\overline{k}[\sigma]
-bases of
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\overline{k})
explicitly in §
4.3
, leading to explicit computation of
E_{\mathbf{a}}
(see §
6
).

1.3.3.
Good reduction.

We address reduction modulo primes of
B
and primes of good reduction for
E_{\mathbf{a}}
in §
4.4
. To define good reduction we adapt the point of view of Gardeyn
[
25
]
, requiring that the rank of a
t
-comotive and its reduction be the same. In Corollary
4.4.11
we confirm that
E_{\mathbf{a}}
has a model with good reduction outside of a finite set of primes
\Sigma_{\mathbf{a}}
of
B
containing the primes dividing
f
. These conditions are sufficient to prove Theorem
D
and Corollary
E
below, but we prove in the appendix that for every prime
\mathfrak{p}
of
B
not dividing
f
, there is a model for
E_{\mathbf{a}}
for which it has good reduction at
\mathfrak{p}
(see Corollary
A.1.17
).

Corollary B
.

Let
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
be effective with
\deg\mathbf{a}>0
, and let
\mathfrak{p}
be a maximal ideal of
B
relatively prime to
f
. Then
E_{\mathbf{a}}
has good reduction at
\mathfrak{p}
.

Another component in our special
L
-value formulas is the determination of the period lattice of
E_{\mathbf{a}}
. These periods had already been computed by Sinha
[
48
]
*§5, but as his
t
-modules were constructed using
t
-motives instead of
t
-comotives, they are not technically the same. However, in the setting of
t
-comotives, one can apply Anderson’s exponentiation theorem (Theorem
2.2.14
, see also
[
30
]
*§2.5
[
39
]
*§3.4), which permits us to bypass some of the complications of
[
48
]
stemming from rigid analysis and homological algebra.

1.3.4.
Rigid analytic trivializations of
H_{\mathbf{a}}
.

A major input into Anderson’s exponentiation theorem is a rigid analytic trivialization of the
t
-comotive (see §
2.2
). Adapting arguments from
[
1
]
*§6.4, we show in Proposition
4.2.6
that a rigid analytic trivialization for
H_{\mathbf{a}}
can be expressed in terms of the infinite product

\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{a}}=\prod_{N=1}^{\infty}g_{\mathbf{a}}^{(N)},

which is a product of Frobenius twists of
g_{\mathbf{a}}
taken in an extension of the Tate algebra
\mathbb{T}_{\theta}
(see Proposition
3.3.26
, and also
[
48
]
*§5.1, where similar products were investigated).

1.3.5.
Interpolation formulas.

For
\mathbf{a}=\sum_{a}m_{a}[a/f]\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
we take
\Pi(\mathbf{a})=\prod_{a}\Pi(a/f)^{m_{a}}
. Anderson
[
5
]
*§5.3 (see also
[
1
]
*§6.3
[
48
]
*§5.3) proved interpolation formulas for Coleman functions yielding for
b\in(A/fA)^{\times}
,

\Pi(b\star\mathbf{a})=\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{a}}(\xi_{b})^{-1},

where
\xi_{b}\in\mathbf{X}(K)
are generic points arising from the embeddings
K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
and
b\star\mathbf{a}
represents the star action. See §
3.3
for more information. Sinha
[
48
]
*§5.2.6, §5.3.9 showed that certain coordinates of periods of
E_{\mathbf{a}}
are expressible in terms of special
\Pi
-values. We extend Sinha’s results to arbitrary coordinates using hyperderivatives on
\overline{k}(\mathbf{X})
(stated as Theorem
4.2.11
). We say
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
is
basic
if it is of the form
\mathbf{a}=[a/f]
, and note that Sinha’s evaluations in
[
48
]
*§5.3 revolve around these basic cases where
(a,f)=1
.

Theorem C
.

Let
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
be effective with
\deg\mathbf{a}>0
. Let
E_{\mathbf{a}}
be constructed with respect to a normalized
\overline{k}[\sigma]
-basis on
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\overline{k})
. The period lattice
\Lambda_{\mathbf{a}}
of
E_{\mathbf{a}}
has an
\mathbf{A}
-basis
\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{r}
so that for index sets
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{a}}
,
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{a}}
defined in §
4.1
, the following hold.

(a)

For
1\leqslant i\leqslant r
, with
\ell_{a}=\mult_{\xi_{b}}(\Xi_{\mathbf{a}})-1
,

\pi_{i}=\begin{pmatrix}\vdots\\
\partial_{t}^{\ell_{b}-j}\bigl(z^{i-1}\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{a}}^{-1}\bigr)\big|_{\xi_{b}}\\
\vdots\end{pmatrix}_{(b,j)\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{a}}}.

The coordinate corresponding to
(b,\ell_{b})\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{a}}
is
\rho_{b}(\zeta)^{i-1}\Pi(b\star\mathbf{a})

(cf.
[
48
]
*§5.3)
.

(b)

When
\mathbf{a}=[a/f]
is basic, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant r
,

\pi_{i}=\begin{pmatrix}\vdots\\
\rho_{b}(\zeta)^{i-1}\Pi\bigl([ba/f]\bigr)\\
\vdots\end{pmatrix}_{b\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{a}}}.

1.4.
Special
L
-values

Our main results relate Goss
L
-functions for
E_{\mathbf{a}}
with
L
-functions for
\chi_{\mathbf{a}}
, and then we prove special value formulas for them in terms of special
\Pi
-values.

1.4.1.
Goss
L
-functions for
E_{\mathbf{a}}
.

For
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
effective with
\deg\mathbf{a}>0
, we pick a model
E_{\mathbf{a}}:\mathbf{A}\to\Mat_{d}(B[\tau])
together with a finite set of primes
\Sigma_{\mathbf{a}}
of
B
, including all primes dividing
f
, outside of which
E_{\mathbf{a}}
has good reduction (see §
5.3
). The Goss
L
-function is

L(E_{\mathbf{a}}^{\vee}/K,\Sigma_{\mathbf{a}},s)=\prod_{\mathfrak{p}\notin\Sigma_{\mathbf{a}}}Q_{\mathbf{a},\mathfrak{p}}^{\vee}\bigl(\mathcal{N}(\mathfrak{p})^{s}\bigr)^{-1},\quad s\in\mathbb{Z},

where
Q_{\mathbf{a},\mathfrak{p}}^{\vee}(X)
is the reciprocal polynomial of the characteristic polynomial of the Frobenius at
\mathfrak{p}
acting on the dual of the Tate module
T_{\lambda}(E_{\mathbf{a}})
(see §
2.4
).

1.4.2.
Identities with Hecke
L
-series.

Letting
\psi_{\mathbf{a}}=\chi_{-\mathbf{a}}
, we prove in Proposition
5.1.10
,

L(E_{\mathbf{a}}^{\vee}/K,\Sigma_{\mathbf{a}},s)=\prod_{b\in(A/fA)^{\times}}L(\psi_{\mathbf{a}}^{\rho_{b}},\Sigma_{\mathbf{a}},s)=L(\psi_{\mathbf{a}},\Sigma_{\mathbf{a}},s)^{r}.

We obtain the first side of this identity by computing the characteristic polynomials of Frobenius of
E_{\mathbf{a}}
using techniques of
[
33
]
and relating them to
\psi_{\mathbf{a}}
(cf.
[
21
]
*§3.3.5). The second part utilizes Galois symmetry (see Lemma
3.4.11
).

1.4.3.
L
-value identities.

The main tools for evaluating Goss
L
-functions are rooted in the work of Taelman
[
49
,
50
,
51
]
. For the
t
-modules
E_{\mathbf{a}}
we apply
L
-value formulas of Fang
[
23
]
, which as in Taelman’s identities equate
L(E_{\mathbf{a}}^{\vee}/K,\Sigma_{\mathbf{a}},0)
with the product of a regulator term
\mathrm{Reg}(E_{\mathbf{a}}/B)\in k_{\infty}^{\times}
and a class module term
\mathrm{h}(E_{\mathbf{a}}/B)\in A_{+}
(see §
5.2
). We appeal also to constructions of Anglès, Ngo Dac, Pellarin, and Tavares Ribeiro
[
6
,
7
,
8
,
9
,
10
]
. Although the model for
E_{\mathbf{a}}
used to construct
L(E_{\mathbf{a}}^{\vee}/K,\Sigma_{\mathbf{a}},s)
may have bad reduction at some primes not dividing
f
, Corollary
B
permits defining
L(E^{\vee}/K,s)
that avoids only Euler factors for
\mathfrak{p}\mid f
(see Remark
5.1.12
). The following theorem and corollary (stated as Theorem
5.3.15
and Corollary
5.3.17
) assume that
(a,f)=1
, but the full results for
(a,f)\neq 1
are in §
5.3
.

Theorem D
.

Let
\mathbf{a}=[a/f]\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
be basic with
(a,f)=1
, and fix
E_{\mathbf{a}}:\mathbf{A}\to\Mat_{d}(B[\tau])
as in §
5.3
. There is
C_{\mathrm{R}}\in\overline{k}^{\times}
with
\sgn(C_{\mathrm{R}})=1
and

C_{\mathrm{R}}^{r}\in\begin{cases}k^{\times}&\text{if $r$ is even,}\\
K^{\times}\ \text{and}\ C_{\mathrm{R}}^{2r}\in k^{\times}&\text{if $r$ is odd;}\end{cases}

C_{\mathrm{H}}\in\overline{k}^{\times}
with
\sgn(C_{\mathrm{H}})=1
so that
\mathrm{h}(E_{\mathbf{a}}/B)=C_{\mathrm{H}}^{r}
; and
C_{\mathrm{E}}\in K^{\times}
with
C_{\mathrm{E}}^{r}\in k^{\times}
and
\sgn(C_{\mathrm{E}})=1
, so that

L(E_{\mathbf{a}}^{\vee}/K,0)=C_{\mathrm{R}}^{r}\cdot C_{\mathrm{H}}^{r}\cdot C_{\mathrm{E}}^{r}\cdot\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in A_{+}\\
\deg b<\deg f\\
(b,f)=1\end{subarray}}\Pi(b/f)^{r}.

The constants
C_{\mathrm{R}}
,
C_{\mathrm{H}}
, and
C_{\mathrm{E}}
, arise from calculations for the regulator, class module, and extra Euler factors respectively, and typically depend on the model for
E_{\mathbf{a}}
(see §
5.3
).

Corollary E
.

Under the same conditions as Theorem
D
,

L(\psi_{\mathbf{a}},0)=C_{\mathrm{R}}\cdot C_{\mathrm{H}}\cdot C_{\mathrm{E}}\cdot\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in A_{+}\\
\deg b<\deg f\\
(b,f)=1\end{subarray}}\Pi(b/f).

Remark 1.4.4
.

These special value formulas rely on
\mathbf{a}
being basic in a fundamental way. By Theorem
C
, the coordinates of periods of
E_{\mathbf{a}}
are
B
-multiples of elements of
k_{\infty}
, and as such the period lattice comprises a portion of Taelman’s regulator lattice when computing
\mathrm{Reg}(E_{\mathbf{a}}/B)
. When
\mathbf{a}
is basic, the rank of
\Lambda_{\mathbf{a}}
is the same as the rank of the regulator lattice, and thus up to a multiple from
A_{+}
the regulator can be determined from the period lattice (see Remark
5.3.7
and Proposition
5.3.9
).
When
\mathbf{a}
is not basic,
\Lambda_{\mathbf{a}}
forms a submodule of strictly smaller rank than the regulator lattice, and there are additional contributions to
\mathrm{Reg}(E_{\mathbf{a}}/B)
coming from as yet to be determined logarithms.

1.5.
Outline

After preliminary discussions in §
2
, we review information on Carlitz cyclotomic fields, Coleman functions, and Anderson Hecke characters in §
3
. We investigate refined properties of Sinha modules
E_{\mathbf{a}}
in §
4
, especially rigid analytic properties of their
t
-comotives in §
4.2
and fields of definition in §
4.3
. In §
5
we prove our main results on Goss and Hecke
L
-series for
E_{\mathbf{a}}
and
\psi_{\mathbf{a}}
, and we provide examples in §
6
. Finally in §
A
we characterize primes of good reduction for
E_{\mathbf{a}}
.

Acknowledgments
.

The authors thank C.-Y. Chang, N. Ramachandran, and F.-T. Wei for useful discussions on the contents of this paper.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Notation

We will use the following notation throughout.

\mathbb{F}_{q}

=

a finite field with
q=p^{m}
elements.

A

=

\mathbb{F}_{q}[\theta]
, a polynomial ring in a variable
\theta
.

A_{+}

=

the monic elements of
A
.

k

=

\mathbb{F}_{q}(\theta)
, the field of fractions of
A
.

k_{\infty}

=

{\mathbb{F}_{q}((1/\theta))}
, the completion of
k
at its infinite place.

\mathbb{C}_{\infty}

=

completion of an algebraic closure of
k_{\infty}
.

{\lvert\,\cdot\,\rvert}_{\infty}

=

the
\infty
-adic norm on
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
, normalized so that
{\lvert\theta\rvert}_{\infty}=q
.

\deg

=

-\ord_{\infty}=\log_{q}{\lvert\,\cdot\,\rvert}_{\infty}
.

\overline{k}

=

the algebraic closure of
k
in
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
.

R^{\mathrm{perf}}

=

for a ring
R\subseteq\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
, the perfection of
R
in
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
.

\widetilde{\theta}

=

a fixed choice of
(-\theta)^{1/(q-1)}
in
\overline{k}
.

f

=

a fixed polynomial in
A_{+}
,
\deg f\geqslant 1
.

a\bmod f

=

for
a\in A
, the remainder of
a
upon division by
f
.

K
,
K_{f}

=

the
f
-th Carlitz cyclotomic extension of
k
(see §
3.1
), contained in
\overline{k}
.

B
,
B_{f}

=

the integral closure of
A
in
K
.

\mathbf{A}

=

\mathbb{F}_{q}[t]
, for a variable
t
independent from
\theta
.

\mathbf{k}
,
\mathbf{K}
,
\mathbf{B}

=

fixed isomorphic copies of
k
,
K
,
B
in
\overline{\mathbb{F}_{q}(t)}
.

2.1.1.
Matrices.

For a ring
R
, we let
\Mat_{\ell\times m}(R)
denote the set of
\ell\times m
matrices with entries in
R
. We set
\Mat_{m}(R)=\Mat_{m\times m}(R)
and
R^{m}=\Mat_{m\times 1}(R)
. For a matrix
M
,
M^{{\mathsf{T}}}
denotes its transpose. When
R
is commutative, we let
\Char(N,X)\in R[X]
to be the characteristic polynomial of a square matrix
N
and
\Char(\alpha,V,X)
to be the characteristic polynomial of an
R
-linear map
\alpha:V\to V
on a free
R
-module
V
of finite rank.

2.1.2.
Rings of functions and Frobenius twisting.

For
c\in\mathbb{C}_{\infty}^{\times}
, we define the
Tate algebra

\mathbb{T}_{c}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\biggl\{\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}a_{i}t^{i}\biggm|{\lvert c\rvert}_{\infty}^{i}\cdot{\lvert a_{i}\rvert}_{\infty}\to 0\biggr\},

which consists of power series that converge on the closed disk of
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
of radius
{\lvert c\rvert}_{\infty}
. The canonical Tate algebra for the closed unit disk is denoted
\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{T}_{1}
. Primarily we will use
\mathbb{T}
and
\mathbb{T}_{\theta}
in this paper. The
Gauss norm
on
\mathbb{T}_{c}
is defined for
g=\sum a_{i}t^{i}\in\mathbb{T}_{c}
as

\lVert g\rVert_{c}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\max_{0\leqslant i<\infty}\bigl({\lvert c\rvert}_{\infty}^{i}\cdot{\lvert a_{i}\rvert}_{\infty}\bigr),

and
\mathbb{T}_{c}
is complete with respect to
\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert_{c}
. Moreover,
\mathbb{T}_{c}
is the completion of
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}[t]
with respect to the Gauss norm. If
F
is complete, then
\mathbb{T}_{c}(F)
is also complete. We will write
\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert=\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert_{1}
. For more information on Tate algebras see
[
24
]
.

For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
g=\sum a_{i}t^{i}\in{\mathbb{C}_{\infty}((t))}
, we define as usual the
n
-th Frobenius twist
to be

g^{(n)}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum a_{i}^{q^{n}}t^{i}
. Restricted to subrings such as
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}[t]
or
\mathbb{T}
, Frobenius twisting is an automorphism. Moreover, for
g\in\mathbb{T}
, we have
\lVert g^{(n)}\rVert=\lVert g\rVert^{q^{n}}
. More generally, for
c\in\mathbb{C}_{\infty}^{\times}
, we have
g\mapsto g^{(n)}:\mathbb{T}_{c}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{T}_{c^{q^{n}}}
. For a matrix
M
with entries in
{\mathbb{C}_{\infty}((t))}
, we let
M^{(n)}
be the corresponding matrix where entrywise
(M^{(n)})_{ij}=M_{ij}^{(n)}
.

2.1.3.
Twisted polynomial rings.

Let
F
be a field extension of
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Let
\tau:F\to F
denote the
q
-th power Frobenius map
x\mapsto x^{q}
. We set
F[\tau]
to be the ring of twisted polynomials, subject to the relation
\tau c=c^{q}\tau
, for
c\in F
. Then we consider
F[\tau]
to be the
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-algebra
\End_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}(\mathbb{G}_{a}/F)
(see
[
29
]
*Ch. 1). We further consider the ring
F[t,\tau]
of polynomials in
t
and
\tau
, which contains
F[\tau]
as a subring, and satisfies

\tau h=h^{(1)}\tau,\quad h\in F[t].

If
F
is perfect, we let
\sigma=\tau^{-1}
, and define
F[\sigma]
and
F[t,\sigma]
similarly with
\sigma h=h^{(-1)}\sigma
.

For
n\geqslant 1
we define
\Mat_{n}(F)[\tau]=\Mat_{n}(F[\tau])
and
\Mat_{n}(F)[\sigma]=\Mat_{n}(F[\sigma])
(when
F
is perfect) similarly so that

\tau M=M^{(1)}\tau,\quad\sigma M=M^{(-1)}\sigma\quad M\in\Mat_{n}(F).

We consider
\Mat_{n}(F[\tau])
to be the
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-algebra
\End_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}(\mathbb{G}_{a}^{n}/F)
, and if
\alpha=M_{0}+M_{1}\tau+\dots+M_{s}\tau^{s}\in\Mat_{n}(F)[\tau]
and
\mathbf{x}\in F^{n}
, then

(2.1.4)

\alpha(\mathbf{x})=M_{0}\mathbf{x}+M_{1}\mathbf{x}^{(1)}+\dots+M_{s}\mathbf{x}^{(s)}.

For such
\alpha
, we set
\mathrm{d}\alpha\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=M_{0}
.

2.1.5.
The adjoint
*
-operator.

For
F
perfect,
*:F[\tau]\to F[\sigma]
is defined by

\alpha=\sum a_{i}\tau^{i}\mapsto\alpha^{*}=\sum a_{i}^{1/q^{i}}\sigma^{i}.

Then
*
is an anti-isomorphism of
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-algebras, and notably
(\alpha\beta)^{*}=\beta^{*}\alpha^{*}
for
\alpha
,
\beta\in F[\tau]
. By abuse of notation the inverse of
*
is also denoted by
*:F[\sigma]\to F[\tau]
, and it is thought of as an anti-involution. See
[
29
]
*§1.7 for more details.

We extend the
*
-operator to matrices,
*:\Mat_{\ell\times m}(F[\tau])\to\Mat_{m\times\ell}(F[\sigma])
, by setting

(\alpha_{ij})^{*}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(\alpha_{ij}^{*})^{{\mathsf{T}}}.

In this way, if
\alpha
,
\beta
are matrices over
F[\tau]
with dimensions so that
\alpha\beta
is defined, then
(\alpha\beta)^{*}=\beta^{*}\alpha^{*}
. Again by abuse of notation the inverse of
*
is also denoted by
*
. See
[
13
]
*§1.5.4
[
39
]
*§2.2 for more details.

2.2.
Anderson
t
-modules and
t
-comotives

Anderson defined
t
-modules in
[
3
]
as higher dimensional versions of Drinfeld modules. Together with this definition Anderson introduced the theory of its corresponding
t
-motive. Later
t
-comotives (née dual
t
-motives) were defined in
[
1
]
, and although one can attach both a
t
-motive and
t
-comotive to a given
t
-module, they do not a priori carry the same information. From the standpoint of abelian
t
-modules, an explicit equivalence was settled by Maurischat
[
38
]
. For more on the general connections among these objects see
[
13
,
30
,
39
]
.

Remark 2.2.1
.

As suggested by Gazda and Maurischat
[
26
]
*§3, we have elected to use the term “
t
-comotive” instead of “dual
t
-motive.” The reason is that the latter term, though historical, can lead to confusion when one refers also to the “dual of a
t
-motive.” For similar reasons and additional consistency we later use “coabelian” instead of “
\mathbf{A}
-finite.”

2.2.2.
t
-modules.

Let
F
be a field extension of
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. We fix an
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-algebra homomorphism
i:\mathbf{A}\to F
, and let
\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\theta}=i(t)
. We call the kernel of
i
the characteristic of
F
, and if
\ker i=\{0\}
, the characteristic is generic. When
F\supseteq A
, we will always choose
i(t)=\theta\in A
as defined in §
2.1
. Otherwise, in particular when
F
is a finite field, we will need to specify
\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\theta}
. An
Anderson
t
-module
defined over
F
is an
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-algebra homomorphism
E:\mathbf{A}\to\Mat_{d}(F)[\tau]
such that
\mathrm{d}E_{t}-\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\theta}\mathrm{I}_{d}
is nilpotent. We refer to
d
as the dimension of
E
, and if
d=1
, then
E
is a Drinfeld module.
Using (
2.1.4
),
E
induces an
\mathbf{A}
-module structure on
L^{d}
, where
L
is an
F
-algebra. That is, for
a\in\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{x}\in L^{d}
, we have
a\cdot\mathbf{x}=E_{a}(\mathbf{x})
. We will write
E(L)
for
L^{d}
with this
\mathbf{A}
-module structure. For
\Lie(E)
, we can identify
\Lie(E)(L)
with
L^{d}
, and then the induced
\mathbf{A}
-module structure is obtained through
a\mapsto\mathrm{d}E_{a}
.

For a
t
-module
E
over
F
and an element
b\in\mathbf{A}
, the
b
-torsion submodule of
E
is
E[b]=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5muF}{}^{d}\mid E_{b}(\mathbf{x})=0\}
.
We say that
E
is regular if there is an integer
r
so that for all
b\in\mathbf{A}
relatively prime to the characteristic
\ker i\subseteq\mathbf{A}
, we have
E[b]\cong(\mathbf{A}/b\mathbf{A})^{r}
. In this case, if
\lambda\in\mathbf{A}_{+}
is a prime element with
\lambda\notin\ker i
, we have the Tate module

T_{\lambda}(E)=\varprojlim E[\lambda^{m}]\cong\mathbf{A}_{\lambda}^{r},

where
\mathbf{A}_{\lambda}
is the
\lambda
-adic completion of
\mathbf{A}
. As usual,
T_{\lambda}(E)
is a Galois module over
\mathbf{A}_{\lambda}
.

2.2.3.
t
-comotives.

We follow the initial definitions from
[
1
]
*§4. Let
F
be a perfect
\mathbf{A}
-field, and let
H
be a left
F[t,\sigma]
-module. Consider the three conditions:

(i)

H
is free of finite rank as a left
F[t]
-module;

(ii)

H
is free of finite rank as a left
F[\sigma]
-module;

(iii)

For
N\gg 0
, we have
(t-\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\theta})^{N}H\subseteq\sigma H
.

We say that
H
is a
t
-comotive
if (ii)–(iii) holds, and additionally
H
is
coabelian
if (i) is satisfied. The
dimension
of
H
is
\rank_{F[\sigma]}(H)
, and if
H
is coabelian, the
rank
of
H
is
\rank_{F[t]}(H)
. Morphisms of
t
-comotives are morphisms of left
F[t,\sigma]
-modules.

2.2.4.
From
t
-modules to
t
-comotives.

Let
E
be a
d
-dimensional
t
-module over a perfect
\mathbf{A}
-field
F
. Then we define the
t
-comotive associated to
E
to be

H(E)=\Mat_{1\times d}(F[\sigma]).

Then
H(E)
has the natural structure of a left
F[\sigma]
-module, and for
b\in\mathbf{A}
and
h\in H(E)
we define
b\cdot h\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=hE_{b}^{*}
. In this way
H(E)
has the structure of a left
F[t,\sigma]
-module, with condition (ii) of
2.2
trivially satisfied. Furthermore, the condition that
\mathrm{d}E_{t}-\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\theta}I_{d}
be nilpotent implies that condition (iii) of
2.2
holds, and so
H(E)
is a
t
-comotive. If
H(E)
is coabelian, then we also say that
E
is coabelian, and then
E
is regular with
r=\rank_{F[t]}H(E)
. For more details, see
[
13
]
*§1.5.4
[
30
]
*§2.5.2
[
39
]
*§2.3.

2.2.5.
From
t
-comotives to
t
-modules.

After some preliminary definitions the transition from
t
-comotives to
t
-modules, originally due to Anderson, can be made explicit. Define two maps
\varepsilon_{0}
,
\varepsilon_{1}:\Mat_{1\times d}(F[\sigma])\to F^{d}
by

(2.2.6)

\varepsilon_{0}\biggl(\sum\mathbf{a}_{i}\sigma^{i}\biggr)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbf{a}_{0}^{{\mathsf{T}}},\quad\varepsilon_{1}\biggl(\sum\mathbf{a}_{i}\sigma^{i}\biggr)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\biggl(\sum\mathbf{a}_{i}^{(i)}\biggr)^{{\mathsf{T}}},

where
\varepsilon_{0}
is
F
-linear and
\varepsilon_{1}
is
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-linear.
Now let
H
be a
t
-comotive. One checks that after selecting an
F[\sigma]
-basis for
H
the induced maps,

(2.2.7)

\varepsilon_{0}:\frac{H}{\sigma H}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}F^{d},\quad\varepsilon_{1}:\frac{H}{(\sigma-1)H}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}F^{d},

are isomorphisms of
F
-vector spaces and
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-vector spaces respectively. They induce
\mathbf{A}
-module structures on
F^{d}
, for which there is a
t
-module
E:\mathbf{A}\to\Mat_{d}(F[\tau])
with

(2.2.8)

\frac{H}{\sigma H}\cong\Lie(E)(F)\ \ \textup{($F[t]$-linear),}\quad\frac{H}{(\sigma-1)H}\cong E(F)\ \ \textup{($\mathbf{A}$-linear)}.

The defining matrices for
E
depend on the choice of
F[\sigma]
-basis for
H
. Specifically, if we fix an
F[\sigma]
-basis
h_{1},\dots,h_{d}
of
H
, then there is a matrix
Q\in\Mat_{d}(F[\sigma])
such that
t(h_{1},\dots,h_{d})^{{\mathsf{T}}}=Q(h_{1},\dots,h_{d})^{{\mathsf{T}}}
. Then
E
is defined by

(2.2.9)

E_{t}=Q^{*}\in\Mat_{d}(F[\tau]).

Moreover, if
h_{1}^{\prime},\dots,h_{d}^{\prime}
is another
F[\sigma]
-basis of
H
, say
(h_{1}^{\prime},\dots,h_{d}^{\prime})^{{\mathsf{T}}}=P(h_{1},\dots,h_{d})^{{\mathsf{T}}}
for
P\in\GL_{d}(F[\sigma])
, then the resulting
t
-module
E^{\prime}:\mathbf{A}\to\Mat_{d}(F[\tau])
satisfies

(2.2.10)

E^{\prime}_{t}=(P^{*})^{-1}\cdot E_{t}\cdot P^{*}

(e.g., see
[
39
]
*Eq. (3.22)). Thus any two such
t
-modules are isomorphic over
F
. Notable also is that the processes
H\mapsto E
and
E\mapsto H
in this paragraph and §
2.2
are mutual inverses up to isomorphism. See
[
13
]
*§1.5.6
[
30
]
*Prop. 2.5.8
[
39
]
*§3.1 for more details.

2.2.11.
Exponentials.

Let
F
be an intermediate field
k\subseteq F\subseteq\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
, and let
E:\mathbf{A}\to\Mat_{d}(F[\tau])
be a
t
-module. Anderson
[
3
]
defined a series
\exp_{E}=\sum_{i\geqslant 0}C_{i}\tau^{i}\in{\Mat_{d}(F)[[\tau]]}
such that
C_{0}=\mathrm{I}_{d}
and as power series in
\tau
,

\exp_{E}\cdot\,\mathrm{d}E_{b}=E_{b}\cdot\exp_{E},\quad b\in\mathbf{A}.

Moreover,
\exp_{E}
is unique and is determined by the single functional equation for
t
itself. Letting
\mathbf{z}=(z_{1},\dots,z_{d})^{{\mathsf{T}}}\in\mathbb{C}_{\infty}[z_{1},\dots,z_{d}]^{d}
denote a system of coordinates for
E(\mathbb{C}_{\infty})
, we can express
\exp_{E}
as
\exp_{E}(\mathbf{z})=\sum_{i\geqslant 0}C_{i}\mathbf{z}^{(i)}\in{F[[z_{1},\dots,z_{d}]]}^{d}
, and the induced function
\exp_{E}:\mathbb{C}_{\infty}^{d}\to\mathbb{C}_{\infty}^{d}
is entire. The functional equation for
\exp_{E}
does not immediately imply that each
C_{i}\in\Mat_{d}(F)
(but rather only in
\Mat_{d}(\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5muF})
), but this can be verified from Anderson’s original arguments in
[
3
]
(see
[
39
]
*Rem. 2.6). The map

\exp_{E}:\Lie(E)(\mathbb{C}_{\infty})\to E(\mathbb{C}_{\infty}),

is an
\mathbf{A}
-module homomorphism, and if it is surjective, then
E
is said to be
uniformizable
. The kernel
\Lambda_{E}=\ker\exp_{E}
is a discrete
\mathbf{A}
-submodule of
\Lie(E)(\mathbb{C}_{\infty})
.

2.2.12.
t
-frames of coabelian
t
-modules.

Continuing with the constructions of the previous paragraph, assume now that
E
is coabelian of rank
r
, and let
\mathbf{h}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{h}_{r}\in H(E)
be an
F[t]
-basis. For
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{r})\in\Mat_{1\times r}(F[t])
, the map

\iota:\Mat_{1\times r}(F[t])\to H(E),\quad(\boldsymbol{\alpha}\mapsto\alpha_{1}\mathbf{h}_{1}+\dots+\alpha_{r}\mathbf{h}_{r}),

is an isomorphism of
F[t]
-modules. Notably, for
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Mat_{1\times r}(F[t])
and
b\in\mathbf{A}
we have

\mathrm{d}E_{b}\bigl((\varepsilon_{0}\circ\iota)(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\bigr)=(\varepsilon_{0}\circ\iota)(b\boldsymbol{\alpha}),\quad E_{b}\bigl((\varepsilon_{1}\circ\iota)(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\bigr)=(\varepsilon_{1}\circ\iota)(b\boldsymbol{\alpha}),

(e.g., see
[
39
]
*Lem. 3.7). If we let
\mathbf{h}=(\mathbf{h}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{h}_{r})^{{\mathsf{T}}}
, then there is
\Phi\in\Mat_{r}(F[t])
with

\sigma\mathbf{h}=\Phi\mathbf{h},

and
(\iota,\Phi)
is a
t
-frame
of
E
. Also,
\det\Phi=c(t-\theta)^{d}
for
c\in F^{\times}
(see
[
39
]
*Prop. 3.5).

2.2.13.
Anderson’s exponentiation theorem.

Continuing with the constructions of §
2.2
, a matrix
\Psi\in\GL_{r}(\mathbb{T})
that satisfies
\Psi^{(-1)}=\Phi\Psi
is a
rigid analytic trivialization
for
E
, or for
\Phi
. The entries of
\Psi
converge everywhere on
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
by
[
1
]
*Prop. 3.1.3.

Anderson proved that the map
\varepsilon_{0}\circ\iota:\Mat_{1\times r}(\mathbb{C}_{\infty}[t])\to\mathbb{C}_{\infty}^{d}
extends to a unique bounded
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
-linear map

\mathcal{E}_{0}:\bigl(\Mat_{1\times r}(\mathbb{T}_{\theta}),\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert_{\theta}\bigr)\to\bigl(\mathbb{C}_{\infty}^{d},{\lvert\,\cdot\,\rvert}_{\infty}\bigr)

of normed vector spaces (see
[
30
]
*Prop. 2.5.8
[
39
]
*Lem. 3.12). Determining
\mathcal{E}_{0}
can be worked out for many common
t
-modules (see
[
39
]
*§3.5 for various examples and §
4.1
below). We also let
\mathcal{E}_{1}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\varepsilon_{1}\circ\iota:\Mat_{1\times r}(\mathbb{C}_{\infty}[t])\to\mathbb{C}_{\infty}^{n}
.
A key result connecting exponentiation on
E
to its
t
-comotive is due to Anderson.

Theorem 2.2.14

(Anderson; see
[
30
]
*Thm. 2.5.21, Cor. 2.5.23, Thm. 2.5.32
[
37
]
*§1
[
39
]
*Thm. 3.13, Thm. 3.18)
.

Let
E:\mathbf{A}\to\Mat_{d}(\mathbb{C}_{\infty}[\tau])
be a coabelian
t
-module. Let
(\iota,\Phi)
be a
t
-frame of
E
.

(a)

Suppose
\mathbf{g}\in\Mat_{1\times r}(\mathbb{T}_{\theta})
and
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Mat_{1\times r}(\mathbb{C}_{\infty}[t])
satisfy
\mathbf{g}^{(-1)}\Phi-\mathbf{g}=\boldsymbol{\alpha}
. Then

\exp_{E}\bigl(\mathcal{E}_{0}(\mathbf{g}+\boldsymbol{\alpha})\bigr)=\mathcal{E}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}).

(b)

E
is uniformizable if and only if it has a rigid analytic trivialization.

(c)

For a rigid analytic trivialization
\Psi
of
E
,

\Lambda_{E}=\mathcal{E}_{0}\bigl(\Mat_{1\times r}(\mathbf{A})\cdot\Psi^{-1}\bigr)\subseteq\mathbb{C}_{\infty}^{n}.

Thus values of
\exp_{E}
can be captured in terms of values of rigid analytic functions satisfying certain difference equations. Moreover, the period lattice is generated by the rows of
\Psi^{-1}
under evaluation by
\mathcal{E}_{0}
.

2.3.
Hecke characters

We adapt definitions from Schappacher
[
44
]
for algebraic Hecke characters, but see also work of Goss
[
27
]
and Hayes
[
32
]
.

2.3.1.
Signs and positivity.

For precision we adopt notions from
[
6
]
*§1 for defining sign functions on
\overline{k}_{\infty}^{\times}
. See also
[
29
]
*§7.2. For
r\in\mathbb{Q}
, we define
\theta^{r}
unambiguously in the following way. We let
\theta_{1}=\theta
, and for
n\geqslant 2
, take
\theta_{n}\in\overline{k}_{\infty}^{\times}\subseteq\mathbb{C}_{\infty}^{\times}
so that
\theta_{n}^{n}=\theta_{n-1}
. Then for
r=m/n!
, we set
\theta^{r}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\theta_{n}^{m}
, which is well-defined. Then
\theta^{r}\theta^{s}=\theta^{r+s}
for all
r
,
s\in\mathbb{Q}
, and moreover, for
r\in\mathbb{Q}
, we have
\deg(\theta^{r})=r
. Letting
U_{\infty}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{x\in\overline{k}_{\infty}:\ord_{\infty}(x-1)>0\}
, we have

\overline{k}_{\infty}^{\times}=\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{\times}\times(\theta^{-1})^{\mathbb{Q}}\times U_{\infty}
,
and so each
x\in\overline{k}_{\infty}^{\times}
can be expressed uniquely as

x=\sgn(x)\cdot\theta^{\deg(x)}\cdot u(x),\quad\sgn(x)\in\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{\times},\ u(x)\in U_{\infty}.

In this way we define the sign function
\sgn:\overline{k}_{\infty}^{\times}\to\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{\times}
. Elements of
\overline{k}_{\infty}^{\times}
of sign
1
are called
positive
, e.g., the monic polynomials
A_{+}
are precisely the positive elements of
A
.

2.3.2.
Algebraic Hecke characters.

Let
K
and
F
be finite separable extensions of
k
contained in
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
, and assume
K/k
is abelian. Let
G=\Gal(K/k)
, and fix
\Theta=\sum m_{\rho}\rho\in\mathbb{Z}[G]
. Let
B
be the integral closure of
A
in
K
. For a non-zero integral ideal
\mathfrak{f}
of
B
, let
\mathcal{I}_{K,\mathfrak{f}}
denote the group of fractional ideals of
K
with support disjoint from
\mathfrak{f}
.
An
algebraic Hecke character

\chi
of conductor dividing
\mathfrak{f}
and infinity type
\Theta
is a group homomorphism

\chi:\mathcal{I}_{K,\mathfrak{f}}\to F^{\times},

such that for any principal ideal
(\alpha)\in\mathcal{I}_{K,\mathfrak{f}}
with
\alpha\in K^{\times}
and
\alpha\equiv 1\pmod{\mathfrak{f}}
, we have

(2.3.3)

\chi((\alpha))=\prod_{\rho\in G}\rho(\alpha)^{m_{\rho}}=\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}\alpha^{\Theta}.

2.4.
Goss
L
-series

We consider
L
-series initially defined by Goss
[
29
]
*Ch. 8, which take values in
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
. Although these
L
-series can be extended to Goss’s non-archimedean analytic space
S_{\infty}
(see
[
29
]
*§8.1), we will need only consider their values at integers.

2.4.1.
L
-series attached to Anderson
t
-modules.

We appeal to the definitions in Goss
[
29
]
*§8.6. Let
F/k
be a finite separable extension with ring of integers
B
, and let
E:\mathbf{A}\to\Mat_{d}(B[\tau])
be a coabelian
t
-module of rank
r
. For
\lambda\in\mathbf{A}_{+}
irreducible, let
\beta_{\lambda}:\Gal(F^{\mathrm{sep}}/F)\to\GL_{r}(\mathbf{A}_{\lambda})
be the representation induced by the Galois action on the Tate module
T_{\lambda}(E)
, and let
\beta_{\lambda}^{\vee}
be its dual. For an integral prime
\mathfrak{p}
of
B
such that
\mathfrak{p}\nmid\lambda(\theta)
, let
\alpha_{\mathfrak{p}}\in\Gal(F^{\mathrm{sep}}/F)
be a Frobenius element and set

P_{\beta_{\lambda},\mathfrak{p}}(X)=\Char(\alpha_{\mathfrak{p}},T_{\lambda}(E),X),\quad P_{\beta_{\lambda}^{\vee},\mathfrak{p}}(X)=\Char(\alpha_{\mathfrak{p}},T_{\lambda}(E)^{\vee},X),

to be the associated characteristic polynomials in
\mathbf{A}_{\lambda}[X]
and
\mathbf{k}_{\lambda}[X]
.

We now assume that
(\beta_{\lambda})
and
(\beta_{\lambda}^{\vee})
form
strictly compatible
families of representations (see Goss
[
29
]
*Def. 8.6.5). Therefore, there is a finite set of primes
\Sigma_{E}
of
B
such that for primes
\mathfrak{p}
outside
\Sigma_{E}
,
P_{\beta_{\lambda},\mathfrak{p}}(X)
and
P_{\beta_{\lambda}^{\vee},\mathfrak{p}}(X)
have coefficients in
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{k}
respectively and are independent of the choice of
\lambda
. Thus we can write

P_{\mathfrak{p}}(X)=P_{\beta_{\lambda},\mathfrak{p}}(X)|_{t=\theta}\in A[X],\quad P_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\vee}(X)=P_{\beta_{\lambda},\mathfrak{p}}^{\vee}(X)|_{t=\theta}\in k[X],

without ambiguity. We further take
Q_{\mathfrak{p}}(X)=X^{r}P_{\mathfrak{p}}(1/X)
and
Q_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\vee}(X)=X^{r}P_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\vee}(1/X)
to be their reciprocal polynomials. For
\mathfrak{p}
a finite prime of
F
lying above
\wp\in A_{+}
, i.e., a maximal ideal of
B
above
\wp A
, we let
\mathcal{N}(\mathfrak{p})=\wp^{[\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}:\mathbb{F}_{\wp}]}
, where
\mathbb{F}_{\wp}=A/\wp A
and
\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}=B/\mathfrak{p}
. Moreover, we extend
\mathcal{N}(\mathfrak{b})
to all integral ideals of
B
, and then

\mathcal{N}(\mathfrak{b})A=\biggl(\prod_{\rho\in\Emb(F/k)}\rho(\mathfrak{b})\biggr)\cap A

gives the compatibility with the ideal norm. The
Goss
L
-series
for
E/F
are defined as

(2.4.2)

L(E/F,s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\prod_{\mathfrak{p}\notin\Sigma_{E}}Q_{\mathfrak{p}}\bigl(\mathcal{N}(\mathfrak{p})^{-s}\bigr)^{-1},\quad L(E^{\vee}/F,s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\prod_{\mathfrak{p}\notin\Sigma_{E}}Q_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\vee}\bigl(\mathcal{N}(\mathfrak{p})^{-s}\bigr)^{-1}.

Here
s\in\mathbb{Z}
, and the products take values (when they converge) in
k_{\infty}
.

Remark 2.4.3
.

Goss
[
29
]
*Ex. 8.6.6.2 demonstrated that, when
E
is a Drinfeld module of positive rank, its associated Galois representations are strictly compatible. In this case the primes
\Sigma_{E}
are the primes of bad reduction for
E
. For higher dimensional
t
-modules the situation is more subtle, as discovered by Gardeyn
[
25
]
*§9. However, as we will see in §
4.4
and §
A
the Sinha modules
E_{\mathbf{a}}
we investigate have well-defined primes of good reduction and we avoid the technicalities in
[
25
]
.

2.4.4.
Hecke
L
-series.

Hecke
L
-series over function fields were initially developed by Goss
[
27
]
. In our context let
\chi:\mathcal{I}_{K,\mathfrak{f}}\to F^{\times}
be a Hecke character as in §
2.3
, and let
B
be the integral closure of
A
in
K
. We define the Goss
L
-series attached to
\chi
to be

L(\chi,s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathfrak{b}\subseteq B\\
(\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{f})=1\end{subarray}}\frac{\chi(\mathfrak{b})}{\mathcal{N}(\mathfrak{b})^{s}}=\prod_{\mathfrak{p}\,\nmid\,\mathfrak{f}}\biggl(1-\frac{\chi(\mathfrak{p})}{\mathcal{N}(\mathfrak{p})^{s}}\biggr)^{-1},\quad s\in\mathbb{Z},

which, when it converges, takes values in the completion of
F
in
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
. For Anderson’s Hecke characters (see Proposition
5.1.10
), these
L
-functions converge for all
s\gg 0
.

2.5.
Special functions

Here we recall various functions associated to Sinha modules that we will need. Where possible we utilize the conventions from
[
1
]
.

2.5.1.
Carlitz exponential and
\mathbf{e}
.

The following constructions go back to Carlitz
[
14
]
, but also appear in many sources such as
[
29
]
*Ch. 3
[
40
]
*Ch. 5
[
43
]
*Ch. 12
[
56
]
*Ch. 2. The Carlitz module
C:\mathbf{A}\to A[\tau]
is the rank
1
Drinfeld module defined by
C_{t}=\theta+\tau
. For
a\in A
, we will abuse notation and write
C_{a}(z)
for the more cumbersome
C_{a(t)}(z)
.

The exponential function for
C
and its logarithm are given by the infinite series,

\exp_{C}(z)=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\frac{z^{q^{i}}}{D_{i}},\quad\log_{C}(z)=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\frac{z^{q^{i}}}{L_{i}},

where
D_{0}=1
,
D_{i}=(\theta^{q^{i}}-\theta)(\theta^{q^{i}}-\theta^{q})\cdots(\theta^{q^{i}}-\theta^{q^{i-1}})
,
i\geqslant 1
,
L_{0}=1
, and
L_{i}=(\theta-\theta^{q})\cdots(\theta-\theta^{q^{i}})
,
i\geqslant 1
.
Then
\ker\exp_{C}=\Lambda_{C}=\widetilde{\pi}A
is generated by the Carlitz period

(2.5.2)

\widetilde{\pi}=-\widetilde{\theta}^{q}\,\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}\Bigl(1-\theta^{1-q^{j}}\Bigr)^{-1}\in k_{\infty}(\widetilde{\theta}),

where
\widetilde{\theta}=(-\theta)^{1/(q-1)}
is a fixed choice of a
(q-1)
-st root of
-\theta
. To be made consistent with the choice of
\theta^{1/(q-1)}
from §
2.3
, this amounts to fixing a root
(-1)^{1/(q-1)}
. Then following
[
1
]
*§5.2 we define
\mathbf{e}:k_{\infty}\to k_{\infty}(\widetilde{\theta})
by

(2.5.3)

\mathbf{e}(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\exp_{C}(\widetilde{\pi}x),\quad x\in k_{\infty}.

If we vary
x\in k^{\times}
, then
\mathbf{e}(x)
runs through torsion points on
C
. That is, for
f\in A_{+}
,

(2.5.4)

C[f(t)]=\bigl\{\mathbf{e}\bigl(a/f\bigr)\mid a\in A\bigr\}.

We note also that
\ker\mathbf{e}=A
and that

{\lvert x\rvert}_{\infty}<1\quad\Rightarrow\quad{\lvert\mathbf{e}(x)\rvert}_{\infty}={\lvert\theta\rvert}_{\infty}^{q/(q-1)}\cdot{\lvert x\rvert}_{\infty}.

Furthermore, as
\exp_{C}
restricted to the open disk in
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
of radius
{\lvert\theta\rvert}_{\infty}^{q/(q-1)}
is an isometry by
[
29
]
*Prop. 4.14.2, we find that
\mathbf{e}(k_{\infty})=\widetilde{\pi}\cdot{\mathbb{F}_{q}[[1/\theta]]}
(see
[
1
]
*§5.2.10).

2.5.5.
The function
\Omega
.

As in
[
1
]
*§3.1.2 we define
\Omega(t)
as

(2.5.6)

\Omega(t)=\widetilde{\theta}^{-q}\,\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}\biggl(1-\frac{t}{\theta^{q^{j}}}\biggr)\in\mathbb{T},

which satisfies the functional equation
\Omega^{(-1)}=(t-\theta)\Omega
. By
[
1
]
*Prop. 3.1.3 it has an infinite radius of convergence on
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
. As such it is a rigid analytic trivialization for the
t
-comotive of the Carlitz module (e.g., see
[
13
]
*§1.5), and
\Omega(\theta)=-1/\widetilde{\pi}
.

In analogy with the Anderson-Thakur function
\omega=\sum_{i\geqslant 0}\mathbf{e}(1/\theta^{i+1})t^{i}
(see
[
2
]
*§2.5), which is a generating series for Carlitz
t
-power torsion, we expand
\Omega(t)
,

(2.5.7)

\Omega(t)=\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\widetilde{\mathbf{e}}\bigl(1/\theta^{i+1}\bigr)t^{i}.

This defines the sequence
\{\widetilde{\mathbf{e}}(1/\theta^{i})\mid i\geqslant 0\}\subseteq k_{\infty}(\widetilde{\theta})
.
Moreover, we define
\widetilde{\mathbf{e}}:k_{\infty}\to k_{\infty}(\widetilde{\theta})
to be the unique continuous
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-linear function that interpolates this sequence and satisfies
\widetilde{\mathbf{e}}|_{A}=0
. The product in (
2.5.6
) implies that
\widetilde{\mathbf{e}}(k_{\infty})\subseteq(k_{\infty}(\widetilde{\theta}))^{q}
, and thus the function

(2.5.8)

\mathbf{e}^{*}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\bigl(x\mapsto\widetilde{\mathbf{e}}(x)^{1/q}\bigr):k_{\infty}\to k_{\infty}(\widetilde{\theta}),

is well-defined, continuous, and
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-linear. One sees readily that
\mathbf{e}^{*}
is the same function
\mathbf{e}^{*}
defined in
[
1
]
*§5.2.5.
Moreover, (
2.5.8
) shows that
\Omega^{(-1)}(t)\in\mathbb{T}(k_{\infty}(\widetilde{\theta}))
.

By comparing (
2.5.6
) and (
2.5.7
), for
i\geqslant 0
,

{\lvert\widetilde{\mathbf{e}}(1/\theta^{i+1})\rvert}_{\infty}={\lvert\widetilde{\theta}\rvert}_{\infty}^{-q}\cdot\bigl|\theta^{-(q+q^{2}+\cdots+q^{i})}\bigr|_{\infty}={\lvert\widetilde{\theta}\rvert}_{\infty}^{-q^{i+1}}
,
and thus for
x\in k_{\infty}^{\times}
with
\deg x<0
,

{\lvert\widetilde{\mathbf{e}}(x)\rvert}_{\infty}={\lvert\widetilde{\theta}\rvert}_{\infty}^{-q^{-\deg x}}.

In particular,

(2.5.9)

{\lvert\mathbf{e}^{*}(x)\rvert}_{\infty}={\lvert\widetilde{\theta}\rvert}_{\infty}^{-q^{-1-\deg x}},\quad x\in k_{\infty}^{\times},\ \deg x<0.

See also
[
1
]
*Pf. of Lem. 5.2.6.

2.5.10.
The Carlitz adjoint and
\widetilde{\mathbf{e}}
.

As defined in
[
28
]

[
29
]
*§3.7, the adjoint of the Carlitz module is defined by the
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-algebra homomorphism
C^{*}:\mathbf{A}\to k^{\mathrm{perf}}[\sigma]
such that
C^{*}_{t}=\theta+\sigma
, and more generally for
a\in\mathbf{A}
,
C_{a}^{*}=(C_{a})^{*}
. In this way
C^{*}
defines an
\mathbf{A}
-module structure on perfect intermediate fields
k^{\mathrm{perf}}\subseteq F\subseteq\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
. Since
t\Omega(t)=\theta\Omega(t)+\Omega^{(-1)}(t)
,

C_{t}^{*}\bigl(\widetilde{\mathbf{e}}(1/\theta^{i+1})\bigr)=\widetilde{\mathbf{e}}(1/\theta^{i}),\quad i\geqslant 0,

and so
\{\widetilde{\mathbf{e}}(1/\theta^{i})\mid i\geqslant 1\}
is a division sequence of
t
-power torsion on
C^{*}
. By (
2.5.7
) we see that
\Omega(t)
is an Anderson generating function for torsion on the adjoint Carlitz module.
Moreover, it follows that for any
x\in k_{\infty}
, we have
C_{t}^{*}(\widetilde{\mathbf{e}}(x))=\widetilde{\mathbf{e}}(\theta x)
. Now as in the case of the Carlitz module, for
a\in A
we will write
C_{a}^{*}
for
C_{a(t)}^{*}
to simplify notation. It follows from the
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-linearity of
\widetilde{\mathbf{e}}
that for
a\in A
and
x\in k_{\infty}
,

(2.5.11)

C_{a}^{*}\bigl(\widetilde{\mathbf{e}}(x)\bigr)=\widetilde{\mathbf{e}}(ax).

See
[
1
]
*Rem. 5.2.9. In particular,
C^{*}[f(t)]=\{\widetilde{\mathbf{e}}(a/f)\mid a\in A\}
for
f\in A
,
f\neq 0
.

Remark 2.5.12
.

Given the similarities between
C\leftrightarrow\mathbf{e}
and
C^{*}\leftrightarrow\widetilde{\mathbf{e}}
, we might have chosen to use ‘
\mathbf{e}^{*}
’ for notation here instead of ‘
\widetilde{\mathbf{e}}
.’ However,
\mathbf{e}^{*}
was already defined in
[
1
]
as what turns out to be the
q
-th root of
\widetilde{\mathbf{e}}
, and we chose to be consistent with this previous work to avoid confusion. Furthermore,
\mathbf{e}^{*}
is the more natural function from the standpoint of Coleman functions and Sinha modules (see §
3.3
and §
4.1
).

2.5.13.
The
\Psi_{N}
polynomials.

We recall
\Psi_{N}(x)\in k[x]
,
N\geqslant 0
, studied initially by Carlitz
[
14
]
and then by Anderson and Thakur
[
2
]
*§3.4. One defines
\Psi_{N}(x)
through

\exp_{C}\bigl(x\log_{C}(z)\bigr)=\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}\Psi_{N}(x)z^{q^{N}}=\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}\biggl(\sum_{j=0}^{N}\frac{x^{q^{j}}}{D_{j}L_{N-j}^{q^{j}}}\biggr)z^{q^{N}}.

We then have the factorizations,

(2.5.14)

\Psi_{N}(x)=\frac{1}{D_{N}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in A\\
\deg a<N\end{subarray}}(x-a),\quad\Psi_{N}(x)-1=\frac{1}{D_{N}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in A_{+}\\
\deg a=N\end{subarray}}(x-a).

Of particular importance to us is how
\Psi_{N}(x)
governs a kind of duality between
\mathbf{e}
and
\mathbf{e}^{*}
. We let
\Res:k_{\infty}\to\mathbb{F}_{q}
denote the projection to
\mathbb{F}_{q}
given by
\Res(\sum c_{i}\theta^{-i})=c_{1}
. That is,
\Res(x)=-\res_{\infty}(x\,d\theta)
on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
. We then have the following result.

Lemma 2.5.15

(
[
1
]
*Lem. 5.4.2)
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{Z}
,
x\in k_{\infty}
, satisfy
\deg x\leqslant\min(-1,N)
. Then

\sum_{i\geqslant 0}\mathbf{e}^{*}\bigl(\theta^{-i-1}\bigr)^{q^{N+1}}\mathbf{e}(\theta^{i}x)=\begin{cases}-\Psi_{N}(x)&\textup{if $N\geqslant 0$,}\\
\Res(\theta^{-N-1}x)&\textup{if $N<0$.}\end{cases}

2.5.16.
Geometric factorial
\Pi(x)
and
\Psi_{N}(x)
.

Recalling Thakur’s factorial
\Pi(x)
from §
1.2
, we also have a factorization in terms of the
\Psi_{N}(x)
polynomials
[
53
]
*Rem. 5.8,

(2.5.17)

\Pi(x)=\prod_{N=0}^{\infty}\bigl(1+\Psi_{N}(x)\bigr)^{-1}.

Indeed this follows from (
2.5.14
), using that
\Psi_{N}(x)
is odd and the fact that
D_{N}
is the product of all monic polynomials in
A
of degree
N
(e.g., see
[
29
]
*Prop. 3.1.6).

3.
Coleman functions and Anderson Hecke characters

3.1.
Cyclotomic fields and Carlitz torsion

The construction of Carlitz cyclotomic fields mirrors the development of cyclotomic extensions of
\mathbb{Q}
. It has its foundations in Carlitz
[
15
]
, who investigated explicit class field theory for
k
, which Hayes
[
31
]
completed. See also
[
29
]
*Ch. 7
[
40
]
*Ch. 7
[
56
]
*Ch. 3.
We fix throughout a polynomial
f\in A_{+}
with
\deg f\geqslant 1
. As noted in (
2.5.4
),
C[f(t)]=\{\mathbf{e}(a/f)\mid a\in A,\ \deg a<\deg f\}
, and we set

\zeta\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\zeta_{f}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbf{e}(1/f),

which generates
C[f(t)]
as an
\mathbf{A}
-module. The
f
-th Carlitz cyclotomic field
is

K\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=K_{f}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=k(\zeta_{f}),

which is a finite Galois extension. As
\mathbf{e}(a/f)=C_{a}(\mathbf{e}(1/f))
, we have
K_{f}=k(C[f(t)])
.

3.1.1.
Galois action.

The irreducible polynomial of
\zeta_{f}
over
k
is the irreducible factor
\delta_{f}(z)
of
C_{f(t)}(z)\in A[z]
such that

\delta_{f}(z)=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in A\\
\deg a<\deg f\\
(a,f)=1\end{subarray}}\Bigl(z-\mathbf{e}(a/f)\Bigr)\in A[z].

It is the analogue for
k
of the classical cyclotomic polynomial. We have an isomorphism

(b\mapsto\rho_{b}):(A/fA)^{\times}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Gal(K_{f}/k),

which is compatible with Artin automorphisms. Moreover, for all
a\in A
,
b\in(A/fA)^{\times}
,

(3.1.2)

\rho_{b}\bigl(\mathbf{e}(a/f)\bigr)=C_{b}\bigl(\mathbf{e}(a/f)\bigr)=\mathbf{e}(ba/f).

See
[
40
]
*§7.1 for details.

3.1.3.
Adjoint Carlitz torsion and
\mathbf{e}^{*}
.

Goss
[
28
]
*Thm. 3.2
[
29
]
*Thm. 1.7.11 showed that

k(C[f(t)])=k(C^{*}[f(t)])
,
and so

(3.1.4)

K_{f}=k\bigl(\widetilde{\mathbf{e}}(1/f)\bigr)=k\bigl(\widetilde{\mathbf{e}}(a/f)\mid a\in A\bigr).

Furthermore,
[
29
]
*Prop. 4.14.13
[
42
]
*Cor. 11 show that the Galois action on
C^{*}[f(t)]
is dual to that on
C[f(t)]
. It follows that for
b\in(A/fA)^{\times}
,

(3.1.5)

\rho_{b}^{-1}\bigl(\widetilde{\mathbf{e}}(a/f)\bigr)=C_{b}^{*}\bigl(\widetilde{\mathbf{e}}(a/f)\bigr)=\widetilde{\mathbf{e}}(ba/f).

Proposition 3.1.6
.

Let
f\in A_{+}
,
\deg f\geqslant 1
. Then

K_{f}=k\bigl(\mathbf{e}^{*}(1/f)\bigr)=k\bigl(\mathbf{e}^{*}(a/f)\mid a\in A\bigr).

Moreover, for
b\in(A/fA)^{\times}
and
a\in A
,

\rho_{b}^{-1}\bigl(\mathbf{e}^{*}(a/f)\bigr)=\mathbf{e}^{*}(ba/f).

Proof.

Since
k_{\infty}(\widetilde{\theta})\cap\overline{k}\subseteq k^{\mathrm{sep}}
, it follows from (
2.5.8
) that
\mathbf{e}^{*}(a/f)\in k^{\mathrm{sep}}
for all
a\in A
. Moreover, since
\mathbf{e}^{*}(x)=\widetilde{\mathbf{e}}(x)^{1/q}
for
x\in k_{\infty}
, it must then be that
\mathbf{e}^{*}(a/f)\in K_{f}=k(\widetilde{\mathbf{e}}(1/f))
for all
a\in A
. The proposition then follows from (
3.1.4
)–(
3.1.5
).
∎

The Carlitz torsion points
\mathbf{e}(a/f)
are automatically integral over
A
since
C_{f}(x)\in A[x]
has leading coefficient
\sgn(f)=1
. However,
\widetilde{\mathbf{e}}(a/f)
and
\mathbf{e}^{*}(a/f)
need not be elements of
B_{f}
, though we can bound their denominators.

Proposition 3.1.7
.

Let
f\in A_{+}
,
\deg f\geqslant 1
, and let
\mathfrak{p}
be a finite prime of
B_{f}
. For
a\in A
,

\ord_{\mathfrak{p}}\bigl(\mathbf{e}^{*}(a/f)\bigr)\geqslant-\frac{1}{q-1}\cdot\ord_{\mathfrak{p}}(f).

Proof.

(cf. Papikian
[
40
]
*Prop. 6.4.1) Letting
m=\deg f
, we note that
C_{f}^{*}(x)^{q^{m}}
is in
A[x]
and has
\widetilde{\mathbf{e}}(a/f)=\mathbf{e}^{*}(a/f)^{q}
as a root. Also,
C_{f}^{*}(x)^{q^{m}}
has degree
q^{m}
in
x
, constant term
1
, and leading coefficient
f^{m}
. The next highest degree term of
C_{f}^{*}(x)^{q^{m}}
has degree at most
q^{m-1}
, so the steepest possible slope in the
\mathfrak{p}
-adic Newton polygon for
C_{f}^{*}(x)^{q^{m}}
would be

\frac{q^{m}\ord_{\mathfrak{p}}(f)}{q^{m}-q^{m-1}}=\frac{q}{q-1}\ord_{\mathfrak{p}}(f).

This yields the desired inequality.
∎

3.2.
Cyclotomic curves over
\mathbb{F}_{q}

The main reference is
[
1
]
*§6.3. See also
[
5
]

[
48
]
*§2.2.

3.2.1.
Definitions.

We define
\mathcal{C}_{f}(t,z)\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[t,z]
by
\mathcal{C}_{f}(t,z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=C_{f}(z)|_{\theta=t}
, and likewise
\mathcal{D}_{f}(t,z)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\delta_{f}(z)|_{\theta=t}\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[t,z]
. We let

U\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=U_{f}/\mathbb{F}_{q}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\Spec\mathbb{F}_{q}[t,z]/(\mathcal{D}_{f}(t,z))=\Spec\mathbf{B}

be the plane curve in
\mathbb{A}^{2}/\mathbb{F}_{q}
, and we let
X/\mathbb{F}_{q}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=X_{f}/\mathbb{F}_{q}
be its nonsingular projective model. For an algebraically closed field
k\subseteq\mathbb{K}\subseteq\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
, we further let

\mathbf{U}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbf{U}_{f}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbb{K}\times_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}U_{f},\quad\mathbf{X}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbf{X}_{f}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbb{K}\times_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}X_{f},

be their extensions to
\mathbb{K}
. It was shown in
[
1
]
*§6.3.2–4 that
U
and
X
satisfy several useful properties. The curves
U
,
\mathbf{U}
,
X
, and
\mathbf{X}
are smooth and absolutely irreducible. The function field
\mathbf{K}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbf{K}_{f}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbb{F}_{q}(X_{f})
is naturally isomorphic to
K
by sending
t\leftrightarrow\theta
,
z\leftrightarrow\zeta_{f}
. As such
(A/fA)^{\times}\cong\Gal(\mathbf{K}/\mathbf{k})
by
b\mapsto\widetilde{\rho}_{b}
, and

(3.2.2)

\widetilde{\rho}_{b}(z)=\mathcal{C}_{b}(t,z)\quad(\in\mathbf{B}).

Projecting onto the
t
-line makes
X
into a
\Gal(\mathbf{K}/\mathbf{k})
-cover of
\mathbb{P}^{1}/\mathbb{F}_{q}
of degree
r\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=[K:k]=(A/fA)^{\times}
.
We have
\mathbb{K}[\mathbf{U}]=\mathbb{K}[t,z]/(\mathcal{D}_{f}(t,z))
and
\mathbb{K}(\mathbf{X})=\mathbb{K}(t,z)
, and for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
n
-fold twisting extends to both
\mathbb{K}[\mathbf{U}]
and
\mathbb{K}(\mathbf{X})
. For
g\in\mathbb{K}(\mathbf{X})
,
\divf(g^{(n)})=\divf(g)^{(n)}
.

3.2.3.
Generic points on
\mathbf{X}_{f}
.

For each
a\in(A/fA)^{\times}
we set

\xi_{a}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\bigl(\theta,\mathbf{e}(a/f)\bigr)=\bigl(\theta,C_{a}(\zeta)\bigr)\in\mathbf{U}(\mathbb{K})\subseteq\mathbf{X}(\mathbb{K}).

The points
\xi_{a}
are distinct and rational over
K
, possessing a compatible
\Gal(K/k)
-action,

\rho_{b}(\xi_{a})=\xi_{ba},\quad b\in(A/fA)^{\times}.

We further let
\xi\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\xi_{1}=(\theta,\zeta)
.

3.2.4.
Points at
\infty
.

Under the cover
t:X\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
projecting on the
t
-line, the decomposition subgroup of
\infty\in\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
in
\Gal(K/k)
corresponds to
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}
. Taking
n=|(A/fA)^{\times}/\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}|=r/(q-1)
, we find that
X
has
n
points above
\infty
,

\infty_{1},\dots,\infty_{n}\in X(\mathbb{F}_{q}),

which are
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-rational. Letting
K^{+}\subseteq K
denote the fixed field of
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}\hookrightarrow\Gal(K/k)
, we obtain the
maximal real subfield
of
K
, and
K^{+}=k(\zeta_{f}^{q-1})
. Then
\infty
is totally split in
K^{+}
, and places above
\infty
in
K^{+}
are totally ramified in
K/K^{+}
. See
[
43
]
*Thm. 12.14.

Letting
I=\infty_{1}+\dots+\infty_{n}
be the sum of the points above infinity in
\Div(\mathbf{X})
, we note that as a function on
\mathbf{X}
,

(3.2.5)

\divf(t-\theta)=-(q-1)I+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in A\\
\deg a<\deg f\\
(a,f)=1\end{subarray}}\xi_{a}.

3.2.6.
Rigid analytic functions.

Let
\mathbb{B}=\mathbb{T}[z]
, where as above we carry the relation
\mathcal{D}_{f}(t,z)=0
. In this way,
\mathbb{B}
is the affinoid algebra of rigid analytic functions on the inverse image of the closed unit disk in
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
under the projection
t:\mathbf{X}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
. We define similarly
\mathbb{B}_{\theta}=\mathbb{T}_{\theta}[z]
, and
\mathbb{B}_{\theta}\subseteq\mathbb{B}
. The Gauss norms on
\mathbb{T}
and
\mathbb{T}_{\theta}
extend to
\mathbb{B}
and
\mathbb{B}_{\theta}
: noting that
1
,
z,\dots,z^{r-1}
is a
\mathbb{T}
-basis for
\mathbb{B}
(respectively, a
\mathbb{T}_{\theta}
-basis for
\mathbb{B}_{\theta}
),

g=\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}g_{i}z^{i}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\lVert g\rVert=\max_{0\leqslant i\leqslant r-1}\bigl\{\lVert g_{i}\rVert\bigr\},

(similarly for
\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert_{\theta}
). The rings
\mathbb{B}
and
\mathbb{B}_{\theta}
are complete with respect to
\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert
and
\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert_{\theta}
. See
[
47
]
*§3.2 for more information in this particular situation.

3.3.
Coleman functions

We review the construction of Coleman functions, which were initially studied by Anderson
[
5
]
and Sinha
[
47
,
48
]
, who used Anderson’s soliton functions to generalize previous examples of Coleman
[
18
]
. The definition of Coleman functions was simplified in
[
1
]
*§6.3, and we rely on the definition there.

3.3.1.
Parameter space
\mathcal{A}_{f}
.
Again we fix
f\in A_{+}
with
\deg f\geqslant 1
, and we define symbols
[x]
for
x\in f^{-1}A
, such that
[x]=[x^{\prime}]
if
x\equiv x^{\prime}\pmod{A}
. We then define
\mathcal{A}_{f}
to be the free abelian group on
[x]
for
x\in f^{-1}A
, and so
\mathcal{A}_{f}
has rank
q^{\deg f}
with

\mathcal{A}_{f}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in A\\
\deg a<\deg f\end{subarray}}\mathbb{Z}\biggl[\frac{a}{f}\biggr].

A parameter
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
of the form
\mathbf{a}=[a/f]
with
a\neq 0
is called
basic
. If
\mathbf{a}=\sum m_{a}[a/f]
has all non-negative coefficients, it is
effective
, and the
degree
of
\mathbf{a}
is

\deg\mathbf{a}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in A\setminus\{0\}\\
\deg a<\deg f\end{subarray}}m_{a}.

Then
\deg
induces a homomorphism on
\mathcal{A}_{f}/(\mathbb{Z}[0/f])
but not on
\mathcal{A}_{f}
itself.

Remark 3.3.2
.

As defined here,
\deg\mathbf{a}=(q-1)\wt\mathbf{a}
from
[
1
]
*§6.1.1.
We do not need to include the case of
[0/f]
, as we will only be concerned with
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
that are effective with
\deg\mathbf{a}>0
; however, we maintain terminology consistent with
[
1
,
46
,
47
,
48
]
.

3.3.3.
The
\star
-operator on
\mathcal{A}_{f}
.

For
b\in A
with
(b,f)=1
, there is a unique automorphism

(\mathbf{a}\mapsto b\star\mathbf{a}):\mathcal{A}_{f}\to\mathcal{A}_{f},

with
b\star[a/f]=[ba/f]
. For
b
,
c\in A
relatively prime to
f
, we have
b\star(c\star\mathbf{a})=(bc)\star\mathbf{a}
.

3.3.4.
Diamond brackets.

For
x\in k_{\infty}
and
N\geqslant 0
we define
\langle x\rangle
,
\langle x\rangle_{N}\in\{0,1\}
as follows (cf.
[
1
]
*§5.5.1). Write
x=a+y
uniquely with
a\in A
and
y\in(1/\theta)\cdot{\mathbb{F}_{q}[[1/\theta]]}
. Then

\langle x\rangle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}1&\textup{if $\sgn(y)=1$,}\\
0&\textup{otherwise,}\end{cases}\quad\langle x\rangle_{N}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}1&\textup{if $y=1/\theta^{N+1}+O(1/\theta^{N+2})$,}\\
0&\textup{otherwise.}\end{cases}

It follows that
\langle x\rangle=\sum_{N\geqslant 0}\langle x\rangle_{N}
. We note that for
a\in A
and
f\in A_{+}
(cf.
[
48
]
*§3.3.7),

(3.3.5)

\langle a/f\rangle=\langle(a\bmod f)/f\rangle=\begin{cases}1&\textup{if $a\bmod f\in A_{+}$,}\\
0&\textup{otherwise.}\end{cases}

We set homomorphisms
\langle\,\cdot\,\rangle_{N}
,
\langle\,\cdot\,\rangle:\mathcal{A}_{f}\to\mathbb{Z}
, with
\langle[a/f]\rangle_{N}=\langle a/f\rangle_{N}
,
\langle[a/f]\rangle=\langle a/f\rangle
.

3.3.6.
Sinha’s divisors.

Let
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
. Define divisors on
\mathbf{X}
by

(3.3.7)

\displaystyle\Xi_{\mathbf{a}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in A\\
\deg b<\deg f\\
(b,f)=1\end{subarray}}\langle b\star\mathbf{a}\rangle\xi_{b},

\displaystyle W_{\mathbf{a}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in A\\
\deg b<\deg f\\
(b,f)=1\end{subarray}}\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\langle b\star\mathbf{a}\rangle_{N}\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\xi_{b}^{(j)}.

The sum defining
W_{\mathbf{a}}
has only finitely many non-zero terms, and so
W_{\mathbf{a}}
is indeed a divisor.
Using (
3.3.5
), we note that for
[a/f]\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
,

(3.3.8)

\displaystyle\Xi_{[a/f]}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in A\\
\deg b<\deg f\\
(b,f)=1\end{subarray}}\biggl\langle\frac{ba}{f}\biggr\rangle\xi_{b},

\displaystyle W_{[a/f]}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{\deg f-2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in A\\
\deg b<\deg f\\
(b,f)=1\\
\deg(ba\bmod f)\leqslant j\end{subarray}}\biggl\langle\frac{ba}{f}\biggr\rangle\xi_{b}^{(\deg f-j-2)},

which coincides with
[
48
]
*§3.3.7. In particular,

(3.3.9)

\Xi_{[1/f]}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in A_{+}\\
\deg b<\deg f\\
(b,f)=1\end{subarray}}\xi_{b},\quad W_{[1/f]}=\sum_{j=0}^{\deg f-2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in A_{+}\\
\deg b\leqslant j\\
(b,f)=1\end{subarray}}\xi_{b}^{(\deg f-j-2)}.

Necessarily,
\Xi_{[0/f]}=W_{[0/f]}=0
. When
[a/f]\neq[0/f]
but possibly
(a,f)\neq 1
, we can write
a/f=a^{\prime}/f^{\prime}
for
f^{\prime}\in A_{+}
and
(a^{\prime},f^{\prime})=1
. Then for the natural projection
\pi:\mathbf{X}_{f}\to\mathbf{X}_{f^{\prime}}
,

(3.3.10)

\Xi_{[a/f]}=\pi^{*}\Xi_{[a^{\prime}/f^{\prime}]},\quad W_{[a/f]}=\pi^{*}W_{[a^{\prime}/f^{\prime}]},

which matches with
[
48
]
*§3.3.7.

Lemma 3.3.11
.

Let
\mathbf{a}=\sum m_{a}[a/f]\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
. The following hold.

(a)

We have
\Xi_{\mathbf{a}}=\sum m_{a}\Xi_{[a/f]}
and
W_{\mathbf{a}}=\sum m_{a}W_{[a/f]}
.

(b)

For
c\in(A/fA)^{\times}
, we have
\Xi_{c\star\mathbf{a}}=\rho_{c}^{-1}(\Xi_{\mathbf{a}})

W_{c\star\mathbf{a}}=\rho_{c}^{-1}(W_{\mathbf{a}})
.

Proof.

Part (a) is straightforward from (
3.3.7
) and the
\mathbb{Z}
-linearity of diamond brackets on
\mathcal{A}_{f}
. For (b) we need only check the cases
\Xi_{[a/f]}
and
W_{[a/f]}
. Choosing
e\in A
with
ec\equiv 1\pmod{f}
, it follows from (
3.3.8
) that

\rho_{c}^{-1}(\Xi_{[a/f]})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in A\\
\deg b<\deg f\\
(b,f)=1\end{subarray}}\biggl\langle\frac{ba}{f}\biggr\rangle\xi_{eb}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in A\\
\deg b<\deg f\\
(b,f)=1\end{subarray}}\biggl\langle\frac{bca}{f}\biggr\rangle\xi_{b}=\Xi_{c\star[a/f]}.

The result for
W_{[a/f]}
is similar.
∎

In particular, since
\Xi_{[a/f]}
and
W_{[a/f]}
are effective, Lemma
3.3.11
(a) implies that
\Xi_{\mathbf{a}}
and
W_{\mathbf{a}}
are effective whenever
\mathbf{a}
is effective. Furthermore, for
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
,

\deg\Xi_{\mathbf{a}}=\frac{r\deg\mathbf{a}}{q-1},\quad r=[K:k].

3.3.12.
f
-dual families.

A pair of sequences
\{a_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\deg f}
,
\{b_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\deg f}
, of polynomials in
A
is called an
f
-dual family
if

\Res\biggl(\frac{a_{i}b_{j}}{f}\biggr)=\delta_{ij},\quad 1\leqslant i,j\leqslant\deg f,

where
\delta_{ij}
is the Kronecker delta. In particular
\{a_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\deg f}
,
\{b_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\deg f}
both represent
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-bases of
A/fA
. Moreover, the pairing

(b\bmod f,c\bmod f)\mapsto\Res(bc/f):A/fA\times A/fA\to\mathbb{F}_{q}

is perfect (see
[
1
]
*§5.4.3), which provides the existence of
f
-dual families.

Example 3.3.13
.

Let
d=\deg f
. As outlined in
[
1
]
*§5.4.3, we note that for
1\leqslant i,j\leqslant d
,

\Res\bigl(\theta^{i+d-j-1}/f\bigr)=\begin{cases}0&\textup{if $i<j$,}\\
1&\textup{if $i=j$,}\\
*&\textup{if $i>j$.}\end{cases}

Thus if
r_{ij}=\Res(\theta^{i+d-j-1}/f)
, then the matrix
(r_{ij})\in\GL_{d}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
is lower triangular with
1
’s on the diagonal. Denoting its inverse by
(s_{ij})
, we find that taking

a_{i}=\sum_{m=1}^{i}s_{im}\theta^{m-1}\quad(1\leqslant i\leqslant d),\quad b_{j}=\theta^{d-j}\quad(1\leqslant j\leqslant d),

we obtain an
f
-dual family. Each
a_{i}
,
b_{j}
is monic with
\deg a_{i}=i-1
and
\deg b_{j}=d-j
.

Lemma 3.3.14
.

Given an
f
-dual family
\{a_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\deg f}
,
\{b_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\deg f}
, for any
c\in A
,

\sum_{i=1}^{\deg f}\Res(ca_{i}/f)b_{i}\equiv c\pmod{f}.

Proof.

Note that if
c\equiv c^{\prime}\pmod{f}
, then for each
i
we have
\Res(ca_{i}/f)=\Res(c^{\prime}a_{i}/f)
.
There are unique
\gamma_{j}\in\mathbb{F}_{q}
so that
c\equiv\sum_{j=1}^{\deg f}\gamma_{j}b_{j}\pmod{f}
. Thus for each
i
,

\Res(ca_{i}/f)=\sum_{j=1}^{\deg f}\gamma_{j}\Res(a_{i}b_{j}/f)=\gamma_{i},

where the last equality follows from the choice of
\{a_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\deg f}
,
\{b_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\deg f}
as an
f
-dual family. The result then follows from the choice of
\{\gamma_{j}\}
.
∎

Our primary application of
f
-dual families is an extension of Lemma
2.5.15
due to Anderson, Brownawell, and the second author.

Theorem 3.3.15

(
[
1
]
*Thm. 5.4.4)
.

Let
f\in A_{+}
,
\deg f\geqslant 1
, be fixed, and let
\{a_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\deg f}
,
\{b_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\deg f}
be an
f
-dual family. For
a\in A
with
\deg a<\deg f
, the following hold.

(a)

For
N\geqslant 0
,

\sum_{i=1}^{\deg f}\mathbf{e}^{*}(a_{i}/f)^{q^{N+1}}\mathbf{e}(b_{i}a/f)=-\Psi_{N}(a/f).

(b)

Furthermore, if
a\in A_{+}
, then

\sum_{i=1}^{\deg f}\mathbf{e}^{*}(a_{i}/f)\mathbf{e}(b_{i}a/f)^{q^{\deg f-\deg a-1}}=1.

The connection with Lemma
2.5.15
is obtained through the following calculation
[
1
]
*p. 290. Note that for
n\geqslant 0
and
x\in k_{\infty}
,
\Res(\theta^{n}x)
is the coefficient of
\theta^{-n-1}
in
x
, and so as in
[
1
]
*§5.2.5, we have

(3.3.16)

\mathbf{e}^{*}(x)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\Res(\theta^{n}x)\mathbf{e}^{*}(1/\theta^{n+1}).

Therefore, for
N\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\deg f}\mathbf{e}^{*}(a_{i}/f)^{q^{N+1}}\mathbf{e}(b_{i}a/f)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\deg f}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\Res(\theta^{n}a_{i}/f)\mathbf{e}^{*}(\theta^{-n-1})^{q^{N+1}}\mathbf{e}(b_{i}a/f)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathbf{e}^{*}(\theta^{-n-1})^{q^{N+1}}\mathbf{e}\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{\deg f}\Res(\theta^{n}a_{i}/f)b_{i}\cdot\frac{a}{f}\biggr)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathbf{e}^{*}(\theta^{-n-1})^{q^{N+1}}\mathbf{e}(\theta^{n}a/f),

where Lemma
3.3.14
implies the last equality, and the rest follows from Lemma
2.5.15
.

Theorem
3.3.15
(a) plays the role here that Anderson’s interpolation formula
[
5
]
*Thm. 2 played in Sinha
[
48
]
, but without the need of soliton machinery. Theorem
3.3.15
(b) is instrumental in determining the divisor of the Coleman function below (see
[
1
]
*§6.3.6).

3.3.17.
Definition of Coleman functions.

Select an
f
-dual family
\{a_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\deg f}
,
\{b_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\deg f}
. For
[a/f]\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
, the
Coleman function

g_{[a/f]}\in\overline{k}(\mathbf{X})
is defined as in
[
1
]
*§6.3.5 by

(3.3.18)

g_{[a/f]}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=1-\sum_{i=1}^{\deg f}\mathbf{e}^{*}(a_{i}/f)\mathcal{C}_{ab_{i}}(t,z).

One checks that this definition is independent of the choice of
f
-dual family
[
1
]
*§6.3.5
[
20
]
*Prop. 3.5.2.2 and the representation of
[a/f]
in
\mathcal{A}_{f}
. We note that
g_{[0/f]}=1
. For
[a/f]\neq[0/f]
, letting
I=\infty_{1}+\dots+\infty_{n}
as in §
3.2
, by
[
1
]
*§6.3.6 the divisor of
g_{[a/f]}
is

\divf\bigl(g_{[a/f]}\bigr)=-I+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in A\\
(b,f)=1\\
\deg b<\deg f\end{subarray}}\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}\biggl\langle\frac{ba}{f}\biggr\rangle_{N}\,\xi_{b}^{(N)}=-I+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in A\\
(b,f)=1\\
\deg b<\deg f\end{subarray}}\biggl\langle\frac{ba}{f}\biggr\rangle\xi_{b}^{(\deg f-\deg(ba\bmod f)-1)}.

Remark 3.3.19
.

When originally defined by Sinha
[
48
]
using solitons, Sinha used the equivalent formulation

(3.3.20)

\divf\bigl(g_{[a/f]}\bigr)=W_{[a/f]}^{(1)}-W_{[a/f]}+\Xi_{[a/f]}-I,\quad([a/f]\neq[0/f]),

which will be important for us in defining Sinha modules in §
4
. There is a natural comparison to make with divisors of shtuka functions for Drinfeld-Hayes modules (see
[
29
]
*§7.11
[
54
]

[
56
]
*§8.2).

Proposition 3.3.21
.

Let
g_{[a/f]}\in\overline{k}(\mathbf{X})
be defined as above. Then

g_{[a/f]}\in B[f^{-1}][t,z].

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{C}_{a}(t,z)\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[t,z]
for all
a\in A
, the result follows from (
3.3.18
) combined with Proposition
3.1.7
.
∎

For
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
,
\mathbf{a}=\sum m_{a}[a/f]
, we set

(3.3.22)

g_{\mathbf{a}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in A\\
\deg a<\deg f\end{subarray}}g_{[a/f]}^{m_{a}},

and so
g_{0}=1
. By construction (and Lemma
3.3.11
(a)),

(3.3.23)

\divf(g_{\mathbf{a}})=W_{\mathbf{a}}^{(1)}-W_{\mathbf{a}}+\Xi_{\mathbf{a}}-I_{\mathbf{a}},

where for uniformity of notation we write
I_{\mathbf{a}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\deg(\mathbf{a})\cdot I
.

3.3.24.
Infinite products of Frobenius twists.

For
a\in A
with
\deg a<\deg f
, we consider
g_{[a/f]}\in K[t,z]
as an element of the affinoid algebra
\mathbb{B}
. We see from (
3.3.18
), since
\mathcal{C}_{ab_{i}}(t,z)\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[t,z]
, that the Gauss norm of
g_{[a/f]}
is governed by the quantities
{\lvert\mathbf{e}^{*}(a_{i}/f)\rvert}_{\infty}
. Moreover, using (
2.5.9
) we see that
\lVert g_{[a/f]}\rVert=1
,
and more granularly that

(3.3.25)

\lVert g_{[a/f]}-1\rVert\leqslant\max_{i=1}^{\deg f}\bigl\{{\lvert\mathbf{e}^{*}(a_{i}/f)\rvert}_{\infty}\bigr\}={\lvert\widetilde{\theta}\rvert}_{\infty}^{-1}<1,

where the maximum in the middle occurs when
\deg a_{i}=\deg f-1
. We thus obtain the following proposition, improving a result of Sinha
[
48
]
*§5.1.

Proposition 3.3.26
.

For
a\in A
with
\deg a<\deg f
, the infinite product

\mathcal{G}_{[a/f]}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\prod_{N=1}^{\infty}g_{[a/f]}^{(N)}

converges in
\mathbb{B}_{\theta}
with respect to
\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert_{\theta}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{G}_{[a/f]}\in\mathbb{B}_{\theta}^{\times}
.

Proof.

We see from (
3.3.25
) that the product for
\mathcal{G}_{[a/f]}
converges in
\mathbb{B}
. Sinha showed this in
[
48
]
*§5.1 using Green’s functions and verified that
\mathcal{G}_{[a/f]}\in\mathbb{B}^{\times}
. To obtain the results in
\mathbb{B}_{\theta}
we analyze (
3.3.18
) with respect to
\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert_{\theta}
(cf. Wei
[
57
]
*Lem. 5.3.2, Rem. 5.3.3).

Each
\mathcal{C}_{ab_{i}}(t,z)\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[t,z]=\mathbf{B}
that appears in (
3.3.18
) can be expressed uniquely as an
\mathbb{F}_{q}[t]
-linear combination of
1
,
z,\dots,z^{r-1}
. Let
M\geqslant 0
be the maximum of the degrees in
t
of these coefficients as we vary over all
\mathcal{C}_{ab_{i}}(t,z)
. It then follows from (
3.3.18
), (
3.3.25
), and the definition of
\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert_{\theta}
, that for
N\geqslant 0
,

\bigl\lVert g_{[a/f]}^{(N)}-1\bigr\rVert_{\theta}\leqslant{\lvert\widetilde{\theta}\rvert}_{\infty}^{-q^{N}}\cdot{\lvert\theta\rvert}_{\infty}^{M}.

(See also §
4.2
.) Thus
\lVert g_{[a/f]}^{(N)}-1\rVert_{\theta}\to 0
as
N\to\infty
, which shows that
\mathcal{G}_{[a/f]}\in\mathbb{B}_{\theta}
. Now by (
3.3.20
) we see that, for
N\geqslant 1
, the zeros of
g_{[a/f]}^{(N)}
lie outside the inverse image under
t:\mathbf{X}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
of the disk of radius
{\lvert\theta\rvert}_{\infty}
in
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
. In fact the
t
-coordinates of the zeros of
g_{[a/f]}^{(N)}
have norm going to infinity as
N\to\infty
. Thus each
g_{[a/f]}^{(N)}
,
N\geqslant 1
, is invertible in
\mathbb{B}_{\theta}
, and also their product is in
\mathbb{B}_{\theta}^{\times}
.
∎

Remark 3.3.27
.

Since for each
b\in(A/fA)^{\times}
we have
t(\xi_{b})=\theta
, this proposition implies that
\mathcal{G}_{[a/f]}
is regular and non-vanishing at each
\xi_{b}\in\mathbf{X}(K)
.

For
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
with
\mathbf{a}=\sum m_{a}[a/f]
, we then define
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{a}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\prod\mathcal{G}_{[a/f]}^{m_{a}}
, which lies in the fraction field of
\mathbb{B}_{\theta}
.

3.3.28.
Interpolation formulas.

The main application of Coleman functions to special
\Pi
-values was proved by Anderson
[
5
]
*§5.3, but see also
[
1
]
*§6.3
[
48
]
*§5.3. Let
a
,
b\in A
, with
(b,f)=1
, and of degrees
<\deg f
. For
N\geqslant 0
, we see from (
3.3.18
) that

(3.3.29)

g_{[a/f]}^{(N+1)}(\xi_{b})=1-\sum_{i=1}^{\deg f}\mathbf{e}^{*}(a_{i}/f)^{q^{N+1}}C_{ab_{i}}(\mathbf{e}(b/f))\\
=1-\sum_{i=1}^{\deg f}\mathbf{e}^{*}(a_{i}/f)^{q^{N+1}}\mathbf{e}(abb_{i}/f)=1+\Psi_{N}\biggl(\frac{ab\bmod f}{f}\biggr),

where the last equality follows from Theorem
3.3.15
(a). Thus by (
2.5.17
),

(3.3.30)

\Pi\bigl((ab\bmod f)/f\bigr)=\mathcal{G}_{[a/f]}(\xi_{b})^{-1}=\mathcal{G}_{[ba/f]}(\xi)^{-1},

which equates specializations of
\mathcal{G}_{[a/f]}
with special
\Pi
-values. This prompts the definition for
a\in A
, not necessarily of degree
<\deg f
,

\Pi\bigl([a/f]\bigr)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\Pi\bigl((a\bmod f)/f\bigr),

and also for
\mathbf{a}=\sum_{a}m_{a}[a/f]
we take
\Pi(\mathbf{a})=\prod_{a}\Pi([a/f])^{m_{a}}
. In this way, we have

(3.3.31)

\Pi(b\star\mathbf{a})=\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{a}}(\xi_{b})^{-1}=\mathcal{G}_{b\star\mathbf{a}}(\xi)^{-1}.

for
b\in A
with
\deg b<\deg f
,
(b,f)=1
.

Remark 3.3.32
.

There is a slight difference between this formula and the corresponding one in
[
1
]
*§6.3.9, which is stated as

\Pi(b\star\mathbf{a})=\prod_{N=1}^{\infty}\bigl(g_{\mathbf{a}}^{(N)}(\xi_{b})\bigr)^{-1}.

This latter one is an infinite product of values in
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
, whereas (
3.3.31
) arises from infinite products of functions in
\mathbb{B}
which are then evaluated at
\xi_{b}
.

3.3.33.
Galois actions on
g_{\mathbf{a}}
.

For
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
, there are two natural Galois actions on
g_{\mathbf{a}}
. First
\Gal(K/k)
acts on the coefficients of
g_{\mathbf{a}}
, and secondly
\Gal(\mathbf{K}(t,z)/\mathbf{k}(t,z))\cong\Gal(\mathbf{K}/\mathbf{k})
, so there is a complementary
\Gal(\mathbf{K}/\mathbf{k})
-action as well.

Proposition 3.3.34
.

Let
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
. For
b\in(A/fA)^{\times}
the following hold.

(a)

\rho_{b}^{-1}(g_{\mathbf{a}})=g_{b\star\mathbf{a}}
.

(b)

\widetilde{\rho}_{b}(g_{\mathbf{a}})=g_{b\star\mathbf{a}}
.

Proof.

It suffices to consider the case
\mathbf{a}=[a/f]
. For (b), if we let
\{a_{i}\}
,
\{b_{i}\}
be
f
-dual bases of
A/fA
, then (
3.2.2
) and (
3.3.18
) imply

\widetilde{\rho}_{b}(g_{[a/f]})=1-\sum_{i=1}^{\deg f}\mathbf{e}^{*}(a_{i}/f)\mathcal{C}_{ab_{i}}\bigl(t,\mathcal{C}_{b}(t,z)\bigr)=1-\sum_{i=1}^{\deg f}\mathbf{e}^{*}(a_{i}/f)\mathcal{C}_{bab_{i}}(t,z)=g_{[ba/f]},

and the result follows. For (a), we observe that

\rho_{b}^{-1}(g_{[a/f]})=1-\sum_{i=1}^{\deg f}\mathbf{e}^{*}(ba_{i}/f)\mathcal{C}_{ab_{i}}(t,z)=1-\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathbf{e}^{*}(1/\theta^{n+1})\sum_{i=1}^{\deg f}\Res(\theta^{n}a_{i}b/f)\mathcal{C}_{ab_{i}}(t,z),

where the first equality follows from Proposition
3.1.6
and (
3.3.18
) and the second from (
3.3.16
). We then find

\rho_{b}^{-1}(g_{[a/f]})=1-\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathbf{e}^{*}(1/\theta^{n+1})\mathcal{C}_{a\sum_{i=1}^{\deg f}\Res(\theta^{n}a_{i}b/f)b_{i}}(t,z)=1-\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathbf{e}^{*}(1/\theta^{n+1})\mathcal{C}_{a\theta^{n}b}(t,z),

where here the first equality follows from fact that
\Res
takes values in
\mathbb{F}_{q}
and the second from Lemma
3.3.14
