. Then we reverse the calculation, while keeping
b
on the right, and

\rho^{-1}_{b}(g_{[a/f]})=1-\sum_{i=1}^{\deg f}\mathbf{e}^{*}(a_{i}/f)\mathcal{C}_{abb_{i}}(t,z)=g_{b\star[a/f]}

as desired. ∎

3.4.
Anderson Hecke characters

We review the construction Hecke characters of Anderson
[
5
]
, extended by Sinha
[
47
]
,

\chi_{\mathbf{a}}:\mathcal{I}_{K,\mathfrak{f}}\to K^{\times},\quad\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{f},

of conductor dividing the radical
\mathfrak{f}
of
fB
. We first define
\chi_{\mathbf{a}}
for
\mathbf{a}
basic and then extend multiplicatively. We should note that Anderson’s definition is achieved through specializations of his soliton function on the surface
X\times X
, whereas we phrase things equivalently in terms of Coleman functions on
\mathbf{X}
itself.

3.4.1.
Reduction of Coleman functions.

Fix a finite prime
\mathfrak{p}
of
B
that is relatively prime to
f
, let
B_{\mathfrak{p}}
be the localization of
B
at
\mathfrak{p}
, and let
\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}=B_{\mathfrak{p}}/\mathfrak{p}B_{\mathfrak{p}}\cong B/\mathfrak{p}
be its residue field. The extensions of scalars
\mathcal{U}=B_{\mathfrak{p}}\times_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}U
and
\mathcal{X}=B_{\mathfrak{p}}\times_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}X
define curves over
\Spec B_{\mathfrak{p}}
. Since
X
is smooth and proper over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, the resulting base extension
\mathcal{X}\to\Spec B_{\mathfrak{p}}
is also smooth and proper (e.g., see
[
45
]
*Prop. IV.2.9). Now
B_{\mathfrak{p}}[t,z]
is the affine coordinate ring of
\mathcal{U}
, which is irreducible, and its fraction field is
K(t,z)=K(\mathcal{X})
.

As
\mathcal{X}
is a smooth and proper model of
X
over
B_{\mathfrak{p}}
, we have
\mathcal{X}(B_{\mathfrak{p}})=\mathbf{X}(K)
, and so there is a well-defined reduction map
Q\mapsto\overline{Q}:\mathbf{X}(K)\to X(\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}})
modulo
\mathfrak{p}
, which extends to divisors
\Div_{K}(\mathbf{X})\to\Div_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}}(X)
. We also have the natural reduction map

h\mapsto\overline{h}:B_{\mathfrak{p}}[t,z]\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}[t,z].

Because the special fiber of
\mathcal{X}
, which is isomorphic to
\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}\times_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}X
, is irreducible, a function
h\in B_{\mathfrak{p}}[t,z]
vanishes on the special fiber if and only if
\overline{h}=0
. Thus if
\overline{h}\neq 0
, the divisor of
h
as an element of
\Div(\mathcal{X})
is supported on horizontal divisors (e.g., see
[
45
]
*§IV.7). Each of these horizontal divisors intersect the special fiber at some closed point of
\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}\times_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}X
. All of this is compatible with taking divisors of
h
as a rational function on the generic fiber of
\mathcal{X}
, yielding the identity in
\Div_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}}(X)
,

(3.4.2)

\divf(\overline{h})=\overline{\divf(h)},\quad(\forall\,h\in B_{\mathfrak{p}}[t,z],\ \overline{h}\neq 0).

Proposition 3.4.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{p}
be a finite prime of
B
that is relatively prime to
f
. For
[a/f]\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
, we have
g_{[a/f]}\in B_{\mathfrak{p}}[t,z]
and

\overline{g}_{[a/f]}\neq 0.

Moreover,

\divf\bigl(\overline{g}_{[a/f]}\bigr)=\overline{W}_{[a/f]}^{(1)}-\overline{W}_{[a/f]}+\overline{\Xi}_{[a/f]}-I_{[a/f]}.

Proof.

The proposition is trivially true if
[a/f]=[0/f]
, since then
g_{[0/f]}=1
. Assume then that
[a/f]\neq[0/f]
.
That
g_{[a/f]}\in B_{\mathfrak{p}}[t,z]
follows directly from Proposition
3.3.21
. To show that
\overline{g}_{[a/f]}\neq 0
we use (
3.3.29
). In particular, taking
N=0
in (
3.3.29
) and noting that
\Psi_{0}(x)=x
, it follows that for
b\in A
with
(b,f)=1
and
\deg b<\deg f
, we have
g_{[a/f]}^{(1)}(\xi_{b})=1+(ab\bmod f)/f
.
Now
\xi_{b}=(\theta,C_{b}(\zeta))
has coordinates in
B_{\mathfrak{p}}
, so

\overline{g}_{[a/f]}^{(1)}(\overline{\xi}_{b})=\overline{g_{[a/f]}^{(1)}(\xi_{b})}\equiv 1+\frac{ab\bmod f}{f}\pmod{\mathfrak{p}}.

We claim that there exists a choice of
b
so that this expression is non-zero modulo
\mathfrak{p}
. Assuming this claim, we conclude that
\overline{g}_{[a/f]}^{(1)}\neq 0
, whence
\overline{g}_{[a/f]}\neq 0
, and then the statement on divisors then follows from (
3.4.2
).
To address the claim, if
f=cf^{\prime}
,
a=ca^{\prime}
, with
(a^{\prime},f^{\prime})=1
and
c\in A_{+}
, then
\{ab\bmod f\mid b\in(A/fA)^{\times}\}=\{cb^{\prime}\bmod f\mid b^{\prime}\in(A/f^{\prime}A)^{\times}\}
. Moreover, if we let
\wp\in A_{+}
be the unique monic prime of
A
contained in
\mathfrak{p}
, then we wish to find
b^{\prime}\in(A/f^{\prime}A)^{\times}
such that
f+(cb^{\prime}\bmod f)\not\equiv 0\pmod{\wp}
. Since
\wp\nmid c
and
\wp\nmid f
, taking
b^{\prime}=\wp
does the trick.
∎

3.4.4.
Anderson’s Hecke character.

Letting
[a/f]\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
, for
\mathfrak{p}
a finite prime of
B
relatively prime to
f
we obtain
\overline{g}_{[a/f]}\in\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}[t,z]
as in Proposition
3.4.3
. Letting
\ell=[\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}:\mathbb{F}_{q}]
,

(3.4.5)

G_{[a/f],\mathfrak{p}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\prod_{i=0}^{\ell-1}\overline{g}_{[a/f]}^{(i)}\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[t,z]=\mathbf{B},

since
\Gal(\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}(t,z)/\mathbb{F}_{q}(t,z))\cong\Gal(\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}/\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Thus we can define

(3.4.6)

\chi_{[a/f]}(\mathfrak{p})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=G_{[a/f],\mathfrak{p}}(\xi)=\prod_{i=0}^{\ell-1}\overline{g}_{[a/f]}^{(i)}(\xi)\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[\theta,\zeta]=B,

where we recall that
\xi=\xi_{1}=(\theta,\zeta)\in\mathbf{X}(K)
. In the following theorem, Anderson
[
5
]
proved that
\chi_{[a/f]}
is an algebraic Hecke character. Sinha
[
46
,
47
]
then investigated
\chi_{\mathbf{a}}
for general parameters. For a version of Anderson’s proof relying on the properties of Coleman functions above, see
[
20
]
*Thm. 4.2.2.2.

Theorem 3.4.7

(Anderson
[
5
]
*§3, Sinha
[
47
]
*§2.2)
.

For
[a/f]\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
, the assignment
\mathfrak{p}\mapsto\chi_{[a/f]}(\mathfrak{p})
from (
3.4.6
) extends multiplicatively to a function
\chi_{[a/f]}:\mathcal{I}_{K,\mathfrak{f}}\to K^{\times}
such that the following hold.

(a)

Letting

\Theta_{[a/f]}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in A\\
\deg b<\deg f\\
(b,f)=1\end{subarray}}\biggl\langle\frac{ba}{f}\biggr\rangle\rho_{b}^{-1}\quad\in\mathbb{Z}[\Gal(K/k)],

for any finite prime
\mathfrak{p}
of
B
we have the equality of ideals in
B
,

\chi_{[a/f]}(\mathfrak{p})\cdot B=\mathfrak{p}^{\Theta_{[a/f]}}.

(b)

For a finite prime
\mathfrak{p}
of
B
and
b\in(A/fA)^{\times}
, we have

\bigl|\rho_{b}\bigl(\chi_{[a/f]}(\mathfrak{p})\bigr)\bigr|_{\infty}={\lvert\widetilde{\theta}\rvert}_{\infty}^{\ell},\quad\ell=[\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}:\mathbb{F}_{q}].

(c)

Moreover,
\chi_{[a/f]}:\mathcal{I}_{K,\mathfrak{f}}\to K^{\times}
is an algebraic Hecke character of conductor dividing
\mathfrak{f}
and infinity type
\Theta_{[a/f]}
. In particular, for
\alpha\in K^{\times}
with
\alpha\equiv 1\pmod{\mathfrak{f}}
,

\chi_{[a/f]}(\alpha B)=\alpha^{\Theta_{[a/f]}}.

Remark 3.4.8
.

(i) Comparing notation we see that
\Theta_{[1/f]}
here is the same as
\Theta_{f}(1)^{*}
in
[
5
]
. (ii) Sinha
[
46
]
*Prop. 3.1 has proved a refined version of part (c) for all
\alpha\in K^{\times}
.

Lemma 3.4.9
.

Let
a
,
b\in A
with
(b,f)=1
. For a finite prime
\mathfrak{p}
of
B
prime to
f
,

\chi_{[ba/p]}(\mathfrak{p})=\rho_{b}\bigl(\chi_{[a/f]}(\mathfrak{p})\bigr).

Proof.

By (
3.3.18
) and (
3.4.6
), we see that for an
f
-dual family
\{a_{i}\}
,
\{b_{j}\}
,

\chi_{[ab/f]}(\mathfrak{p})=\prod_{i=1}^{\ell-1}\biggl(1-\sum_{j=1}^{\deg f}\overline{\mathbf{e}^{*}(a_{j}/f)}^{\,q^{i}}C_{ab_{j}}(C_{b}(\zeta))\biggr)=G_{[a/f],\mathfrak{p}}(\xi_{b})=\rho_{b}\bigl(G_{[a/f],\mathfrak{p}}(\xi)\bigr),

and the result follows.
∎

3.4.10.
General Anderson Hecke characters.

As investigated by Sinha
[
47
]
, for
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
we define

\chi_{\mathbf{a}}:\mathcal{I}_{K,\mathfrak{f}}\to K^{\times}
, by setting

\chi_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathfrak{b})=\prod_{a}\chi_{[a/f]}(\mathfrak{b})^{m_{a}},\quad\mathbf{a}=\sum_{a}m_{a}[a/f].

Then
\chi_{\mathbf{a}}
is an algebraic Hecke character of conductor dividing
\mathfrak{f}
with infinity type
\Theta_{\mathbf{a}}=\sum_{a}m_{a}\Theta_{[a/f]}
. For
\rho_{b}\in\Gal(K/k)
, Lemma
3.4.9
shows that

\chi_{\mathbf{a}}^{\rho_{b}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\rho_{b}\circ\chi_{\mathbf{a}}=\chi_{b\star\mathbf{a}}

is a Hecke character. The character
\chi_{\mathbf{a}}
is also Galois symmetric.

Lemma 3.4.11

(Sinha
[
46
]
*§3.2)
.

Let
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
. For
\mathfrak{b}\in\mathcal{I}_{K,\mathfrak{f}}
and
\rho\in\Gal(K/k)
,

\chi_{\mathbf{a}}^{\rho}(\mathfrak{b})=\chi_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathfrak{b}^{\rho}).

Proof.

It suffices to prove this identity for
\mathbf{a}=[a/f]
, and
\mathfrak{b}=\mathfrak{p}
a maximal ideal of
B
. We claim that
(\rho^{-1}\circ\chi_{[a/f]}\circ\rho)(\mathfrak{p})=\chi_{[a/f]}(\mathfrak{p})
. Consider the following commutative diagram:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{\mathfrak{p}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B/\mathfrak{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}_{\infty},}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{\mathfrak{p}^{\rho}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B/\mathfrak{p}^{\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x\,\mapsto\,\overline{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x\,\mapsto\,\widetilde{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

where
i
is a chosen embedding of
\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}
in
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
and
j
is the embedding of
\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}^{\rho}}
then induced by
\rho^{-1}
.
We observe that for
x\in B_{\mathfrak{p}}
,

i(\overline{x})=j(\widetilde{\rho(x)}).

Let
\ell=[\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}:\mathbb{F}_{q}]=[\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}^{\rho}}:\mathbb{F}_{q}]
. Suppose that
\rho=\rho_{b}
for
b\in(A/fA)^{\times}
, and take
c\in A
such that
bc\equiv 1\pmod{f}
. Then (
3.4.6
) implies

(\rho^{-1}\circ\chi_{[a/f]}\circ\rho)(\mathfrak{p})=(\rho_{c}\circ\chi_{[a/f]})(\mathfrak{p}^{\rho_{b}})=\rho_{c}\biggl(\prod_{m=0}^{\ell-1}\widetilde{g}_{[a/f]}^{(m)}(\xi_{1})\biggr)=\prod_{m=0}^{\ell-1}\widetilde{g}_{[a/f]}^{(m)}(\xi_{c}),

where
\widetilde{g}_{[a/f]}
is the reduction of
g_{[a/f]}
modulo
\mathfrak{p}^{\rho_{b}}
. Taking
\{a_{n}\}
,
\{b_{n}\}
to be an
f
-dual family, continuing this calculation, and using (
3.3.18
) with the diagram above, yields

\displaystyle(\rho^{-1}\circ\chi_{[a/f]}\circ\rho)(\mathfrak{p})

\displaystyle=\prod_{m=0}^{\ell-1}\biggl(1-\sum_{n=1}^{\deg f}j\Bigl(\widetilde{\mathbf{e}^{*}(a_{n}/f)}\Bigr)^{q^{m}}\rho_{c}(\mathbf{e}(ab_{n}/f))\biggr)

\displaystyle=\prod_{m=0}^{\ell-1}\biggl(1-\sum_{n=1}^{\deg f}i\bigl(\overline{\rho_{c}(\mathbf{e}^{*}(a_{n}/f))}\bigr)^{q^{m}}\mathbf{e}(ab_{n}c/f)\biggr),

where now applying Proposition
3.1.6
,

\displaystyle=\prod_{m=0}^{\ell-1}\biggl(1-\sum_{n=1}^{\deg f}i\bigl(\overline{\mathbf{e}^{\star}(a_{n}b/f)}\bigr)^{q^{m}}\mathbf{e}(ab_{n}c/f)\biggr).

Since
\{a_{n}b\}
,
\{b_{n}c\}
is also an
f
-dual family, we finally obtain

(\rho^{-1}\circ\chi_{[a/f]}\circ\rho)(\mathfrak{p})=\prod_{m=0}^{\ell-1}\biggl(1-\sum_{n=1}^{\deg f}i\bigl(\overline{\mathbf{e}^{*}(a_{n}/f)}\bigr)^{q^{m}}\mathbf{e}(ab_{n}/f)\biggr)^{-1}=\chi_{[a/f]}(\mathfrak{p}),

as desired.
∎

4.
Sinha modules

4.1.
Sinha
t
-comotives and
t
-modules

As in §
3
, fix
f\in A_{+}
,
\deg f\geqslant 1
, and pick effective
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
with
\deg\mathbf{a}>0
. Let
\mathbb{K}
be an algebraically closed intermediate field
k\subseteq\mathbb{K}\subseteq\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
. As in §
3.2
, we take
\mathbf{U}=\mathbb{K}\times_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}U
and
\mathbf{X}=\mathbb{K}\times_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}X
. We then set

(4.1.1)

H_{\mathbf{a}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=H^{0}(\mathbf{U},\mathcal{O}_{\mathbf{X}}(-W_{\mathbf{a}}^{(1)})),

which is a non-zero ideal of
\mathbb{K}[t,z]
, the affine coordinate ring of
\mathbf{U}
. If we wish to indicate the dependence on
\mathbb{K}
, we will write
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{K})
.
Following
[
1
]
*§6.4, we make
H_{\mathbf{a}}
into a left
\mathbb{K}[t,\sigma]
-module using the inherent left
\mathbb{K}[t]
-module structure and setting

\sigma h\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=g_{\mathbf{a}}h^{(-1)},\quad h\in H_{\mathbf{a}}.

Then
H_{\mathbf{a}}
is a coabelian
t
-comotive by
[
1
]
*§6.4.2. The following lemma is then a consequence of
[
11
]
*Prop. 4.2.3, but we provide proofs of (a)–(c) in this case for completeness. That
H_{\mathbf{a}}
has a finite
\mathbb{K}[\sigma]
-basis will be revisited in §
4.3
.

Lemma 4.1.2
.

The
t
-comotive
H_{\mathbf{a}}
is coabelian. Moreover, the following hold.

(a)

The rank of
H_{\mathbf{a}}
is
r=[K:k]=\#(A/fA)^{\times}
.

(b)

The dimension of
H_{\mathbf{a}}
is
d=\deg(\Xi_{\mathbf{a}})=\deg(\mathbf{a})\cdot r/(q-1)
.

(c)

We have
\sigma H_{\mathbf{a}}=H^{0}(\mathbf{U},\mathcal{O}_{\mathbf{X}}(-W_{\mathbf{a}}^{(1)}-\Xi_{\mathbf{a}}))
.

Proof.

The ring
\mathbb{K}[t,z]
is the extension of scalars of
\mathbf{B}=\mathbb{F}_{q}[t,z]
to
\mathbb{K}
. Since
U/\mathbb{F}_{q}
is smooth and absolutely irreducible,
\mathbb{K}[t,z]
is also a Dedekind domain, and
[\mathbb{K}[t,z]:\mathbb{K}[t]]=[\mathbf{B}:\mathbf{A}]=r
. As an ideal of
\mathbb{K}[t,z]
,
H_{\mathbf{a}}
also has rank
r
over
\mathbb{K}[t]
, which establishes (a).

We next prove (c). We see from (
3.3.23
) and (
4.1.1
) that
\sigma H_{\mathbf{a}}\subseteq H^{0}(\mathbf{U},\mathcal{O}_{\mathbf{X}}(-W_{\mathbf{a}}^{(1)}-\Xi_{\mathbf{a}}))
. For the opposite containment, suppose that
\beta\in\mathbb{K}[t,z]
satisfies

\divf(\beta)|_{\mathbf{U}}\geqslant W_{\mathbf{a}}^{(1)}+\Xi_{\mathbf{a}}
. Then letting
\alpha=\beta^{(1)}/g_{\mathbf{a}}^{(1)}
, we find that
\divf(\alpha)|_{\mathbf{U}}\geqslant W_{\mathbf{a}}^{(1)}
. Thus
\alpha\in H_{\mathbf{a}}
, and
\sigma\alpha=\beta
, which proves (c) (see also
[
1
]
*§6.4.2).
For (b), we have

\rank_{\mathbb{K}[\sigma]}(H_{\mathbf{a}})=\dim_{\mathbb{K}}\biggl(\frac{H_{\mathbf{a}}}{\sigma H_{\mathbf{a}}}\biggr)=\dim_{\mathbb{K}}\left(\frac{H^{0}(\mathbf{U},\mathcal{O}_{\mathbf{X}}(-W_{\mathbf{a}}^{(1)}))}{H^{0}(\mathbf{U},\mathcal{O}_{\mathbf{X}}(-W_{\mathbf{a}}^{(1)}-\Xi_{\mathbf{a}}))}\right).

Through a short calculation the right-hand side is
\deg(\Xi_{\mathbf{a}})
by
[
11
]
*Lem. 4.2.4.
∎

4.1.3.
Definition of Sinha modules.

As in §
2.2
, we use the
t
-comotive
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{K})
to define the
Sinha module

E_{\mathbf{a}}:\mathbf{A}\to\Mat_{d}(\mathbb{K}[\tau]),

where
d=\deg(\Xi_{\mathbf{a}})
as in Lemma
4.1.2
. The Sinha module
E_{\mathbf{a}}
is an abelian Anderson
t
-module, and since we can take
\mathbb{K}=\overline{k}
, we can find a model for
E_{\mathbf{a}}
defined over
\overline{k}
. A few remarks are in order.

•

Since multiplication by
b\in\mathbf{B}
on
H_{\mathbf{a}}
is
\mathbb{K}[t,\sigma]
-linear, we obtain a natural
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-algebra homomorphism
\mathbf{B}\to\End_{\mathbb{K}[t,\sigma]}(H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{K}))
. Through functoriality there is a natural extension

E_{\mathbf{a}}:\mathbf{B}\to\Mat_{d}(\mathbb{K}[\tau]).

•

The model for
E_{\mathbf{a}}
depends on the choice of
\mathbb{K}[\sigma]
-basis for
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{K})
as in (
2.2.9
)–(
2.2.10
). We will explore different models in §
4.4
.

•

The
t
-comotives
H_{\mathbf{a}}
were originally defined in
[
1
]
*§6.4, based on a similar
t
-motivic construction of Sinha
[
46
,
48
]
. Sinha also investigated the
t
-modules associated to these
t
-motives and determined a number of their properties, including the calculation of their period lattices in terms of special
\Gamma
-values. The
t
-modules
E_{\mathbf{a}}
defined here are not strictly speaking the ones that he worked with but are isogenous to them. Sinha modules are occasionally referred to in terms of “soliton
t
-modules” or “soliton
t
-motives” (e.g., see
[
11
]
*§1.5.3
[
12
]
*§4
[
57
]
*§5.3), based as they are on Anderson’s soliton functions
[
5
]
.

•

If we write
\mathbf{a}=\sum m_{a}[a/f]
, then
g_{\mathbf{a}}
,
H_{\mathbf{a}}
, and
E_{\mathbf{a}}
do not depend on
m_{0}
, and we will without loss of generality assume that
m_{0}=0
.

4.1.4.
Hyperderivatives with respect to
t
.

We recall the sequence of
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
-linear operators
\partial_{t}^{j}:\mathbb{C}_{\infty}[t]\to\mathbb{C}_{\infty}[t]
,
j\geqslant 0
, defined by
\partial_{t}^{j}(t^{m})=\binom{m}{j}t^{m-j}
. These operators extend uniquely to both
{\mathbb{C}_{\infty}[[t]]}
and
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}(t)^{\mathrm{sep}}
(e.g., see
[
19
]
*§4
[
34
]

[
39
]
*§2.4). Then each
\partial_{t}^{j}
leaves
\mathbb{T}
and
\mathbb{T}_{\theta}
invariant. Since
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}(\mathbf{X})=\mathbb{C}_{\infty}(t,z)
is separable over
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}(t)
, we can uniquely define
\partial_{t}^{j}(z)
for each
j\geqslant 0
. Moreover, by
[
19
]
*Thm. 5,

\partial_{t}^{j}:\mathbb{C}_{\infty}(\mathbf{X})\to\mathbb{C}_{\infty}(\mathbf{X}),\quad j\geqslant 0.

In this way we can extend
\partial_{t}^{j}
to
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
-linear operators on the fraction fields of
\mathbb{B}=\mathbb{T}[z]
and
\mathbb{B}_{\theta}=\mathbb{T}_{\theta}[z]
. To be a bit more precise, we note that
\partial_{t}^{j}(\mathcal{C}_{f}(t,z))=0
, and if we write

\mathcal{C}_{f}(t,z)=f(t)z+\sum_{i=1}^{\deg f}a_{i}(t)z^{q^{i}},\quad a_{i}(t)\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[t],

then the product rule
[
34
]
*§2.2
[
39
]
*Prop. 2.14(a) implies that

\partial_{t}^{j}\bigl(\mathcal{C}_{f}(t,z)\bigr)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{j}\partial_{t}^{\ell}(f(t))\partial_{t}^{j-\ell}(z)+\sum_{i=1}^{\deg f}\sum_{\ell=0}^{j}\partial_{t}^{\ell}(a_{i}(t))\partial_{t}^{j-\ell}\bigl(z^{q^{i}}\bigr)=0.

By the
p
-th power rule
[
34
]
*§2.2
[
39
]
*Prop. 2.14(b), for
1\leqslant i\leqslant\deg f
the
\partial_{t}^{j-\ell}(z^{q^{i}})
terms are either
0
or a power of
\partial_{t}^{k}(z)
with
k\leqslant(j-\ell)/q
. In particular the only occurrence of
\partial_{t}^{j}(z)
in the above expression is in the first sum with
\ell=0
, and all other derivatives of
z
occur with order strictly less than
j
. Thus an induction argument implies that
\partial_{t}^{j}(z)\in\mathbb{F}_{q}(t)[z]=\mathbf{K}
, and more generally

\partial_{t}^{j}:\mathbf{k}\otimes_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbb{B}\to\mathbf{k}\otimes_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbb{B},\quad\partial_{t}^{j}:\mathbf{k}\otimes_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbb{B}_{\theta}\to\mathbf{k}\otimes_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbb{B}_{\theta},\quad j\geqslant 0.

We think of
\mathbf{k}\otimes_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbb{B}
,
\mathbf{k}\otimes_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbb{B}_{\theta}
as subrings of the fraction fields of
\mathbb{B}
,
\mathbb{B}_{\theta}
. For any
g\in\mathbf{k}\otimes_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbb{B}_{\theta}
, we can evaluate
\partial_{t}^{j}(g)
at any of the points
\xi_{a}\in\mathbf{X}(\mathbb{C}_{\infty})
for
a\in(A/fA)^{\times}
.

4.1.5.
Expansions at
\xi_{a}
.

Let
a\in(A/fA)^{\times}
, and let
h\in\mathbb{C}_{\infty}(\mathbf{X})
be regular at
\xi_{a}
. Since
t-\theta
is a uniformizer at
\xi_{a}
by (
3.2.5
), in the completion of the local ring at
\xi_{a}
we can expand
h
as power series,

h=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\partial_{t}^{j}(h)|_{\xi_{a}}\cdot(t-\theta)^{j}.

In particular
\partial_{t}^{j}(h)|_{\xi_{a}}=0
for
0\leqslant j<\ord_{\xi_{a}}(h)
, and
\partial_{t}^{j}(h)|_{\xi_{a}}\neq 0
for
j=\ord_{\xi_{a}}(h)
.

4.1.6.
Normalized
\mathbb{K}[\sigma]
-bases.

We make the following definitions (cf. Sinha
[
48
]
*§5.2.4). For a divisor
D\in\Div(\mathbf{X})
and
P\in\mathbf{X}(\mathbb{K})
, we let
\mult_{P}(D)\in\mathbb{Z}
be the multiplicity of
P
occurring in
D
. We then define the index sets

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{a}}=\left\{(a,j)\in A\times\mathbb{Z}\biggm|\begin{gathered}\deg a<\deg f,\ (a,f)=1,\ \mult_{\xi_{a}}(\Xi_{\mathbf{a}})\neq 0,\\
0\leqslant j\leqslant\mult_{\xi_{a}}(\Xi_{\mathbf{a}})-1\end{gathered}\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{a}}=\{a\in A\mid\deg a<\deg f,\ (a,f)=1,\ \mult_{\xi_{a}}(\Xi_{\mathbf{a}})\neq 0\}.

We observe that
\#\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{a}}=\deg(\Xi_{\mathbf{a}})=d
as in Lemma
4.1.2
(b), and
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{a}}
is simply the projection of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{a}}
to
A
. Suppose that
\{\widetilde{h}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{h}_{d}\}
is a
\mathbb{K}[\sigma]
-basis of
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{K})
. By Lemma
4.1.2
(c) and
[
11
]
*Lem. 4.2.4, we can take a
\mathbb{K}
-linear change of variables on
\{\widetilde{h}_{i}\}
to obtain a new
\mathbb{K}[\sigma]
-basis
\{h_{(a,j)}\mid(a,j)\in S_{\mathbf{a}}\}
so that

(4.1.7)

h_{(a,j)}\in H^{0}(\mathbf{U},\mathcal{O}_{\mathbf{X}}(-W_{\mathbf{a}}^{(1)}-\Xi_{\mathbf{a}}+(j+1)\xi_{a}))\setminus H^{0}(\mathbf{U},\mathcal{O}_{\mathbf{X}}(-W_{\mathbf{a}}^{(1)}-\Xi_{\mathbf{a}}+j\xi_{a})),

and, taking

\ell_{a}=\mult_{\xi_{a}}(\Xi_{\mathbf{a}})-1,

we have

(4.1.8)

\frac{h_{(a,j)}}{(t-\theta)^{\ell_{a}-j}}\bigg|_{\xi_{a}}=1.

We note that we can do this since
t-\theta
is a uniformizer at
\xi_{a}
, and

\ord_{\xi_{a}}(h_{(a,j)})=\mult_{\xi_{a}}(W_{\mathbf{a}}^{(1)}+\Xi_{\mathbf{a}})-j-1=\ell_{a}-j,

this last equality following because
W_{\mathbf{a}}^{(1)}
and
\Xi_{\mathbf{a}}
have disjoint supports.
Suppose further that for
a\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{a}}
and
0\leqslant j,k\leqslant\ell_{a}
, we have

(4.1.9)

\partial_{t}^{k}\bigl(h_{(a,j)}\bigr)\big|_{\xi_{a}}=\begin{cases}1&\textup{if $k=\ell_{a}-j$,}\\
0&\textup{if $k\neq\ell_{a}-j$.}\end{cases}

In this case, we call a
\mathbb{K}[t]
-basis
\{h_{(a,j)}\mid(a,j)\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{a}}\}
for
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{K})
a
normalized basis
.

4.1.10.
Constructing normalized bases.

As observed in §
4.1
, we can find a
\mathbb{K}[t]
-basis
\{h_{(a,j)}\mid(a,j)\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{a}}\}
satisfying (
4.1.7
)–(
4.1.8
), but condition (
4.1.9
) is not automatic. However, (
4.1.7
)–(
4.1.8
) do imply, as in §
4.1
, that
for
a\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{a}}
and
0\leqslant j,k\leqslant\ell_{a}
,

(4.1.11)

\partial_{t}^{k}\bigl(h_{(a,j)}\bigr)\big|_{\xi_{a}}=\begin{cases}1&\textup{if $k=\ell_{a}-j$,}\\
0&\textup{if $0\leqslant k<\ell_{a}-j$.}\end{cases}

Thus for each
a\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{a}}
the Wronskian matrix

M_{a}=\left.\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{t}^{\ell_{a}}(h_{(a,0)})&\cdots&\partial_{t}^{\ell_{a}}(h_{(a,\ell_{a})})\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
\partial_{t}^{1}(h_{(a,0)})&\cdots&\partial_{t}^{1}(h_{(a,\ell_{a})})\\
h_{(a,0)}&\cdots&h_{(a,\ell_{a})}\end{pmatrix}\right|_{\xi_{a}}=\begin{pmatrix}1&\partial_{t}^{\ell_{a}}(h_{(a,1)})|_{\xi_{a}}&\cdots&\partial_{t}^{\ell_{a}}(h_{(a,\ell_{a})})|_{\xi_{a}}\\
&1&\ddots&\vdots\\
&&1&\partial_{t}^{1}(h_{(a,\ell_{a})})|_{\xi_{a}}\\
&&&1\end{pmatrix}

is upper triangular with
1
’s along the diagonal. Notably,
M_{a}^{-1}
has the same shape, and

M_{a},\ M_{a}^{-1}\in\GL_{\ell_{a}+1}\bigl(\mathbb{F}_{q}\bigl[\partial_{t}^{k}\bigl(h_{(a,j)}\bigr)\big|_{\xi_{a}}:0\leqslant j\leqslant\ell_{a},\ \ell_{a}-j<k\leqslant\ell_{a}\bigr]\bigr).

Now for each
a\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{a}}
let

\bigl(h^{\prime}_{(a,0)},\ldots,h^{\prime}_{(a,\ell_{a})}\bigr)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\bigl(h_{(a,0)},\dots,h_{(a,\ell_{a})}\bigr)M_{a}^{-1}.

We claim that
\{h^{\prime}_{(a,j)}\mid(a,j)\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{a}}\}
is a normalized
\mathbb{K}[\sigma]
-basis for
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{K})
. Indeed for each
a\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{a}}
, because
M_{a}^{-1}
is upper triangular with
1
’s on the diagonal,
\{h^{\prime}_{(a,j)}\}
satisfies (
4.1.7
)–(
4.1.8
). Also, for
0\leqslant j\leqslant\ell_{a}
,

\bigl(\partial_{t}^{j}(h^{\prime}_{(a,0)}),\ldots,\partial_{t}(h^{\prime}_{(a,\ell_{a})})\bigr)\big|_{\xi_{a}}=\bigl(\partial_{t}^{j}(h_{(a,0)},\ldots,\partial_{t}^{j}(h_{(a,\ell_{a})})\bigr)\big|_{\xi_{a}}\cdot M_{a}^{-1}.

Letting
M_{a}^{\prime}
be the Wronskian matrix for
h^{\prime}_{(a,0},\dots,h^{\prime}_{(a,\ell_{a})}
as above,

M_{a}^{\prime}=M_{a}\cdot M_{a}^{-1}=\mathrm{I}.

Thus
\{h^{\prime}_{(a,j)}\mid(a,j)\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{a}}\}
satisfies (
4.1.9
) and forms a normalized
\mathbb{K}[\sigma]
-basis of
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{K})
.

Lemma 4.1.12
.

Fix a normalized
\mathbb{K}[\sigma]
-basis
\{h_{(a,j)}\}
for
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{K})
. For
h\in H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{C}_{\infty})
,

h=\sum_{(a,j)\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{a}}}\partial_{t}^{\ell_{a}-j}(h)|_{\xi_{a}}\cdot h_{(a,j)}\pmod{\sigma H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{C}_{\infty})}.

Proof.

Pick
c_{(a,j)}\in\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
so that
h=h^{\prime}+\sum_{(a,j)\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{a}}}c_{(a,j)}\cdot h_{(a,j)}
, with
h^{\prime}\in\sigma H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{C}_{\infty})
. Then for
k\geqslant 0
,

\partial_{t}^{k}(h)=\partial_{t}^{k}(h^{\prime})+\sum_{a\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{a}}}\sum_{j=0}^{\ell_{a}}c_{(a,j)}\cdot\partial_{t}^{k}\bigl(h_{(a,j)}\bigr).

For
b\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{a}}
, we note that (i)
\ord_{\xi_{b}}(h^{\prime})>\ell_{b}
; (ii)
\ord_{\xi_{b}}(h_{(a,j)})>\ell_{b}
when
a\neq b
; and as before (iii)
\ord_{\xi_{b}}(h_{(b,j)})=\ell_{b}-j
. Thus if
k\leqslant\ell_{b}
, then replacing
k
with
\ell_{b}-k
and evaluating at
\xi_{b}
, we have

\partial_{t}^{\ell_{b}-k}(h)|_{\xi_{b}}=\sum_{j=0}^{\ell_{b}}c_{(b,j)}\cdot\partial_{t}^{\ell_{b}-k}\bigl(h_{(b,j)}\bigr)\big|_{\xi_{b}}=c_{(b,k)},

where the last equality follows from (
4.1.9
).
∎

4.1.13.
The map
\mathcal{E}_{0}
for
H_{\mathbf{a}}
.

Continuing with the notation above, assume we have chosen a normalized
\mathbb{K}[\sigma]
-basis
\{h_{(a,j)}\}
for
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{K})
. We define

\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{0}:H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{C}_{\infty})\to\mathbb{C}_{\infty}^{d}

by

\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{0}(h)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{pmatrix}\vdots\\
\partial_{t}^{\ell_{a}-j}(h)|_{\xi_{a}}\\
\vdots\end{pmatrix}_{(a,j)\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{a}}},

which is
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
-linear and
whose kernel is precisely
\sigma H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{C}_{\infty})
. For a
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}[t]
-basis
\{v_{1},\dots,v_{r}\}
of
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{C}_{\infty})
, we
let
\widetilde{\iota}:\Mat_{1\times r}(\mathbb{C}_{\infty}[t])\to H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{C}_{\infty})
be the evident
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}[t]
-isomorphism. We then have a commutative diagram, where the vertical isomorphism is the
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}[\sigma]
-linear map induced by the basis
\{h_{(a,j)}\}
and
\iota
is then the map induced by
\widetilde{\iota}
:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Mat_{1\times r}(\mathbb{C}_{\infty}[t])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Mat_{1\times d}(\mathbb{C}_{\infty}[\sigma])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}_{\infty}^{d}.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Mat_{1\times r}(\mathbb{C}_{\infty}[t])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{C}_{\infty})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\iota}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{0}}

In this way
\mathcal{E}_{0}:\Mat_{1\times r}(\mathbb{T}_{\theta})\to\mathbb{C}_{\infty}^{d}
is defined continuously via
\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{0}\circ\widetilde{\iota}=\varepsilon_{0}\circ\iota
. Therefore, for
(\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{r})\in\Mat_{1\times r}(\mathbb{T}_{\theta})
, if we let
\beta=\beta_{1}v_{1}+\dots+\beta_{r}v_{r}
, then

(4.1.14)

\mathcal{E}_{0}(\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{r})=\begin{pmatrix}\vdots\\
\partial_{t}^{\ell_{a}-j}(\beta)|_{\xi_{a}}\\
\vdots\end{pmatrix}_{(a,j)\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{a}}}.

In particular, in the case that
\mult_{\xi_{a}}(\Xi_{\mathbf{a}})=1
for all
a\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{a}}
, e.g., when
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
is basic, things are even simpler. In this case
\ell_{a}=0
for all
a\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{a}}
, and we obtain

(4.1.15)

\mathcal{E}_{0}(\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{r})=\begin{pmatrix}\vdots\\
\beta(\xi_{a})\\
\vdots\end{pmatrix}_{a\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{a}}}.

Lemma 4.1.16
.

Fix a normalized
\mathbb{K}[\sigma]
-basis of
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{K})
as above. For
b\in\mathbf{B}
, the matrix
\mathrm{d}E_{\mathbf{a},b}
is a block diagonal matrix, where for each
a\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{a}}
, there is an
(\ell_{a}+1)\times(\ell_{a}+1)
upper-triangular block in
\mathrm{d}E_{\mathbf{a},b}
of the form

\begin{pmatrix}b(\xi_{a})&\partial_{t}^{1}(b)|_{\xi_{a}}&\cdots&\partial_{t}^{\ell_{a}}(b)|_{\xi_{a}}\\
&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
&&b(\xi_{a})&\partial_{t}^{1}(b)|_{\xi_{a}}\\
&&&b(\xi_{a})\end{pmatrix}=\rho_{a}\begin{pmatrix}b(\xi)&\partial_{t}^{1}(b)|_{\xi}&\cdots&\partial_{t}^{\ell_{a}}(b)|_{\xi}\\
&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
&&b(\xi)&\partial_{t}^{1}(b)|_{\xi}\\
&&&b(\xi)\end{pmatrix}.

Proof.

As in (
2.2.8
), the induced map

\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{0}:\frac{H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{C}_{\infty})}{\sigma H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{C}_{\infty})}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Lie(E_{\mathbf{a}})(\mathbb{C}_{\infty})

is an isomorphism of
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}[t]
-modules. And in fact it is an isomorphism of
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}[t,z]
-modules. Now for
b\in\mathbf{B}\subseteq\mathbb{C}_{\infty}[t,z]
and
h\in H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{C}_{\infty})
, we see from the product rule,

\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{t}^{\ell_{a}}(bh)|_{\xi_{a}}\\
\vdots\\
\partial_{t}^{1}(bh)|_{\xi_{a}}\\
(bh)|_{\xi_{a}}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}b(\xi_{a})&\partial_{t}^{1}(b)|_{\xi_{a}}&\cdots&\partial_{t}^{\ell_{a}}(b)|_{\xi_{a}}\\
&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
&&b(\xi_{a})&\partial_{t}^{1}(b)|_{\xi_{a}}\\
&&&b(\xi_{a})\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{t}^{\ell_{a}}(h)|_{\xi_{a}}\\
\vdots\\
\partial_{t}^{1}(h)|_{\xi_{a}}\\
(h)|_{\xi_{a}}\end{pmatrix}.

The conclusion then follows from Lemma
4.1.12
(and its proof) with respect to our normalized
\mathbb{K}[\sigma]
-basis.
∎

Remark 4.1.17
.

In the language of
[
11
]
*§4.2,
H_{\mathbf{a}}
is a CM
t
-comotive with generalized CM type
(\mathbf{K},\Xi_{\mathbf{a}})
. In this way
E_{\mathbf{a}}
is a
t
-module with CM by
\mathbf{B}
and generalized CM type
(\mathbf{K},\Xi_{\mathbf{a}})
as in
[
11
]
*§8.

4.2.
Analytic properties of Sinha modules

Fix effective
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
with
\deg\mathbf{a}>0
as in §
4.1
. It was shown in
[
1
]
*Lem. 6.4.3 that
H_{\mathbf{a}}
is rigid analytically trivial, and as such the
t
-module
E_{\mathbf{a}}
is uniformizable.
This was also demonstrated in
[
11
]
*§4.3 using Hilbert-Blumenthal
t
-modules. As we will need more refined information about the rigid analytic trivialization for
H_{\mathbf{a}}
, we present a modified version of the constructions of
[
1
]
*§6.4 here. One should also compare to Wei
[
57
]
*§5.3.

4.2.1.
Precursor to rigid analytic trivialization.

We begin by following the setup of
[
1
]
*Lem. 6.4.3 but pay attention when possible to fields of definition. The set
\{1,z,\dots,z^{r-1}\}
is a
K[t]
-basis of
K[t,z]
. Since
g_{\mathbf{a}}\in K[t,z]
by Proposition
3.3.21
(as
\mathbf{a}
is effective), we can define
\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}\in\Mat_{r}(K[t])
as the matrix representing multiplication by
g_{\mathbf{a}}
with respect to
\{1,z,\dots,z^{r-1}\}
. In other words, letting
\mathbf{z}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(1,z,\dots,z^{r-1})^{{\mathsf{T}}}
,

(4.2.2)

g_{\mathbf{a}}\cdot\mathbf{z}\equiv\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}\cdot\mathbf{z}\pmod{\mathcal{D}_{f}(t,z)}.

Likewise, since
\{1,z,\dots,z^{r-1}\}
is an
\mathbb{F}_{q}[t]
-basis of
\mathbf{B}=\mathbb{F}_{q}[t,z]
, we can define
Z(t)\in\Mat_{r}(\mathbb{F}_{q}[t])
to represent multiplication by
z
on
\mathbf{B}
with respect to this basis,

(4.2.3)

z\cdot\mathbf{z}\equiv Z(t)\cdot\mathbf{z}\pmod{\mathcal{D}_{f}(t,z)}.

Now as in Proposition
3.3.21
and the proof of Proposition
3.3.26
, we can write

(4.2.4)

g_{\mathbf{a}}=1+\sum_{i=0}^{M}\sum_{j=0}^{r-1}c_{ij}t^{i}z^{j},\quad c_{ij}\in B[f^{-1}],

for some
M\geqslant 0
. It then follows from (
4.2.2
)–(
4.2.3
) that

(4.2.5)

\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}=\mathrm{I}_{r}+\sum_{i=0}^{M}\sum_{j=0}^{r-1}c_{ij}t^{i}Z(t)^{j}\quad\in\Mat_{r}(B[f^{-1}][t]).

By (
2.5.9
) and (
3.3.25
), we see that
{\lvert c_{ij}\rvert}_{\infty}\leqslant{\lvert\widetilde{\theta}\rvert}_{\infty}^{-1}<1
for all
i
,
j
, and so
\lVert\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}-\mathrm{I}_{r}\rVert<1
. Thus we can define

\Psi_{\mathbf{a}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\prod_{N=1}^{\infty}\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}^{(N)},\quad\Bigl(=\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}^{(1)}\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}^{(2)}\cdots\Bigr),

which converges in
\Mat_{r}(\mathbb{T})
. We observe that
\Psi_{\mathbf{a}}^{(-1)}=\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}\Psi_{\mathbf{a}}
, and so from
[
1
]
*Prop. 3.1.3 it follows that the entries of
\Psi_{\mathbf{a}}
are entire functions. Moreover,
\Psi_{\mathbf{a}}\in\GL_{r}(\mathbb{T}_{\theta}(k_{\infty}(\zeta)))
.

Proposition 4.2.6
.

For effective
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
with
\deg\mathbf{a}>0
, let
\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}
be defined as in (
4.2.5
). Then there exists
\Delta\in\GL_{r}(\mathbf{B}[f(t)^{-1}])
so that the following diagonalizations hold.

(a)

\displaystyle\Delta^{-1}\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}\Delta=\begin{pmatrix}\ddots&&\\
&g_{b\star\mathbf{a}}&\\
&&\ddots\end{pmatrix}_{b\in(A/fA)^{\times}}
.

(b)

\displaystyle\Delta^{-1}\Psi_{\mathbf{a}}\Delta=\begin{pmatrix}\ddots&&\\
&\mathcal{G}_{b\star\mathbf{a}}&\\
&&\ddots\end{pmatrix}_{b\in(A/fA)^{\times}}
.

Proof.

For
b\in(A/fA)^{\times}
, let
z_{b}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\widetilde{\rho}_{b}(z)
in
\mathbf{K}
. If we apply
\widetilde{\rho}_{b}
to the identity in (
4.2.3
), we see that
(1,z_{b},\dots,z_{b}^{r-1})^{{\mathsf{T}}}
is an eigenvector of
Z(t)
with eigenvalue
z_{b}
. We define
\Delta\in\GL_{r}(\mathbf{B}[f(t)^{-1}])
so that

(4.2.7)

\Delta\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{pmatrix}\cdots&1&\cdots\\
\cdots&z_{b}&\cdots\\
&\vdots&\\
\cdots&z_{b}^{r-1}&\cdots\end{pmatrix}_{b\in(A/fA)^{\times}},

where the columns are indexed by
b\in(A/fA)^{\times}
in some order. To confirm that
\Delta\in\GL_{r}(B[f(t)^{-1}])
, we observe that
\det\Delta^{2}
is the discriminant of the
\mathbf{A}
-basis
\{1,z,\dots,z^{r-1}\}
of
\mathbf{B}
. In particular, it is invertible and its determinant is divisible only by ramified primes of
\mathbf{B}
, namely those dividing
f(t)
. Therefore, we have the diagonalization

\Delta^{-1}Z(t)\Delta=\diag\bigl(z_{b}\mid b\in(A/fA)^{\times}\bigr).

Then (
4.2.4
) and (
4.2.5
) imply that

\Delta^{-1}\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}\Delta=\diag\bigl(\widetilde{\rho}_{b}(g_{\mathbf{a}})\mid b\in(A/fA)^{\times}\bigr),

and (a) follows from Proposition
3.3.34
(b). To prove (b) let
\Psi_{R}=\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}^{(1)}\cdots\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}^{(R)}
for
R\geqslant 1
. Then (a) implies

\Delta^{-1}\Psi_{R}\Delta=\prod_{N=1}^{R}\diag\bigl(g_{b\star\mathbf{a}}^{(N)}\mid b\in(A/fA)^{\times}\bigr).

As
R\to\infty
, the left-hand side has limit
\Delta^{-1}\Psi_{\mathbf{a}}\Delta
by the definition of
\Psi_{\mathbf{a}}
. The right-hand side has the desired limit by Proposition
3.3.26
.
∎

4.2.8.
Rigid analytic trivialization of
H_{\mathbf{a}}
.

As
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{K})=H^{0}(\mathbf{U}/\mathbb{K},\mathcal{O}_{\mathbf{X}/\mathbb{K}}(-W_{\mathbf{a}}^{(1)}))
, we note that since the support of
W_{\mathbf{a}}^{(1)}
is
K^{(1)}
-rational (as are the points at infinity), we can find a
\mathbb{K}[t]
-basis
\{n_{1},\dots,n_{r}\}
of
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{K})
such that
\{n_{1},\dots,n_{r}\}\subseteq K^{(1)}[t,z]
, (e.g., see
[
43
]
*Thm. 5.4). Let
\mathbf{n}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(n_{1},\dots,n_{r})^{{\mathsf{T}}}
, and choose
\Phi\in\Mat_{r}(K^{\mathrm{perf}}[t])
so that
\sigma\mathbf{n}=\Phi\mathbf{n}
. Since
\{\sigma n_{1},\dots,\sigma n_{r}\}\subseteq K[t,z]
, we see that under these choices,

(4.2.9)

\Phi\in\Mat_{r}(K[t]).

Now there exists a unique matrix
Q\in\Mat_{r}(K^{(1)}[t])
such that
\mathbf{n}\equiv Q\cdot\mathbf{z}\pmod{\mathcal{D}_{f}(t,z)}
. As noted in
[
1
]
*Pf. of Prop. 6.4.4,
Q\in\GL_{r}(\mathbb{T}_{\theta})
and
\det Q(\theta)\neq 0
. On the one hand,
\sigma\mathbf{n}=\Phi\mathbf{n}\equiv\Phi Q\cdot\mathbf{z}\pmod{\mathcal{D}_{f}(t,z)}
, but on the other, using (
4.2.5
),

\sigma\mathbf{n}\equiv\sigma Q\cdot\mathbf{z}\equiv Q^{(-1)}g_{\mathbf{a}}\cdot\mathbf{z}\equiv Q^{(-1)}\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}\cdot\mathbf{z}\pmod{\mathcal{D}_{f}(t,z)}.

Therefore,
\Phi Q=Q^{(-1)}\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}
. If we set
\Psi\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=Q\Psi_{\mathbf{a}}\in\GL_{r}(\mathbb{T}_{\theta})
, then

\Psi^{(-1)}=Q^{(-1)}\Psi_{\mathbf{a}}^{(-1)}=Q^{(-1)}\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}\Psi_{\mathbf{a}}=\Phi Q\Psi_{\mathbf{a}}=\Phi\Psi,

and so
\Psi
is a rigid analytic trivialization for
H_{\mathbf{a}}
and has entries in
\mathbb{T}_{\theta}(k_{\infty}(\zeta))
.

4.2.10.
Periods of
E_{\mathbf{a}}
.

Using Anderson’s exponentiation theorem (Theorem
2.2.14
), we can calculate the period lattice
\Lambda_{\mathbf{a}}\subseteq\mathbb{C}_{\infty}^{d}
for
E_{\mathbf{a}}
in terms of special
\Pi
-values. We obtain an extension of one of Sinha’s main theorems from
[
48
]
*§5.3.3, §5.3.9.

Theorem 4.2.11
.

Let
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
be effective with
\deg\mathbf{a}>0
. Let
E_{\mathbf{a}}
be constructed with respect to a normalized
\mathbb{K}[\sigma]
-basis on
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{K})
. The period lattice
\Lambda_{\mathbf{a}}
of
E_{\mathbf{a}}
has an
\mathbf{A}
-basis
\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{r}
so that the following hold.

(a)

For
1\leqslant i\leqslant r
,

\pi_{i}=\begin{pmatrix}\vdots\\
\partial_{t}^{\ell_{b}-j}\bigl(z^{i-1}\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{a}}^{-1}\bigr)\big|_{\xi_{b}}\\
\vdots\end{pmatrix}_{(b,j)\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{a}}}.

The coordinate corresponding to
(b,\ell_{b})\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{a}}
is
\rho_{b}(\zeta)^{i-1}\Pi(b\star\mathbf{a})

(cf.
[
48
]
*§5.3)
.

(b)

When
\mathbf{a}=[a/f]
is basic, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant r
,

\pi_{i}=\begin{pmatrix}\vdots\\
\rho_{b}(\zeta)^{i-1}\Pi\bigl([ba/f]\bigr)\\
\vdots\end{pmatrix}_{b\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{a}}}.

Proof.

Fix a normalized
\mathbb{K}[\sigma]
-basis
\{h_{(a,j)}\mid(a,j)\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{a}}\}
of
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{K})
as in §
4.1
, and let
E_{\mathbf{a}}:\mathbf{A}\to\Mat_{d}(\mathbb{K}[\sigma])
be the corresponding
t
-module. We fix all of the same notation from earlier in §
4.2
. As
\Psi=Q\Psi_{\mathbf{a}}
is a rigid analytic trivialization of
E_{\mathbf{a}}
, if we take
\mathbf{r}\in\Mat_{1\times r}(\mathbb{T}_{\theta})
to be a row of
\Psi^{-1}
, we have
\mathbf{r}^{(-1)}\Phi-\mathbf{r}=0
,
and so
\mathcal{E}_{0}(\mathbf{r})\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{a}}
by Theorem
2.2.14
(a). Furthermore, as
\mathcal{E}_{0}(\mathbf{r})
ranges over all rows of
\Psi^{-1}
, we obtain a
\mathrm{d}E_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbf{A})
-basis of
\Lambda_{\mathbf{a}}
by Theorem
2.2.14
(c).

Now the entries of
\mathbf{n}=Q\mathbf{z}\in\Mat_{r\times 1}(K^{(1)}[t,z])
form a
\mathbb{K}[t]
-basis of
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{K})
. If
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{r})\in\Mat_{1\times r}(\mathbb{C}_{\infty}[t])
, then by §
4.1
,
(\varepsilon_{0}\circ\iota)(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=(\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{0}\circ\widetilde{\iota})(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{0}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot Q\cdot\mathbf{z})
.
Extending
\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{0}
continuously to
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{0}:\mathbb{B}_{\theta}\to\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
, we have for
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{r})\in\Mat_{1\times r}(\mathbb{T}_{\theta})
,

\mathcal{E}_{0}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot Q\cdot\mathbf{z}),

as in (
4.1.14
). Recalling from Proposition
4.2.6
(b) that
\Psi=Q\Psi_{\mathbf{a}}=Q\Delta\Gamma_{\mathbf{a}}\Delta^{-1}
, where we set
\Gamma_{\mathbf{a}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\diag(\mathcal{G}_{b\star\mathbf{a}}\mid b\in(A/fA)^{\times})
, we have

\Psi^{-1}=\Delta\Gamma_{\mathbf{a}}^{-1}\Delta^{-1}Q^{-1}.

Therefore, if
\mathbf{s}_{i}\in\Mat_{1\times r}(\mathbb{T}_{\theta})
is the standard basis vector with
1
in the
i
-th entry, then taking
\mathbf{r}_{i}=\mathbf{s}_{i}\Psi^{-1}
, we set

\pi_{i}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathcal{E}_{0}(\mathbf{r}_{i})=\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{0}\bigl(\mathbf{s}_{i}\cdot\Delta\Gamma_{\mathbf{a}}^{-1}\Delta^{-1}\cdot\mathbf{z}\bigr),

as defined in (
4.1.14
). By (
4.2.7
), if we index the columns of
\Delta
so that the first one is
\mathbf{z}
(and corresponds to
b=1\in(A/fA)^{\times}
), we have
\Delta^{-1}\cdot\mathbf{z}=(1,0,\dots,0)^{{\mathsf{T}}}
. It follows that

\pi_{i}=\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{0}\Bigl(\mathbf{s}_{i}\bigl(\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{a}}^{-1},z\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{a}}^{-1},\dots,z^{r-1}\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{a}}^{-1}\bigr)^{{\mathsf{T}}}\Bigr)=\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{0}\bigl(z^{i-1}\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{a}}^{-1}\bigr).

Thus (
4.1.14
) implies the formula for
\pi_{i}
in (a) as desired. The coordinates of
\pi_{i}
corresponding to
(b,\ell_{b})\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{a}}
are
(z^{i-1}\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{a}}^{-1})|_{\xi_{b}}=\rho_{b}(\zeta)^{i-1}\Pi(b\star\mathbf{a})
from (
3.3.31
).
∎

Remark 4.2.12
.

Sinha’s identities in
[
48
]
*§5.3.3, 5.3.9 apply to the case
\mathbf{a}=[1/f]
, and if we compare his formulas to Theorem
4.2.11
(b), we see that his period is almost exactly
\pi_{1}
, with only a discrepancy of a factor
b/f
when
\deg b=\deg f-1
.

4.3.
Fields of definition

Again we fix effective
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
with
\deg\mathbf{a}>0
. The goal of this section is to show that the associated
t
-module
E_{\mathbf{a}}
has a model defined over
K
. As previously observed, we see from
[
1
]
*§6.4
[
11
]
*Thm. 4.2.2, using arguments that go back to
[
48
]
*Thm. 3.2.5, that
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\overline{k})
has a
\overline{k}[\sigma]
-basis and that therefore
E_{\mathbf{a}}
has a model over
\overline{k}
. However, in all of these arguments the
\overline{k}[\sigma]
-basis is not explicitly constructed, but rather relies on an existential result of Anderson (see
[
3
]
*Lem. 1.4.5
[
1
]
*Prop. 4.3.2) to show that it suffices to prove that
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\overline{k})
is finitely generated over
\overline{k}[\sigma]
.

4.3.1.
The definability problem over
K
.

To show that
E_{\mathbf{a}}
has a model defined over
K
, we first establish that
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{K})
has a (normalized)
\mathbb{K}[\sigma]
-basis consisting of functions in
K[t,z]
(see Proposition
4.3.2
). Then we analyze the construction of
E_{\mathbf{a}}
with respect to this basis as in (
2.2.9
) to show that this model of
E_{\mathbf{a}}
is defined over
K
(see Theorem
4.3.29
).

We make two definitions:

•

A
\mathbb{K}
-subspace of
\mathbb{K}[t,z]
(in particular a subspace of
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{K})
) is
defined over
K
as a
\mathbb{K}
-vector space
if it has a
\mathbb{K}
-basis contained in
K[t,z]
.

•

A free left
\mathbb{K}[\sigma]
-submodule of
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{K})
is
defined over
K
as a
\mathbb{K}[\sigma]
-module
if it has a
\mathbb{K}[\sigma]
-basis contained in
K[t,z]
.

We note that a free
\mathbb{K}[\sigma]
-submodule may be defined over
K
as a
\mathbb{K}[\sigma]
-module but not as a
\mathbb{K}
-vector space due to the action of
\sigma
. Nevertheless, when it will not cause confusion we will refer to
\mathbb{K}
-subspaces and
\mathbb{K}[\sigma]
-submodules as “defined over
K
” without additional qualifiers when it is clear which situation we are in from the context.

Proposition 4.3.2
.

For
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
effective with
\deg\mathbf{a}>0
, the following hold.

(a)

H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{K})
is defined over
K
as a
\mathbb{K}[\sigma]
-module.

(b)

H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{K})
has a normalized
\mathbb{K}[\sigma]
-basis defined over
K
.

Remark 4.3.3
.

(i) A major issue with showing that
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{K})
is defined over
K
is that
K
is not perfect. For example, one can use the arguments in
[
1
]
*§6.4
[
11
]
*§4.2 to show that
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{K})
is defined over
K^{\mathrm{perf}}
, but descending to
K
itself is more tricky.
(ii) The proof of Proposition
4.3.2
takes several steps. It is essentially a more laborious version of the proofs of
[
1
]
*Lem. 6.4.1
[
11
]
*Thm. 4.2.2
[
48
]
*Thm. 3.2.5, but where we pay attention at each stage to obtaining bases of
\mathbb{K}
-subspaces and
\mathbb{K}[\sigma]
-submodules defined over
K
.

4.3.4.
Riemann-Roch spaces.

For a divisor
D\in\Div_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{X})
, we define

\mathcal{L}(D)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=H^{0}(\mathbf{X},\mathcal{O}_{\mathbf{X}}(D)),

which is a finite dimensional
\mathbb{K}
-vector space. We note that if
D\in\Div_{K}(\mathbf{X})
and
\mathcal{L}(D)\subseteq\mathbb{K}[t,z]
, then
\mathcal{L}(D)
is defined over
K
by
[
43
]
*Thm. 5.4. For
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, we set

(4.3.5)

M_{i}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathcal{L}(-W_{\mathbf{a}}^{(1)}+iI_{\mathbf{a}})\subseteq H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{K}),

which is then finite dimensional over
\mathbb{K}
and defined over
K
.

Lemma 4.3.6
.

The following hold.

(a)

For
i<0
,
M_{i}=\{0\}
. We have
M_{0}\subseteq M_{1}\subseteq M_{2}\subseteq\cdots
, and
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{K})=\cup_{i=0}^{\infty}M_{i}
.

(b)

For
i
,
j\in\mathbb{Z}
,
M_{i}^{(-j)}=\mathcal{L}(-W_{\mathbf{a}}^{(1-j)}+iI_{\mathbf{a}})
.

(c)

For
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and
j\geqslant 1
,

\displaystyle\sigma^{j}M_{i}

\displaystyle=g_{\mathbf{a}}g_{\mathbf{a}}^{(-1)}\cdots g_{\mathbf{a}}^{(-j+1)}M_{i}^{(-j)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}(-W_{\mathbf{a}}^{(1)}-\Xi_{\mathbf{a}}-\Xi_{\mathbf{a}}^{(-1)}-\cdots-\Xi_{\mathbf{a}}^{(-j+1)}+(i+j)I_{\mathbf{a}}).

(d)

For
i
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and
j
,
\ell\geqslant 0
,

\sigma^{j}M_{i}\cap\sigma^{\ell}M_{k}=\mathcal{L}\bigl(-W_{\mathbf{a}}^{(1)}-\Xi_{\mathbf{a}}-\Xi_{\mathbf{a}}^{(-1)}-\cdots-\Xi_{\mathbf{a}}^{(-\max(j,\ell)+1)}+\min(i+j,k+\ell)I_{\mathbf{a}}\bigr).

(e)

For
i
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and
j
,
\ell\geqslant 0
,

\sigma^{j}M_{i}\cap\sigma^{\ell}M_{k}=\begin{cases}\{0\},&\text{if $\max(j,\ell)>\min(i+j,k+\ell)$,}\\
\sigma^{\max(j,\ell)}M_{\min(i+j,k+\ell)-\max(j,\ell)},&\text{if $\max(j,\ell)\leqslant\min(i+j,k+\ell)$.}\end{cases}

Equivalently, for
i
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\ell\geqslant 0
,
M_{i}\cap\sigma^{\ell}M_{k}=\sigma^{\ell}M_{\min(i,k+\ell)-\ell}
.

(f)

For
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and
j\geqslant 0
,

M_{i}+\sigma M_{i}+\cdots+\sigma^{j}M_{i}\subseteq M_{i+j}
.

(g)

There exists
N\geqslant 0
such that for
i>N
,
M_{i}=M_{i-1}+\sigma M_{i-1}
.

Proof.

Parts (a)–(c) are straightforward applications of the Riemann-Roch theorem together with the definition of
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{K})
and its
\sigma
-action. Part (d) follows from (c) and the definition of
\mathcal{L}(D)
. The identity in the second case of (e) also holds for the first, as
M_{i}=\{0\}
if
i<0
, and this identity follows from (d). The equivalence in (e) is then a simple calculation, using that if
\ell\geqslant j
, then
\sigma^{j}M_{i}\cap\sigma^{\ell}M_{k}=\sigma^{j}(M_{i}\cap\sigma^{\ell-j}M_{k})
. Part (f) follows from (c). For (g), we note that (e) implies

M_{i-1}\cap\sigma M_{i-1}=\sigma M_{i-2}.

Therefore, as
\dim_{\mathbb{K}}M_{i-1}=\dim_{\mathbb{K}}\sigma M_{i-1}
, we have
\dim_{\mathbb{K}}(M_{i-1}+\sigma M_{i-1})=2\dim_{\mathbb{K}}M_{i-1}-\dim_{\mathbb{K}}M_{i-2}
. Now the Riemann-Roch theorem implies that there exists
N\geqslant 0
such that for
i>N
we have
\dim_{\mathbb{K}}M_{i-2}=-\deg W_{\mathbf{a}}^{(1)}+(i-2)\deg I_{\mathbf{a}}-\gamma+1
, where
\gamma
is the genus of
\mathbf{X}
. In particular,

\dim_{\mathbb{K}}M_{i-1}=\dim_{\mathbb{K}}M_{i-2}+\deg I_{\mathbf{a}}
. Thus for
i>N
,

\dim_{\mathbb{K}}(M_{i-1}+\sigma M_{i-1})=\dim_{\mathbb{K}}M_{i-1}+\deg I_{\mathbf{a}}=\dim_{\mathbb{K}}M_{i}.

As
M_{i-1}+\sigma M_{i-1}\subseteq M_{i}
by (f), we are done.
∎

Remark 4.3.7
.

We recall that Lemma
4.3.6
(g) is one of the essential tools in showing that
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{K})
is finitely generated over
\mathbb{K}[\sigma]
in
[
1
]
*§6.4
[
11
]
*Thm. 4.2.2. It will also play a crucial role for us at the end of the proof of Proposition
4.3.2
.

Criteria 4.3.8
.

For more flexible use in §
4.4
, we formalize some of these properties. Let us assume we have a sequence
\{M_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
of finite dimensional
\mathbb{K}
-vector subspaces of
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{K})
satisfying the following.

(a)

For
i<0
,
M_{i}=\{0\}
. We have
M_{0}\subseteq M_{1}\subseteq M_{2}\subseteq\cdots
, and
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{K})=\cup_{i=0}^{\infty}M_{i}
.

(b)

For
i
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\ell\geqslant 0
,

M_{i}\cap\sigma^{\ell}M_{k}=\sigma^{\ell}M_{\min(i,k+\ell)-\ell}.

(c)

For
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and
j\geqslant 0
,
M_{i}+\sigma M_{i}+\cdots+\sigma^{j}M_{i}\subseteq M_{i+j}
.

(d)

There exists
N\geqslant 0
such that for
i>N
,
M_{i}=M_{i-1}+\sigma M_{i-1}
.

That is,
\{M_{i}\}
satisfies the properties in Lemma
4.3.6
(a), (e)–(g), and in particular our original choice of
\{M_{i}\}
in (
4.3.5
) satisfies these criteria.

4.3.9.
Definitions of spaces and bases.

Fix a sequence
\{M_{i}\}
of
\mathbb{K}
-vector subspaces of
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{K})
as in Criteria
4.3.8
.
For
i\geqslant 0
, we recursively construct
\mathbb{K}
-vector spaces,

U_{i}\subseteq V_{i}\subseteq M_{i},

together with respective
\mathbb{K}
-bases
T_{i}\subseteq U_{i}
and
T_{i}\subseteq S_{i}\subseteq V_{i}
.

We set
U_{0}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=V_{0}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=M_{0}
. If
M_{0}=\{0\}
, we set
S_{0}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=T_{0}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\emptyset
. Otherwise, we let
S_{0}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=T_{0}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{u_{0,1},\dots,u_{0,s_{0}}\}
be any
\mathbb{K}
-basis for
M_{0}
.

For
i>0
, if
M_{i}=M_{i-1}+\sigma M_{i-1}
, we set
T_{i}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\emptyset
. Otherwise we let
T_{i}
be a set of
\mathbb{K}
-linearly independent elements of
M_{i}
such that
T_{i}
induces a
\mathbb{K}
-basis of
M_{i}/(M_{i-1}+\sigma M_{i-1})
. We write
T_{i}=\{u_{i,1},\dots,u_{i,s_{i}}\}
, and we set

U_{i}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\Span_{\mathbb{K}}(T_{i}),\quad V_{i}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=V_{i-1}+U_{i},\quad S_{i}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=S_{i-1}\cup T_{i}.

Since
M_{i}=(M_{i-1}+\sigma M_{i-1})\oplus U_{i}
by construction, we see that the sum
V_{i-1}\oplus U_{i}
is direct (as
V_{i-1}\subseteq M_{i-1}
), and so
S_{i}
is a
\mathbb{K}
-basis of
V_{i}
. Moreover,

(4.3.10)

V_{i}=\bigoplus_{\ell=0}^{i}U_{\ell},

where we will use “
\oplus
” to indicate that an internal sum is direct.

Remark 4.3.11
.

If
M_{0}
is defined over
K
, then we can find
T_{0}=S_{0}\subseteq K[t,z]
.
Suppose further that for each
i>0
, both
M_{i}
and
M_{i-1}+\sigma M_{i-1}
are defined over
K
. Then
M_{i}/(M_{i-1}+\sigma M_{i-1})
is defined over
K
, and so at each stage we can choose
T_{i}\subseteq K[t,z]
. Therefore, under these additional conditions we can select
T_{i}\subseteq S_{i}\subseteq K[t,z]
for all
i\geqslant 0
.

Lemma 4.3.12
.

For
i\geqslant 0
, the following hold.

(a)

S_{i}
is a
\mathbb{K}[\sigma]
-linearly independent subset of
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{K})
.

(b)

\displaystyle M_{i}=\bigoplus_{\ell=0}^{i}\bigoplus_{j=0}^{i-\ell}\sigma^{j}U_{\ell}=\bigoplus_{j=0}^{i}\sigma^{i-j}V_{j}
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
i
, and consider first the case
i=0
. Then (b) holds automatically since
M_{0}=U_{0}=V_{0}
. If
S_{0}=\emptyset
, then (a) holds trivially. So we suppose
S_{0}\neq\emptyset
, and suppose

(4.3.13)

\alpha_{1}u_{0,1}+\cdots+\alpha_{s_{0}}u_{0,s_{0}}=0,\quad\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{s_{0}}\in\mathbb{K}[\sigma].

For a finite subset
X\subseteq\mathbb{K}[\sigma]
, we set

\max\deg_{\sigma}(X)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\max_{\alpha\in X}\bigl(\deg_{\sigma}(\alpha)\bigr).

We proceed by induction on
\max\deg_{\sigma}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{s_{0}})
. Suppose
\max\deg_{\sigma}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{s_{0}})\leqslant 0
, in which case
\alpha_{n}\in\mathbb{K}
for all
1\leqslant n\leqslant s_{0}
. The
\mathbb{K}
-linear independence of
S_{0}
implies
\alpha_{1}=\cdots=\alpha_{s_{0}}=0
. Next suppose that
\max\deg_{\sigma}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{s_{0}})\leqslant j
for
j>0
. For each
n
we write

\alpha_{n}=\beta_{n}+b_{n}\sigma^{j},\quad\deg_{\sigma}(\beta_{n})\leqslant j-1,\ b_{n}\in\mathbb{K}.

Then (
4.3.13
) implies

(4.3.14)

\beta_{1}u_{0,1}+\dots+\beta_{s_{0}}u_{0,s_{0}}=-\sigma^{j}\bigl(b_{1}^{q^{j}}u_{0,1}+\dots+b_{s_{0}}^{q^{j}}u_{0,s_{0}}\bigr)

The left-hand side of this expression is in

U_{0}+\sigma U_{0}+\dots+\sigma^{j-1}U_{0}=M_{0}+\sigma M_{0}+\dots+\sigma^{j-1}M_{0}\subseteq M_{j-1},

where the final containment follows from Criterion
4.3.8
(c). The right-hand side of (
4.3.14
) is in
\sigma^{j}U_{0}=\sigma^{j}M_{0}
. By Criterion
4.3.8
(b),
M_{j-1}\cap\sigma^{j}M_{0}=\{0\}
, so both sides of (
4.3.14
) are
0
. By the induction hypothesis,
\beta_{1}=\dots=\beta_{s_{0}}=0
, and the
\mathbb{K}
-linear independence of
S_{0}
implies that
b_{1}=\dots=b_{s_{0}}=0
. Thus
S_{0}
is
\mathbb{K}[\sigma]
-linearly independent.

Now we consider the case
i>0
. The induction hypothesis implies two things:

(4.3.15)

\bigoplus_{j=0}^{\infty}\sigma^{j}V_{m}=\bigoplus_{j=0}^{\infty}\biggl(\bigoplus_{\ell=0}^{m}\sigma^{j}U_{\ell}\biggr)=\bigoplus_{\ell=0}^{m}\bigoplus_{j=0}^{\infty}\sigma^{j}U_{\ell},\quad(0\leqslant m\leqslant i-1),

where all of these internal sums of
\mathbb{K}
-vector spaces are direct;
also,

(4.3.16)

M_{m}=\bigoplus_{\ell=0}^{m}\bigoplus_{j=0}^{m-\ell}\sigma^{j}U_{\ell},\quad(0\leqslant m\leqslant i-1).

Notably this second identity implies that

(4.3.17)

M_{i-1}+\sigma M_{i-1}=\biggl(\bigoplus_{\ell=0}^{i-1}\bigoplus_{j=0}^{i-1-\ell}\sigma^{j}U_{\ell}\biggr)+\biggl(\bigoplus_{\ell=0}^{i-1}\bigoplus_{j=1}^{i-\ell}\sigma^{j}U_{\ell}\biggr)=\bigoplus_{\ell=0}^{i-1}\bigoplus_{j=0}^{i-\ell}\sigma^{j}U_{\ell},

where the final term is a direct sum by (
4.3.15
). Now because
M_{i}=(M_{i-1}+\sigma M_{i})\oplus U_{i}
by the definition of
U_{i}
, we see that the first identity in part (b) for
i
follows immediately, and the second from (
4.3.10
). The rest of the proof is devoted to proving (a).

Suppose that
T_{i}=\emptyset
. In this case
S_{i}=S_{i-1}
and
U_{i}=\{0\}
, and then (a) holds by the induction hypothesis.
Now assume that
T_{i}=\{u_{i,1},\dots,u_{i,s_{i}}\}\neq\emptyset
, and suppose that

(4.3.18)

\sum_{m=0}^{i}\sum_{n=1}^{s_{m}}\alpha_{m,n}u_{m,n}=0,\quad\alpha_{m,n}\in\mathbb{K}[\sigma].

Here we use the convention that
s_{m}=0
if
T_{m}=\emptyset
, whereby the corresponding sum is empty. We make the following
claim
: if for
j\geqslant i
,

(4.3.19)

\max\deg_{\sigma}(\alpha_{m,n}\mid 1\leqslant n\leqslant s_{m})\leqslant j-m,\quad\forall\,m,\ 0\leqslant m\leqslant i,

then
\alpha_{m,n}=0
for all
0\leqslant m\leqslant i
,
1\leqslant n\leqslant s_{m}
.

We prove the claim by induction on
j
. For the base case we assume
j=i
, so that
\deg_{\sigma}\alpha_{m,n}\leqslant i-m
. Then the left-hand side of (
4.3.18
) is an element of
\oplus_{m=0}^{i}\oplus_{\ell=0}^{i-m}\sigma^{\ell}U_{m}
, which is direct by the proof of (b) for
i
above. Thus
\alpha_{m,n}=0
for all
0\leqslant m\leqslant i
,
1\leqslant n\leqslant s_{m}
. Now take
j>i
, and assume the claim holds up to
j-1
. First suppose

(4.3.20)

\displaystyle\max\deg_{\sigma}(\alpha_{0,1},\dots,\alpha_{0,s_{0}})\leqslant j,

\displaystyle\max\deg_{\sigma}(\alpha_{m,n}\mid 1\leqslant n\leqslant s_{m})\leqslant j-1-m,\quad\forall\,m,\ 1\leqslant m\leqslant i.

For
1\leqslant n\leqslant s_{0}
, write
\alpha_{0,n}=\beta_{n}+b_{n}\sigma^{j}
,
\deg_{\sigma}(\beta_{n})\leqslant j-1
,
b_{n}\in\mathbb{K}
. We rewrite (
4.3.18
) as

(4.3.21)

\sum_{n=1}^{s_{0}}\beta_{n}u_{0,n}+\sum_{m=1}^{i}\sum_{n=1}^{s_{m}}\alpha_{m,n}u_{m,n}=-\sigma^{j}\biggl(\sum_{n=1}^{s_{0}}b_{n}^{q^{j}}u_{0,n}\biggr).

Using that
U_{m}\subseteq M_{m}
together with Criterion
4.3.8
(c), we find that the left-hand side is in
M_{j-1}
. The right-hand side is in
\sigma^{j}M_{0}
. Thus the expressions on both sides of (
4.3.21
) are in
M_{j-1}\cap\sigma^{j}M_{0}=\{0\}
(using Criterion
4.3.8
(b)). Therefore, the induction hypothesis implies that
\beta_{n}=0
for
1\leqslant n\leqslant s_{0}
and that
\alpha_{m,n}=0
for
1\leqslant m\leqslant i
,
1\leqslant n\leqslant s_{m}
. Likewise the
\mathbb{K}
-linear independence of
u_{0,1},\dots,u_{0,s_{0}}
implies that
b_{n}=0
for
1\leqslant n\leqslant s_{0}
. We conclude that under the conditions in (
4.3.20
), the claim is proved.

We now suppose that
1\leqslant\ell\leqslant i
. We will show that if the claim holds when

(4.3.22)

\displaystyle\max\deg_{\sigma}(\alpha_{m,n}\mid 1\leqslant n\leqslant s_{m})\leqslant j-m,\quad\forall\,m,\ 0\leqslant m\leqslant\ell-1,

\displaystyle\max\deg_{\sigma}(\alpha_{m,n}\mid 1\leqslant n\leqslant s_{m})\leqslant j-1-m,\quad\forall\,m,\ \ell\leqslant m\leqslant i,

then it also holds when

(4.3.23)

\displaystyle\max\deg_{\sigma}(\alpha_{m,n}\mid 1\leqslant n\leqslant s_{m})\leqslant j-m,\quad\forall\,m,\ 0\leqslant m\leqslant\ell,

\displaystyle\max\deg_{\sigma}(\alpha_{m,n}\mid 1\leqslant n\leqslant s_{m})\leqslant j-1-m,\quad\forall\,m,\ \ell+1\leqslant m\leqslant i.

Once we show this then the claim is proved, thus concluding the induction on
j
as well as the induction on
i
and completing the proof of part (a).

We proceed as in the previous cases. For
0\leqslant m\leqslant\ell
and
1\leqslant n\leqslant s_{m}
, we write
\alpha_{m,n}=\beta_{m,n}+b_{m,n}\sigma^{j-m}
,
\deg_{\sigma}\beta_{m,n}\leqslant j-m-1
,
b_{m,n}\in\mathbb{K}
. Rearranging (
4.3.18
), we have

(4.3.24)

\sum_{m=0}^{\ell}\sum_{n=1}^{s_{m}}\beta_{m,n}u_{m,n}+\sum_{m=\ell+1}^{i}\sum_{n=1}^{s_{m}}\alpha_{m,n}u_{m,n}=-\sum_{m=0}^{\ell}\sigma^{j-m}\biggl(\sum_{n=1}^{s_{m}}b_{m,n}^{q^{j-m}}u_{m,n}\biggr).

As in previous cases, the left-hand side is in
M_{j-1}
, and the right is visibly in

\sigma^{j-\ell}\cdot\sum_{m=0}^{\ell}\sigma^{\ell-m}M_{m}\subseteq\sigma^{j-\ell}M_{\ell},\quad\textup{(Criterion~\ref{Cr:Mi}(c)).}

Thus both sides of (
4.3.24
) are in

M_{j-1}\cap\sigma^{j-\ell}M_{\ell}=\sigma^{j-\ell}M_{\ell-1},\quad\textup{(Criterion~\ref{Cr:Mi}(b)).}

Since
\ell-1\leqslant i-1
, we see from (
4.3.16
) that

(4.3.25)

\sigma^{j-\ell}M_{\ell-1}=\sigma^{j-\ell}\Biggl(\biggl(\bigoplus_{\lambda=0}^{\ell-1}\sigma^{\lambda}U_{0}\biggr)\oplus\biggl(\bigoplus_{\lambda=0}^{\ell-2}\sigma^{\lambda}U_{1}\biggr)\oplus\cdots\oplus\bigl(U_{\ell-2}\oplus\sigma U_{\ell-2}\bigr)\oplus U_{\ell-1}\Biggr).

On the other hand, the right-hand side of (
4.3.24
) is in

(4.3.26)

\sigma^{j-\ell}\bigl(\sigma^{\ell}U_{0}\oplus\sigma^{\ell-1}U_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\sigma U_{\ell-1}\oplus U_{\ell}\bigr).

If
\ell\leqslant i-1
, then this sum is indeed direct, as all terms are then sub-terms of (
4.3.15
). If
\ell=i
, then this sum is direct by our previous proof of part (b) for
i
. When we compare the terms that appear in (
4.3.25
) and (
4.3.26
), there is no overlap, so it must be that both sides of (
4.3.24
) are
0
, and then the vanishing of coefficients follows as in previous cases. Thus, we have completed the proof of (a).
∎

Remark 4.3.27
.

Returning to the case that
M_{i}=\mathcal{L}(-W_{\mathbf{a}}^{(1)}+iI_{\mathbf{a}})
as in (
4.3.5
), we have already observed each
M_{i}
is defined over
K
. By Lemma
4.3.6
(c), for
i>0
,

M_{i-1}+\sigma M_{i-1}=\mathcal{L}(-W_{\mathbf{a}}^{(1)}+(i-1)I_{\mathbf{a}})+\mathcal{L}(-W_{\mathbf{a}}^{(1)}-\Xi_{\mathbf{a}}+iI_{\mathbf{a}}),

and so it follows that
M_{i-1}+\sigma M_{i-1}
is defined over
K
. By Remark
4.3.11
, we can choose
T_{i}\subseteq K[t,z]
for each
i\geqslant 0
. Therefore, in this case the
\mathbb{K}[\sigma]
-linear span of
S_{i}
is defined over
K
as a
\mathbb{K}[\sigma]
-module.

Proof of Proposition
4.3.2
.

Let
N\geqslant 0
be chosen as in Criterion
4.3.8
(d). By construction then, for
i>N
we have
U_{i}=\{0\}
and
V_{i}=V_{i-1}
. We claim that
S_{N}
is a
\mathbb{K}[\sigma]
-basis of
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{K})
. By Lemma
4.3.12
(a),
S_{N}
is
\mathbb{K}[\sigma]
-linearly independent. From Lemma
4.3.12
(b), we find that for
i>N
,

M_{i}=\bigoplus_{j=0}^{N}\sigma^{i-j}V_{j}\oplus\bigoplus_{j=N+1}^{i}\sigma^{i-j}V_{N}\subseteq\bigoplus_{j=0}^{i}\sigma^{i-j}V_{N},

and as
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{K})=\cup_{i=0}^{\infty}M_{i}
, we see that
V_{N}
, and hence
S_{N}
, generate
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{K})
as
\mathbb{K}[\sigma]
-modules.

As noted in Remark
4.3.27
, if we take
\{M_{i}\}
defined as in (
4.3.5
), then we can choose the elements of
S_{N}
to be in
K[t,z]
, and so
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{K})
is defined over
K
. Furthermore, in the construction of a normalized
\mathbb{K}[\sigma]
-basis in §
4.1
, §
4.1
, if the given basis
\{\widetilde{h}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{h}_{d}\}
is defined over
K
, then their values at the points
\xi_{a}
are all in
K
, and thus the linear changes of variables to obtain the normalized
\{h^{\prime}_{(a,j)}\}_{a\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{a}}}
are defined over
K
.
∎

Remark 4.3.28
.

The proofs of Proposition
4.3.2
and Lemma
4.3.12
show that the recursive definitions of
S_{0}\subseteq S_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq S_{N}
in §
4.3
provide an algorithm for calculating
\mathbb{K}[\sigma]
-bases of
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{K})
.

Theorem 4.3.29
.

Let
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
be effective with
\deg\mathbf{a}>0
. Then
E_{\mathbf{a}}
has a model defined over
K
.

Proof.

We may as well switch to the case that
\mathbb{K}=\overline{k}
. By Proposition
4.3.2
, we can choose a
\overline{k}[\sigma]
-basis of
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\overline{k})
defined over
K
. Since transforming such a basis to a normalized one can be done over
K
, as in §
4.1
, it suffices to assume our basis is normalized. To that end, let
\{h_{(a,j)}\mid(a,j)\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{a}}\}\subseteq H_{\mathbf{a}}(\overline{k})
be a normalized
\overline{k}[\sigma]
-basis defined over
K
. We wish to determine the matrix
Q^{*}
as in (
2.2.9
) and show that the entries of
Q^{*}
are all in
K[\tau]
. Fix
(a_{0},j_{0})\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{a}}
. Then

(4.3.30)

t\cdot h_{(a_{0},j_{0})}=\sum_{i\geqslant 0}\sum_{(a,j)\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{a}}}\alpha_{i,(a,j)}\sigma^{i}h_{(a,j)}=\sum_{i\geqslant 0}\sum_{(a,j)\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{a}}}\alpha_{i,(a,j)}g_{\mathbf{a}}g_{\mathbf{a}}^{(-1)}\cdots g_{\mathbf{a}}^{(-i+1)}h_{(a,j)}^{(-i)},

for unique
\alpha_{i,(a,j)}\in\overline{k}
, almost all of which are
0
. We will be done after proving the following
claim
: for
i\geqslant 0
and
(a,j)\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{a}}
, we have
\alpha_{i,(a,j)}^{(i)}\in K
.

We proceed by induction on
i
. When
i=0
, Lemma
4.1.12
implies for
(a,j)\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{a}}
that

\alpha_{0,(a,j)}=\partial_{t}^{\ell_{a}-j}\bigl(t\cdot h_{(a_{0},j_{0})}\bigr)\big|_{\xi_{a}},

which is an element of
K
since
t\cdot h_{(a_{0},j_{0})}\in K[t,z]
and differentiation with respect to
t
leaves
K(t,z)
invariant, as in §
4.1
. This concludes the base case.

Now let
m\geqslant 1
and suppose that
\alpha_{i,(a,j)}^{(i)}\in K
for all
0\leqslant i\leqslant m-1
and
(a,j)\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{a}}
. Let
b\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{a}}
. For
0\leqslant\ell\leqslant\ell_{b}
, we apply
\partial_{t}^{\ell}
to (
4.3.30
) and obtain,

\partial_{t}^{\ell}\bigl(t\cdot h_{(a_{0},j_{0})}\bigr)=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{a}}}\sum_{j=0}^{\ell_{a}}\sum_{i\geqslant 0}\alpha_{i,(a,j)}\partial_{t}^{\ell}\bigl(g_{\mathbf{a}}g_{\mathbf{a}}^{(-1)}\cdots g_{\mathbf{a}}^{(-i+1)}h_{(a,j)}^{(-i)}\bigr).

For
i\geqslant 0
, we note that
g_{\mathbf{a}}g_{\mathbf{a}}^{(-1)}\cdots g_{\mathbf{a}}^{(-i+1)}h_{(a,j)}^{(-i)}

vanishes along the divisor
W_{\mathbf{a}}^{(1)}+\Xi_{\mathbf{a}}+\Xi_{\mathbf{a}}^{(-1)}+\dots+\Xi_{\mathbf{a}}^{(-i+1)}
. Therefore, when
i>m
, we have
\ord_{\xi_{b}}(g_{\mathbf{a}}^{(m)}\cdots g_{\mathbf{a}}^{(m-i+1)}h_{(a,j)}^{(m-i)})>\ell_{b}
, which implies that

\partial_{t}^{\ell}(g_{\mathbf{a}}^{(m)}\cdots g_{\mathbf{a}}^{(m-i+1)}h_{(a,j)}^{(m-i)})|_{\xi_{b}}=0
. Thus, using the product rule, twisting
m
times, and evaluating at
\xi_{b}
, we obtain

(4.3.31)

\partial_{t}^{\ell}\bigl(t\cdot h_{(a_{0},j_{0})}^{(m)}\bigr)\big|_{\xi_{b}}=\sum_{i=0}^{m}\sum_{a\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{a}}}\sum_{j=0}^{\ell_{a}}\alpha_{i,(a,j)}^{(m)}\sum_{k=0}^{\ell}\partial_{t}^{\ell-k}\bigl(g_{\mathbf{a}}^{(m)}\cdots g_{\mathbf{a}}^{(m-i+1)}\bigr)\partial_{t}^{k}\bigl(h_{(a,j)}^{(m-i)}\bigr)\big|_{\xi_{b}}.

Before evaluating we have used that differentiation with respect to
t
commutes with Frobenius twisting. Again differentiation with respect to
t
leaves
K(t,z)
invariant, so the left-hand side of this equation is in
K
. Likewise, by the induction hypothesis,

\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}\sum_{a\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{a}}}\sum_{j=0}^{\ell_{a}}\alpha_{i,(a,j)}^{(m)}\sum_{k=0}^{\ell}\partial_{t}^{\ell-k}\bigl(g_{\mathbf{a}}^{(m)}\cdots g_{\mathbf{a}}^{(m-i+1)}\bigr)\partial_{t}^{k}\bigl(h_{(a,j)}^{(m-i)}\bigr)\big|_{\xi_{b}}\in K.

The remaining terms to account for in (
4.3.31
) have
i=m
, and are thus

(4.3.32)

\sum_{a\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{a}}}\sum_{j=0}^{\ell_{a}}\alpha_{m,(a,j)}^{(m)}\sum_{k=0}^{\ell}\partial_{t}^{\ell-k}\bigl(g_{\mathbf{a}}^{(m)}\cdots g_{\mathbf{a}}^{(1)}\bigr)\partial_{t}^{k}\bigl(h_{(a,j)}\bigr)\big|_{\xi_{b}}.

If
a\neq b
, then as observed in the proof of Lemma
4.1.12
,
\ord_{\xi_{b}}(h_{(a,j)})>\ell_{b}
, so as
k\leqslant\ell_{b}
in (
4.3.32
), we have
\partial_{t}^{k}(h_{(b,j)})|_{\xi_{b}}=0
. When
a=b
, we apply (
4.1.9
), and see that the only possibly non-zero terms in (
4.3.32
) occur when
a=b
,
k=\ell_{b}-j
. This condition on
k
forces
\ell_{b}-\ell\leqslant j\leqslant\ell_{b}
. Bringing everything together and using (
4.1.9
), we find

(4.3.33)

\sum_{j=\ell_{b}-\ell}^{\ell_{b}}\alpha_{m,(b,j)}^{(m)}\partial_{t}^{\ell+j-\ell_{b}}\bigl(g_{\mathbf{a}}^{(m)}\dots g_{\mathbf{a}}^{(1)}\bigr)\big|_{\xi_{b}}\in K.

If
\ell=0
, then there is only one term, and we obtain

\alpha_{m,(b,\ell_{b})}^{(m)}\bigl(g_{\mathbf{a}}^{(m)}\cdots g_{\mathbf{a}}^{(1)}\bigr)\big|_{\xi_{b}}\in K.

For
n>0
, the zeros of
g_{\mathbf{a}}^{(n)}
are supported on
\{\xi_{a}^{(k)}\mid a\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{a}},\ k\geqslant n\}
, whence
(g_{\mathbf{a}}^{(m)}\cdots g_{\mathbf{a}}^{(1)})|_{\xi_{b}}\in K^{\times}
. Thus
\alpha_{m,(b,\ell_{b})}^{(m)}\in K
. Suppose that we have shown that
\alpha_{m,(b,\ell_{b})}^{(m)}
,
\alpha_{m,(b,\ell_{b}-1)}^{(m)},\dots,\alpha_{m,(b,\ell_{b}-\ell+1)}^{(m)}\in K
. Then (
4.3.33
) implies that
\alpha_{m,(b,\ell_{b}-\ell)}^{(m)}(g_{\mathbf{a}}^{(m)}\cdots g_{\mathbf{a}}^{(1)})|_{\xi_{b}}\in K
, and so as in the
\ell=0
case, we obtain
\alpha_{m,(b,\ell_{b}-\ell)}^{(m)}\in K
. Therefore, by induction on
\ell
we have shown that
\alpha_{m,(b,j)}^{(m)}\in K
for all
0\leqslant j\leqslant\ell_{b}
, and thus also for all
b\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{a}}
. This completes the proof of the claim and the theorem.
∎

4.4.
Reduction modulo finite primes

Let
f\in A_{+}
with
\deg f\geqslant 1
, and fix
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
effective with
\deg\mathbf{a}>0
. As Theorem
4.3.29
fashions a model of
E_{\mathbf{a}}
defined over
K
, we can conjugate by a suitable matrix
b\mathrm{I}
,
b\in B
, to clear denominators and obtain a model

E_{\mathbf{a}}:\mathbf{A}\to\Mat_{d}(B[\tau])

defined over
B
. In this section we explore different models for
E_{\mathbf{a}}
and investigate good reduction at finite primes of
B
. We more fully determine primes of good reduction in §
A
.

4.4.1.
Primes in
B^{\mathrm{perf}}
.

Temporarily let
F/k
be a finite separable extension, and let
B\subseteq F
be the integral closure of
A
in
F
.
For a maximal ideal
\mathfrak{p}\subseteq B
, let
B_{\mathfrak{p}}
be the localization of
B
at
\mathfrak{p}
. We take
\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}=B_{\mathfrak{p}}/\mathfrak{p}B_{\mathfrak{p}}\cong B/\mathfrak{p}
. We also have
\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{perf}}\subseteq B^{\mathrm{perf}}\subseteq(B_{\mathfrak{p}})^{\mathrm{perf}}\subseteq F^{\mathrm{perf}}
, where for
S\subseteq F
,
S^{\mathrm{perf}}=\{\alpha\in F^{\mathrm{perf}}\mid\exists\,n\geqslant 0,\ \alpha^{p^{n}}\in S\}
. It is straightforward to check that
B^{\mathrm{perf}}
is a perfect ring and that
\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{perf}}
is an ideal of
B^{\mathrm{perf}}
. The following properties can be verified (cf.
[
26
]
*Lem. 2.3).

•

\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{perf}}
is a maximal ideal of
B^{\mathrm{perf}}
.

•

\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{perf}}\cap B=\mathfrak{p}
.

•

B^{\mathrm{perf}}/\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{perf}}\cong\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

•

The localization
(B^{\mathrm{perf}})_{\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{perf}}}
is the same as
(B_{\mathfrak{p}})^{\mathrm{perf}}
, and will be denoted
B_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\mathrm{perf}}
.

4.4.2.
Reduction of
t
-modules.

Suppose that
E:\mathbf{A}\to\Mat_{d}(B_{\mathfrak{p}}[\tau])
is a
t
-module. We will denote the reduction of
E
modulo
\mathfrak{p}
by

\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5muE}:\mathbf{A}\to\Mat_{d}(\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}[\tau]),

which is a
t
-module over
\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}
defined in the natural way. That is, if
E_{a}=\sum_{i=0}^{s}C_{i}\tau^{i}
,
C_{i}\in\Mat_{d}(B_{\mathfrak{p}})
, for
a\in\mathbf{A}
, then
\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5muE}_{a}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{i=0}^{s}\overline{C}_{i}\tau^{i}
,
\overline{C}_{i}\in\Mat_{d}(\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}})
, where
\overline{C}_{i}
is obtained from
C_{i}
by reducing its entries modulo
\mathfrak{p}
. For any
a\in\mathbf{A}
, we have commutative diagrams,
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(B_{\mathfrak{p}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5muE}(\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}),}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(B_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\mathrm{perf}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5muE}(\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}),}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(B_{\mathfrak{p}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5muE}(\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(B_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\mathrm{perf}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5muE}(\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5muE}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5muE}_{a}}

where the horizontal maps are the natural reduction maps. In other words,

E(B_{\mathfrak{p}})\twoheadrightarrow\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5muE}(\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}})
and
E(B_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\mathrm{perf}})\twoheadrightarrow\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5muE}(\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}})
are
\mathbf{A}
-module homomorphisms.

4.4.3.
Reduction of
t
-comotives.

Fix a maximal ideal
\mathfrak{p}\subseteq B
. Suppose that
E:\mathbf{A}\to\Mat_{d}(B_{\mathfrak{p}}[\tau])
is a
t
-module over
B_{\mathfrak{p}}
. We define the
t
-comotive associated to
E
over
B_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\mathrm{perf}}
,

H(E)(B_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\mathrm{perf}})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\Mat_{1\times d}(B_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\mathrm{perf}}[\sigma])\subseteq H(E)(F^{\mathrm{perf}})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\Mat_{1\times d}(F^{\mathrm{perf}}[\sigma]).

Then
H(E)(F^{\mathrm{perf}})
is the
t
-comotive of
E
over
F^{\mathrm{perf}}
as in §
2.2
, and
H(E)(B_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\mathrm{perf}})
inherits the structure of a left
B_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\mathrm{perf}}[t,\sigma]
-module.

Likewise, we have the
t
-comotive of the reduction of
E
modulo
\mathfrak{p}
,
H(\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5muE})(\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\Mat_{1\times d}(\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}[\sigma])
. Then for any
a\in\mathbf{A}
, we have a commutative diagram,
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(E)(B_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\mathrm{perf}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5muE})(\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}).}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(E)(B_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\mathrm{perf}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5muE})(\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\cdot)E_{a}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\cdot)\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5muE}_{a}^{*}}

4.4.4.
Good reduction.

When
E:\mathbf{A}\to\Mat_{d}(B_{\mathfrak{p}}[\tau])
is coabelian, we say that
E
has
good reduction
if
\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5muE}
is coabelian and the rank of
\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5muE}
, i.e., the rank of
H(\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5muE})(\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}})
as an
\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}[t]
-module, is the same as the rank of
E
. Otherwise,
E
has
bad reduction
.

If
E:\mathbf{A}\to\Mat_{d}(K[\tau])
is a coabelian
t
-module, then we say that
E
has
good reduction at
\mathfrak{p}
if
E
is isomorphic over
K[\tau]
to a
t
-module
E^{\prime}:\mathbf{A}\to\Mat_{d}(B_{\mathfrak{p}}[\tau])
that has good reduction. Otherwise,
E
has
bad reduction at
\mathfrak{p}
.

4.4.5.
Good reduction of
E_{\mathbf{a}}
.

Returning to the setting of Sinha modules, let
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
be effective with
\deg\mathbf{a}>0
. Suppose
\mathfrak{p}\in B
is a maximal ideal prime to
f
, and let

H_{\mathbf{a},\mathfrak{p}}=H_{\mathbf{a},\mathfrak{p}}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{\mathfrak{p}})=H^{0}\bigl(U,\mathcal{O}_{X}(-\overline{W}_{\mathbf{a}}^{(1)})\bigr)\subseteq\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{\mathfrak{p}}[t,z],

where
\overline{W}_{\mathbf{a}}\in\Div_{\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}}(X)
is the reduction of
W_{\mathbf{a}}
modulo
\mathfrak{p}
as in §
3.4
. We make
H_{\mathbf{a},\mathfrak{p}}
into a left
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{\mathfrak{p}}[t,\sigma]
-module by setting
\sigma h\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\overline{g}_{\mathbf{a}}h^{(-1)}
for
h\in H_{\mathbf{a},\mathfrak{p}}
, which is well-defined by Proposition
3.4.3
.
Moreover,
H_{\mathbf{a},\mathfrak{p}}
is an integral ideal of
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{\mathfrak{p}}[t,z]
and so is free and finitely generated as an
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{\mathfrak{p}}[t]
-module of rank
r=[K:k]
. As
\overline{W}_{\mathbf{a}}
and
\overline{\Xi}_{\mathbf{a}}
have disjoint supports from
I_{\mathbf{a}}
, the same proof as in
[
1
]
*§6.4.2 demonstrates that
H_{\mathbf{a},\mathfrak{p}}
is a
t
-comotive over
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{\mathfrak{p}}
. As in Lemma
4.1.2
, it satisfies

(4.4.6)

\displaystyle\rank H_{\mathbf{a},\mathfrak{p}}=\rank_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{\mathfrak{p}}[t]}H_{\mathbf{a},\mathfrak{p}}=\rank H_{\mathbf{a}}=r,

\displaystyle\dim H_{\mathbf{a},\mathfrak{p}}=\rank_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{\mathfrak{p}}[\sigma]}H_{\mathbf{a},\mathfrak{p}}=\deg\overline{\Xi}_{\mathbf{a}}=\deg\Xi_{\mathbf{a}}=\dim H_{\mathbf{a}}=d.

We let
H_{\mathbf{a},\mathfrak{p}}(\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}})=H_{\mathbf{a},\mathfrak{p}}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{\mathfrak{p}})\cap\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}[t,z]
, and note that
H_{\mathbf{a},\mathfrak{p}}(\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}})
is a
t
-comotive as a left
\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}[t,\sigma]
-module with the same rank and dimension as
H_{\mathbf{a}}
. Furthermore, the algorithm mapped out in §
4.3
, and in particular in Proposition
4.3.2
, works equally well for
H_{\mathbf{a},\mathfrak{p}}(\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}})
, from which we can obtain an
\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}[\sigma]
-basis of
H_{\mathbf{a},\mathfrak{p}}(\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}})
that is defined over
\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

Proposition 4.4.7
.

Let
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
be effective with
\deg\mathbf{a}>0
, and let
\mathfrak{p}
be a maximal ideal of
B
relatively prime to
f
. Suppose that
\{h_{1},\dots,h_{d}\}
is a
\overline{k}[\sigma]
-basis of
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\overline{k})
such that

(a)

\{h_{1},\dots,h_{d}\}
is contained in
B_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\mathrm{perf}}[t,z]
,

(b)

\{\overline{h}_{1},\dots,\overline{h}_{d}\}
generates
H_{\mathbf{a},\mathfrak{p}}(\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}})
as an
\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}[\sigma]
-module.

Then
\{\overline{h}_{1},\dots,\overline{h}_{d}\}
is an
\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}[\sigma]
-basis of
H_{\mathbf{a},\mathfrak{p}}(\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}})
, and
\{h_{1},\dots,h_{d}\}
is a
B_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\mathrm{perf}}[\sigma]
-basis of
H_{\mathbf{a}}(B_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\mathrm{perf}})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=H_{\mathbf{a}}(\overline{k})\cap B_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\mathrm{perf}}[t,z]
.

Proof.

We consider the reduction map
\pi:H_{\mathbf{a}}(B_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\mathrm{perf}})\to H_{\mathbf{a},\mathfrak{p}}(\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}})
, which commutes with the action of
\sigma
. The kernel of
\pi
is
\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{perf}}\cdot H_{\mathbf{a}}(B_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\mathrm{perf}})=H_{\mathbf{a}}(K^{\mathrm{perf}})\cap\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{perf}}\cdot B_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\mathrm{perf}}[t,z]
. Also,

\sigma\bigl(\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{perf}}\cdot H_{\mathbf{a}}(B_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\mathrm{perf}})\bigr)=\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{perf}}\cdot\sigma\bigl(H_{\mathbf{a}}(B_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\mathrm{perf}})\bigr),

since
\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{perf}}
is invariant under Frobenius twisting. We take
S=\{h_{1},\dots,h_{d}\}
and
\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5muS}=\{\overline{h}_{1},\dots,\overline{h}_{d}\}
. We note that as
\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5muS}
generates
H_{\mathbf{a},\mathfrak{p}}(\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}})/\sigma H_{\mathbf{a},\mathfrak{p}}(\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}})
, which has dimension
d
over
\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}
, it follows that
\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5muS}
is
\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}
-linearly independent. Nakayama’s lemma
[
35
]
*§X.4 implies that
S
induces a
B_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\mathrm{perf}}
-basis of
H_{\mathbf{a}}(B_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\mathrm{perf}})/\sigma H_{\mathbf{a}}(B_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\mathrm{perf}})
. By induction, for
\ell\geqslant 1
,
\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5muS}\cup\sigma\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5muS}\cup\dots\cup\sigma^{\ell-1}\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5muS}
is an
\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}
-basis of
H_{\mathbf{a},\mathfrak{p}}(\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}})/\sigma^{\ell}H_{\mathbf{a},\mathfrak{p}}(\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}})
, implying that
S\cup\sigma S\cup\dots\cup\sigma^{\ell-1}S
induces a
B_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\mathrm{perf}}
-basis of
H_{\mathbf{a}}(B_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\mathrm{perf}})/\sigma^{\ell}H_{\mathbf{a}}(B_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\mathrm{perf}})
. Taking a direct limit over
\ell
, we find that
S
is a
B_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\mathrm{perf}}[\sigma]
-basis of
H_{\mathbf{a}}(B_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\mathrm{perf}})
.
∎

Proposition 4.4.8
.

For
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
with
\deg\mathbf{a}>0
, let
E_{\mathbf{a}}:\mathbf{A}\to\Mat_{d}(K[\tau])
be constructed as in Theorem
4.3.29
with respect to a
\overline{k}[\sigma]
-basis
\{h_{1},\dots,h_{d}\}
of
H_{\mathbf{a}}(\overline{k})
defined over
K
. Suppose
\mathfrak{p}
is a prime of
B
, not dividing
f
, such that
\{h_{1},\dots,h_{d}\}
satisfies the conditions of Proposition
4.4.7
. Then
E_{\mathbf{a}}
has good reduction at
\mathfrak{p}
.

Proof.

As in Theorem
4.3.29
, we can use
S=\{h_{1},\dots,h_{d}\}
together with (
2.2.9
) to construct a model
E_{\mathbf{a}}:\mathbf{A}\to\Mat_{d}(K[\tau])
defined over
K
. Likewise, we use
\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5muS}=\{\overline{h}_{1},\dots,\overline{h}_{d}\}
and (
2.2.9
) to construct a
t
-module
E_{\mathbf{a},\mathfrak{p}}:\mathbf{A}\to\Mat_{d}(\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}[\tau])
. Using the
\mathbf{A}
-stability of
H_{\mathbf{a}}(B_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\mathrm{perf}})
and
H_{\mathbf{a},\mathfrak{p}}(\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}})
, we have a commutative diagram via (
2.2.9
),

(4.4.9)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Mat_{1\times d}(B_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\mathrm{perf}}[\sigma])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\mathbf{a}}(B_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\mathrm{perf}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\mathbf{a},\mathfrak{p}}(\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Mat_{1\times d}(\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}[\sigma])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Mat_{1\times d}(B_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\mathrm{perf}}[\sigma])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\mathbf{a}}(B_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\mathrm{perf}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\mathbf{a},\mathfrak{p}}(\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Mat_{1\times d}(\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}[\sigma]).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\cdot)E_{\mathbf{a},t}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t(\cdot)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t(\cdot)}
